Non-arithmetic hyperbolic orbifolds attached to unitary Shimura varieties Date: August 8, 2026. Mathematics Subject Classification: 20F65, 22E40, 53C35.
Olivier de Gaay Fortman

Abstract
Abstract: We develop a new method of constructing non-arithmetic lattices in the projective orthogonal group PO ​ ( n , 1 ) \text{PO}(n,1) for every integer n n larger than one. The technique is to consider anti-holomorphic involutions on a complex arithmetic ball quotient, glue their fixed loci along geodesic subspaces, and show that the resulting metric space carries canonically the structure of a complete real hyperbolic orbifold. The volume of various of these non-arithmetic orbifolds can be explicitly calculated.



1

Introduction

To study locally symmetric spaces
M=\Gamma\backslash G/K
of finite volume, one would like to classify lattices in semi-simple Lie groups. This difficult problem can be simplified somewhat
via the notion of arithmeticity. Namely, arithmetic lattices can be classified in a way which is close to the classification of semi-simple algebraic groups over number fields, something which is well-understood. Moreover, if a semi-simple Lie group
G
is non-compact and not isogenous to
{\textnormal{PO}}(n,1)
or
{\textnormal{PU}}(n,1)
, then every irreducible lattice
\Gamma\subset G
is arithmetic
[
Mar75
,
Cor92
,
GS92
]
. Thus, to describe all lattices, one is left with the problem of classifying non-arithmetic lattices in
{\textnormal{PO}}(n,1)
and in
{\textnormal{PU}}(n,1)
, a complicated task which remains completely open in general.

In this paper, we focus on
{\mathrm{PO}}(n,1)
. Our goal is twofold. Firstly, we provide a new construction of lattices in the Lie group
{\mathrm{PO}}(n,1)
for arbitrary
n\geq 2
. Secondly, we show that for an explicit sequence of lattices
\left\{\Gamma_{n}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)\right\}_{n\geq 2}
constructed in this way, the lattice
\Gamma_{n}
is non-arithmetic for each
n\geq 2
.

Our construction can be sketched as follows. We consider complex hyperbolic space
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
for some
n\geq 2
, together with an arithmetic lattice
L\subset{\mathrm{PU}}(n,1)
and a set of anti-isometric involutions
\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}H^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}
that are chosen in a suitable way. Since the fixed locus
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\coloneqq(\mathbb{C}H^{n})^{\alpha}
is isomorphic to real hyperbolic
n
-space
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
, one obtains a real hyperbolic orbifold quotient space
L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
for each of our chosen involutions
\alpha
, where
L_{\alpha}\coloneqq{\mathrm{Stab}}_{L}(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha})
is the stabilizer of
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
in the group
L
. We glue the spaces
L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
for varying
\alpha
together along suitable geodesic subspaces to obtain a metric space
M
, which we then provide with a real hyperbolic orbifold structure. The latter turns out to be complete, so that each connected component
M_{i}\subset M
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
modulo a certain lattice
\Gamma_{i}\subset{\mathrm{PO}}(n,1)
. For a precise statement, see Theorem
1.2
below. The conjugacy classes of the lattices
\Gamma_{i}
form the output of our construction.

Subsequently, we show that for some choices made in the above construction, there exists a connected component
M_{i}\subset M
such that any lattice
\Gamma_{i}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
with
M_{i}\cong\Gamma_{i}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}
is non-arithmetic; see Theorem
1.6
below. Moreover, the volume of
M_{i}\cong\Gamma_{i}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}
can be explicitly calculated in some cases, see Section
1.3
.

Philosophically, this construction seems to ressemble the construction of Gromov–Piatetski-Shapiro
[
GPS87
]
, in the sense that we glue hyperbolic orbifolds along geodesic subspaces. In technical detail, however, the two constructions are different (see Section
1.2
). Therefore, we ask in Question
1.7
whether for each
n\geq 2
, the conjugacy class of non-arithmetic lattices
\Gamma\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
we obtain via Theorem
1.6
is commensurable with any of the Gromov–Piatetski-Shapiro conjugacy classes.

Our results also have applications to the theory of real moduli spaces. Let
{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
be the moduli space of stable real cubic surfaces. For one of the non-arithmetic lattices
\Gamma\subset\textnormal{PO}(4,1)
we construct in the above way, we provide a homeomorphism
{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\cong\Gamma\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{4}
restricting to an isomorphism of orbifolds on the locus of smooth cubics. Recall that Allcock, Carlson and Toledo have provided
{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
with a non-arithmetic complete hyperbolic orbifold structure in their work
[
ACT10
]
. The hyperbolic orbifold structure we define on
{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
via the above homeomorphism
{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\cong\Gamma\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{4}
agrees with the one in
loc. cit.
(cf. Theorem
1.11
).

1.1

Construction of the lattices

Let us explain our first main result in more detail. For any number field
K
, we let
\mathcal{O}_{K}
denote its ring of integers. Recall that a number field
K
is called a CM field when
K
is an imaginary quadratic extension
K\supset F
of a number
F
which is totally real, meaning that the image of every embedding
F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is contained in
\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}
. Significant examples of CM fields are imaginary quadratic field extensions of
\mathbb{Q}
and cyclotomic number fields.

Central in this paper is the notion of
admissible hermitian lattice
. By this, we mean a pair

{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)

where
K
is a CM field whose different ideal is generated by a totally imaginary element, and where, for some
n\geq 1
,
\Lambda
is a finite free
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module equipped with a non-degenerate hermitian form
h\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{K}
of signature
(n,1)
with respect to one embedding
\tau\colon K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and of signature
(n+1,0)
with respect to the embeddings
\varphi\colon K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
non-conjugate to
\tau
(for an explanation of these notions, see Definition
2.2
in Section
2
). The
rank
of
{\mathscr{L}}
is the rank of
\Lambda
as finite free
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1
. Define

V\coloneqq\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K},\tau}\mathbb{C}.

Then
V
is a complex vector space of dimension
n+1
equipped with a hermitian form
h\colon V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
of signature
(n,1)
. Let
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
denote the complex hyperbolic space of lines
\ell\subset V
which are negative with respect to the hermitian form
h
.

Let
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
be the group of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear automorphisms
\phi\colon\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda
compatible with the hermitian form
h
. Let
\mu_{K}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}
be the torsion subgroup of
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}
. Since
\mu_{K}
consists of roots of unity, it acts on
\Lambda
by multiplication. We define

L\coloneqq\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)/\mu_{K}\subset{\mathrm{Isom}}(\mathbb{C}H^{n}).

Let
{\mathscr{R}}
be the set of elements
r\in\Lambda
with
h(r,r)=1
, and for
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
, we let
H_{r}\coloneqq\left\{\ell\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}\colon h(r,\ell)=0\right\}
. Define
{\mathscr{H}}\coloneqq\cup_{r\in{\mathscr{R}}}H_{r}
. The hyperplane arrangement
{\mathscr{H}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
turns out to be an
orthogonal arrangement
, in the sense of
[
ACT02
]
, see Theorem
2.5
below. In other words, if two different hyperplanes
H_{r}\neq H_{t}
for
r,t\in{\mathscr{R}}
meet, then they are orthogonal along their intersection.

By Dirichlet’s unit theorem,
\mu_{K}
is a finite cyclic group; let
\zeta\in\mu_{K}
be a generator. For
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
, define an isometry
\phi_{r}\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
as follows:

\phi_{r}(x)=x-(1-\zeta)h(x,r)\cdot r,\quad\quad x\in\Lambda.

We call
\phi_{r}
a
reflection in the hyperplane
H_{r}
.
For
x\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, let

G(x)\coloneqq\langle\phi_{r}\mid x\in H_{r}\rangle\subset L

be the subgroup generated by reflections
\phi_{r}
in the hyperplanes
H_{r}
with
x\in H_{r}
.

Let

\sigma\colon K\rightarrow K

be the involution whose fixed locus is the maximal totally real field
F\subset K
. An
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-linear bijection
\alpha\colon\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda
is called
anti-unitary
if
\alpha(\lambda x)=\sigma(\lambda)\alpha(x)
and
h(\alpha(x),\alpha(y))=\sigma(h(x,y))
for each
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
x,y\in\Lambda
.
We let
{\mathscr{A}}
be the set of anti-unitary involutions, and let
P{\mathscr{A}}=\mu_{K}\setminus{\mathscr{A}}
be the quotient of
{\mathscr{A}}
by the action of
\mu_{K}
on
{\mathscr{A}}
by multiplication. For each
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\coloneqq\left(\mathbb{C}H^{n}\right)^{\alpha}\quad\text{ and }\quad L_{\alpha}\coloneqq{\mathrm{Stab}}_{L}(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}).

(1)

Then
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
is isometric to real hyperbolic
n
-space
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
(see Lemma
3.4
), and we prove that the natural embedding
L_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow{\mathrm{Isom}}(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha})\cong\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
identifies
L_{\alpha}
with an arithmetic lattice in
\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
(see Theorem
3.6
).

The following definition is the starting point of our gluing construction.

Definition 1.1
.

Define a relation
\sim
on the disjoint union
\sqcup_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
in the following way. Let
(x,\alpha),(y,\beta)\in\sqcup_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
, where
x\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\beta}
, for
\alpha,\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}
. Then
(x,\alpha)\sim(y,\beta)
if and only if
x=y\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
and
\beta\circ\alpha\in G(x)
.

Then
\sim
is an equivalence relation, see Lemma
4.3
. Moreover, by Lemma
4.4
, the action of
L
on
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
induces an action of
L
on
\sqcup_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
compatible with
\sim
. Therefore,
L
acts naturally on the quotient space
\left(\sqcup_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\right)/_{\sim}
.
We define

\displaystyle M({\mathscr{L}})\coloneqq L\setminus\left(\left(\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\right)/_{\sim}\right).

(2)

Let
C{\mathscr{A}}\subset P{\mathscr{A}}
be a set of representatives for the quotient
L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}
, where, as above,
P{\mathscr{A}}
is the set of
\mu_{K}
-equivalence classes of anti-unitary involutions
\alpha\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
and where
L=\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)/\mu_{K}
acts on
P{\mathscr{A}}
by conjugation.
Recall that a
real hyperbolic orbifold
is a second countable Hausdorff space locally modeled on
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
modulo finite isometry groups, see e.g.
[
Thu80
, Chapter 13]
,
[
ALR07
, Chapter 1]
or
[
Lan20
]
.

The following theorem is the first main result of this paper.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1\geq 2
.
Define the topological space
M({\mathscr{L}})
as in equation (
2
). The following assertions are true.

1.

There is a canonical real hyperbolic orbifold structure on the topological space
M({\mathscr{L}})
together with a natural open immersion of hyperbolic orbifolds

\displaystyle\coprod_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})

(3)

whose complement has measure zero.

2.

For each connected component
M_{i}
of the orbifold
M({\mathscr{L}})
, there exists a lattice
\Gamma_{i}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
and an isomorphism of real hyperbolic orbifolds

M_{i}\cong\Gamma_{i}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}.

3.

The cardinality of the set
C{\mathscr{A}}\cong L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}
and the volume
{\mathrm{vol}}(M({\mathscr{L}}))
of the hyperbolic orbifold
M({\mathscr{L}})
are both finite. Furthermore, we have:

{\mathrm{vol}}(M({\mathscr{L}}))={\mathrm{vol}}\left(\coprod_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\right)=\sum_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}{\mathrm{vol}}\left(L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\right).

Example 1.3
.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1\geq 2
. For an anti-unitary involution
\alpha\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
, let
M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)\subset M({\mathscr{L}})
be the connected component containing the image of the natural map
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})
. If we have
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathscr{H}}=\emptyset
, then
M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)\cong L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
.

1.2

Arithmetic nature of the lattices

It is natural to ask whether which of the lattices one produces in the above way, are arithmetic. Let us make this question more precise.
As a corollary of Theorem
1.2
, for each
n\geq 2
one gets an assignment

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\{({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)\text{ with }{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)\text{ an admissible \phantom{nnn}}\right.\\
\left.\text{hermitian lattice of rank $n+1$ and $\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}$}\right\}\end{split}\;\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\;\begin{split}\left\{\text{conjugacy classes of \phantom{n}}\right.\\
\left.\text{lattices }\Gamma\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)\right\}\end{split}

(4)

defined by sending
({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)
to the conjugacy class of any lattice

\Gamma({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)

that satisfies
M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)\cong\Gamma({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}
, where
M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)
is the connected component of
M({\mathscr{L}})
that contains the image of the natural map
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})
. We would like to understand which conjugacy classes in the image of (
4
) are arithmetic.

Example 1.4
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer, and let
{\mathscr{L}}
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1
. If
\alpha\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
is an anti-unitary involution such that
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathscr{H}}=\emptyset
, then
[\Gamma({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)]=[L_{\alpha}]
as conjugacy classes of lattices in
\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
,
see Example
1.3
. Moreover, the lattice
L_{\alpha}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
is arithmetic as we prove in Theorem
3.6
.

It seems natural to ask whether this is the only obstruction to non-arithmeticity.

Question 1.5
.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer. Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1
, and
\alpha\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
an anti-unitary involution such that
{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathscr{H}}\neq\emptyset
. Is it true that the conjugacy class of lattices
\Gamma({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
associated to
({\mathscr{L}},\alpha)
by the assignment (
4
) is non-arithmetic?

For each
n\geq 2
, we can answer this question positively in one particular case, yielding the second main result of this paper. Let
\zeta_{3}\coloneqq e^{2\pi i/3}\in\mathbb{C}
and consider the admissible hermitian lattice

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n}\coloneqq\left(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3}),\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}\right),

(5)

where

\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}
is the free
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]
-module of rank
n+1
equipped with the hermitian form
h={\mathrm{diag}}(-1,1,\dotsc,1)
defined as
h(x,y)=-x_{0}\bar{y}_{0}+x_{1}\bar{y}_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{y}_{n}
. We call
{\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n}
the
Eisenstein
hermitian lattice of rank
n+1
. Define an anti-unitary involution
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}
by
\alpha_{0}(x)=\bar{x}
, let
M({\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n},\alpha_{0})\subset M({\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n})
be the connected component containing the image of the map
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{0}}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n})
, and let

\displaystyle\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)

(6)

be a lattice such that
M({\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n},\alpha_{0})\cong\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}
, see Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem 1.6
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
,
the lattice
\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
is non-arithmetic.

We point out that for
n=2,3,4
, the lattice
\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
is not cocompact. See Remark
1.12
below.

The way in which we obtain a non-arithmetic lattice in
\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
for
n\geq 2
seems to be an orbifold analogue of the construction of non-arithmetic lattices provided by Gromov and Piatetski–Shapiro
[
GPS87
]
. Apart from the analogy, their construction is technically different from ours. Gromov and Piatetski–Shapiro glue hyperbolic manifolds with boundary whose fundamental groups are Zariski dense in
{\mathrm{PO}}(n,1)
and lie in non-commensurable arithmetic lattices. Here, we glue real hyperbolic orbifolds with boundary and corners whose orbifold fundamental groups are generally not Zariski dense in
{\mathrm{PO}}(n,1)
(but do lie in arithmetic lattices that are generally not all commensurable). In light of these differences, it makes sense to ask whether the lattices we construct are essentially different.

Question 1.7
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
. Consider the non-arithmetic lattice
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
defined in (
6
) above (cf. Theorem
1.6
).
Is the lattice
\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
commensurable with any of the Gromov–Piatetski-Shapiro lattices (cf.
[
GPS87
]
)? Is
\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
commensurable with any other non-arithmetic lattice in
\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
currently known to exist, such as one of those constructed by Belolipetsky and Thomson
[
BT11
]
?

Next, we apply Theorem
1.2
to another sequence of admissible hermitian lattices
{\mathscr{L}}
, defined in the following way. Let
p>3
be a prime number, let
\zeta_{p}=e^{2\pi i/p}\in\mathbb{C}
, and assume that the class number of
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
is odd. Let
\lambda
be a unit in the ring of integers of
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p}+\zeta_{p}^{-1})
such that
\lambda
is positive for one embedding
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p}+\zeta_{p}^{-1})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
and negative for all other embeddings
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p}+\zeta_{p}^{-1})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, and such that
\lambda\not\equiv-1\bmod(1-\zeta_{p})
(such an element exists, see Lemma
7.1
in Section
7
). For
n\geq 2
, put

{\mathscr{L}}_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda)\coloneqq(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p}),\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]^{n,1}_{\lambda}).

Here,
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]^{n,1}_{\lambda}
is the free
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]
-module of rank
n+1
equipped with the hermitian form
h
defined as

h(x,y)=-\lambda\cdot x_{0}\bar{y}_{0}+x_{1}\bar{y}_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{y}_{n}
. It is readily verified that
{\mathscr{L}}_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda)
is an admissible hermitian lattice. Define an anti-unitary involution
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]^{n,1}_{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]^{n,1}_{\lambda}
as
\alpha_{0}(x)=\bar{x}
, let
M({\mathscr{L}}_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda),\alpha_{0})\subset M({\mathscr{L}}_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda))
be the connected component containing the image of the natural map
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{0}}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}}_{\zeta_{5}}^{n}(\lambda))
, and let

\displaystyle\Gamma^{n}_{\zeta_{p}}(\lambda)\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)

(7)

be a lattice such that
M({\mathscr{L}}_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda),\alpha_{0})\cong\Gamma^{n}_{\zeta_{5}}(\lambda)\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}
, see Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
p>3
and
\lambda
be as above.
For
n\geq 2
, let
\Gamma^{n}_{\zeta_{p}}(\lambda)\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
be the lattice defined in equation (
7
) above. Assume that each anti-unitary involution of
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]^{2,1}_{\lambda}
is
\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]^{2,1}_{\lambda})
-conjugate to an anti-unitary involution of the form
(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\epsilon_{0}\bar{x}_{0},\epsilon_{1}\bar{x}_{1},\epsilon_{2}\bar{x}_{2})
with
\epsilon_{i}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}
. Assume moreover that
\Gamma^{2}_{\zeta_{p}}(\lambda)\subset\textnormal{PO}(2,1)
is non-arithmetic. Then
\Gamma^{n}_{\zeta_{p}}(\lambda)\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
is non-arithmetic for all
n\geq 2
.

We prove an analogous result for admissible hermitian lattices of the form
{\mathscr{L}}=(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-p}),(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-p})})^{n,1})
, where
p\geq 3
is an arbitrary prime number, see Theorem
8.1
in Section
8
. For
p=3
, this reduction to the case
n=2
in the proof of the non-arithmeticity of
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{eis}}
is fact what allows us to prove Theorem
1.6
in Section
9.3
.

In a follow-up paper, see
[
GF23
]
, we use the theory developed here to investigate the structure of the moduli space of stable real binary quintics, thereby extending results of Allcock, Carlson and Toledo (see Section
1.4
below).
We will identify this moduli space with the space
M({\mathscr{L}}_{\zeta_{5}}^{2}(\lambda))
, where
\lambda=\zeta_{5}+\zeta_{5}^{-1}
, and use this description to prove that the conditions in Theorem
1.8
are verified in this case. In particular, by Theorem
1.8
,
this will prove non-arithmeticity of
\Gamma^{n}_{\zeta_{5}}(\lambda)\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
for all
n\geq 2
. We will also show that the lattice
\Gamma^{2}_{\zeta_{5}}(\lambda)\subset\textnormal{PO}(2,1)
is cocompact.

1.3

Volume

We return our attention to the Eisenstein hermitian lattices
{\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n}=(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3}),\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1})
for
n\geq 2
. Let
{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
be the set of anti-unitary involutions of
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}
, let
C{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
be a set of representatives for the action of
L^{n}\coloneqq\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1})/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
on
P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}={\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
, and define
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
and
L_{\alpha}^{n}\subset{\mathrm{Isom}}(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha})
as in equation (
1
).
For
i=0,\dotsc,n
, consider the integral quadratic form
\Psi_{i}^{n}
defined as

\Psi_{i}^{n}(x_{0},\dotsc,x_{n})=-x_{0}^{2}+3x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+3x_{i}^{2}+x_{i+1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}.

We use Theorem
1.2
to prove the following result.

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
. Let
\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
be the non-arithmetic lattice defined in equation (
6
) above (cf. Theorem
1.6
). Then there are a union of geodesic subspaces
{\mathscr{H}}_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n}
for
i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}
and open immersions

\coprod_{i=0}^{n}{\mathrm{PO}}(\Psi_{i}^{n},\mathbb{Z})\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}-{\mathscr{H}}_{i}\right)\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n}).

In particular,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\mathrm{Vol}}\left({\mathrm{PO}}(\Psi_{i}^{n},\mathbb{Z})\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}\right)\leq

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Vol}}\left(\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}\right),

(8)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Vol}}\left(\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}\right)\leq\sum_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}{\mathrm{Vol}}(L^{n}_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}).

(9)

We note that there is a canonical bijection between
L^{n}\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
and the non-abelian cohomology group
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,L^{n})
, where
G=\mathbb{Z}/2
acts on the group
L^{n}=\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1})/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
via conjugation by the involution
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}
defined as
\alpha_{0}(x)=\bar{x}
, see Proposition
3.7
.
Moreover, by work of Prasad
[
Pra89
]
, there is a closed formula for the volume of the arithmetic orbifold
{\mathrm{PO}}(\Psi_{i}^{n},\mathbb{Z})\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}
; see also
[
Bel04
,
BE12
]
. For instance, consider the quadratic form
\Psi_{0}^{n}(x_{0},\dotsc,x_{n})=-x_{0}^{2}+x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}
for
n=2r\geq 2
even; by
[
RT97
, Theorem 6]
, we have:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Vol}}\left({\mathrm{PO}}(\Psi_{0}^{2r},\mathbb{Z})\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{2r}\right)=\left(2^{r}+\varepsilon_{r}\right)\cdot\frac{\pi^{r}}{(2r)!}\prod_{k=1}^{r}\left|B_{2k}\right|

with the
B_{2k}
Bernoulli numbers, and with
\varepsilon_{r}=1
if
r\equiv 0,1\bmod 4
and
\varepsilon_{r}=-1
if
r\equiv 2,3\bmod 4
.

We expect that (
8
) is an equality whenever
n
is even:

Conjecture 1.10
.

Let
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Then (
8
) is an equality; in other words, the volume of the non-arithmetic hyperbolic orbifold
M({\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n},\alpha_{0})\cong\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}
equals

{\mathrm{Vol}}\left(\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}{\mathrm{Vol}}\left({\mathrm{PO}}(\Psi_{i}^{n},\mathbb{Z})\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}\right).

Work of Allcock, Carlson and Toledo
[
ACT06
,
ACT10
]
implies Conjecture
1.10
for
n\in\left\{2,4\right\}
. In fact, if the cardinality of
L^{n}\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}={\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,L^{n})
equals
n+1
, then (
8
) and (
9
) are both equalities, thus Conjecture
1.10
holds for such
n
. This happens for
n=4
by
[
ACT10
, Theorem 4.1]
, and for
n=2
by Theorem
9.5
in Section
9.3
. We do not know if the equality
\#{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,L^{n})=n+1

is a coincidence for
n\in\left\{2,4\right\}
, or holds for all
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.

For
n=3
, one has
\#{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,L^{n})=5=n+2
(cf.
[
ACT07
, Remark 6]
), which implies that (
8
) or (
9
) must be a strict inequality in this case.

Conjecture
1.10
would provide a way to explicitly calculate the volume of the non-arithmetic quotients
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}
for
n\geq 2
even. To the best of our knowledge, there is not much known about explicit volume computations in the other known families of non-arithmetic quotients in arbitrary dimension. For a lattice that comes from an interbreeding construction
[
GPS87
]
or generalizations (c.f.
[
Rai13
,
GL14
]
), its covolume is a rational linear combination of the volumes of the arithmetic quotients that are glued, see
[
Eme17
, Corollary 1.5]
or
[
EM21
, Theorem 1.12]
. Note, however, that the rational coefficients are not provided, and that in general the arithmetic pieces used for the interbreeding construction are not explicated.

1.4

Uniformization of real moduli spaces

Theorems
1.2
and
1.6
also have applications to the study of moduli spaces of real algebraic varieties. Write
{\mathscr{C}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
for the space of non-zero
cubic forms with real coefficients in four variables which are stable in the sense of geometric invariant theory, and let
{\mathscr{C}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathscr{C}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
be the open subspace of cubic forms whose associated cubic surface is smooth. The orbifold quotients

{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}=\textnormal{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\mathscr{C}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\supset\textnormal{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\mathscr{C}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}={\mathscr{M}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}

are the respective moduli spaces of stable and smooth real cubic surfaces. By
[
ACT10
, Theorem 1.2]
, there are a non-arithmetic lattice
P\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(4,1)
, a homeomorphism

\displaystyle{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\cong P\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{4},

(10)

and a
P\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}
-invariant union
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{4}
of two- and three-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{R}H^{4}
such that (
10
) restricts to an orbifold isomorphism

{\mathscr{M}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}\cong P\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{4}-{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}\right)
.

Our theory in dimension
n=4
relates to this result as follows. Consider the open immersion

\coprod_{i=0}^{4}{\mathrm{PO}}(\Psi_{i}^{4},\mathbb{Z})\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{4}-{\mathscr{H}}_{i}\right)\hookrightarrow\Gamma^{4}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{4},

cf. Theorem
1.9
.

Theorem 1.11
.

Let
{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\supset{\mathscr{M}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
be the moduli spaces of stable and smooth real cubic surfaces. Then the following assertions are true.

1.

There are a union of geodesic subspaces
{\mathscr{H}}_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{4}
for
i=0,\dotsc,4
and a homeomorphism

\displaystyle{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\cong\Gamma_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{4}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{4}

(11)

restricting to an orbifold isomorphism
{\mathscr{M}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}\cong\coprod_{i=0}^{4}{\mathrm{PO}}(\Psi_{i}^{4},\mathbb{Z})\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{4}-{\mathscr{H}}_{i}\right)
.

2.

When
{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
is given the orbifold structure induced by (
10
), then (
11
) is an isomorphism of orbifolds. In particular, the lattices
P\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{4}
are conjugate.

Our results have similar connections with
[
ACT06
, Section 5]
. Namely, if
{\mathscr{N}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}(\infty)
is the moduli space of stable real binary sextics that have a double root at
\infty
, then
{\mathscr{N}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}(\infty)\cong\Gamma_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{2}
and the lattice
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eis}}^{2}\subset\textnormal{PO}(2,1)
is conjugate to lattice
\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty}
defined in
[
ACT06
, Section 5]
.
We will prove this in Theorem
9.4
in Section
9.2
.

Remark 1.12
.

By Theorems
1.11
and
9.4
, the lattice
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
is not cocompact for
n=2,4
, because the moduli spaces
{\mathscr{N}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}(\infty)
and
{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
are not compact.

1.5

Orthogonality of the hyperplane arrangement

Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1\geq 3
. Define

{\mathscr{H}}=\cup_{r\in{\mathscr{R}}}H_{r}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}

as in Section
1.1
. A crucial ingredient in the proof of Theorem
1.2
is the fact that the hyperplane arrangement
{\mathscr{H}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is an orthogonal arrangement in the sense of
[
ACT02
]
. In other words, for
r,t\in\Lambda
such that
h(r,r)=h(t,t)=1
,
H_{r}\neq H_{t}
and
H_{r}\cap H_{t}\neq\emptyset
, one has
h(r,t)=0
. See Theorem
2.5
in Section
12
.

In view of
[
ACT02
, Theorem 1.2]
, the following is a corollary of Theorem
2.5
.

Theorem 1.13
.

Let
n\geq 2
.
Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1
. Define the lattice
L\subset{\mathrm{Isom}}(\mathbb{C}H^{n})
and the hyperplane arrangement
{\mathscr{H}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
as in Section
1.1
.
Then the orbifold fundamental group
\pi_{1}^{\textnormal{orb}}\left(L\setminus\left(\mathbb{C}H^{n}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)\right)
is not a lattice in any Lie group with finitely many connected components.

2

Admissible hermitian lattices

The goal of this section is to introduce and study admissible hermitian lattices, who form the input of our gluing construction.

2.1

Admissible hermitian lattices

Notation 2.1
.

Throughout this paper,
K
will denote a CM field with maximal totally real subfield
F\subset K
. We let
\sigma
denote the unique involution
\sigma\colon K\rightarrow K
such that for each embedding
\varphi\colon K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, one has
\varphi\circ\sigma={\mathrm{conj}}\circ\varphi
, where
{\mathrm{conj}}\colon\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
denotes complex conjugation. We have
F=K^{\sigma}
as subfields of
K
, and there is an element
a\in K
with
K=F(a)
,
a^{2}\in F
and
\sigma(a)=-a
. We let
\mathcal{O}_{K}\subset K
and
\mathcal{O}_{F}\subset F
denote the respective rings of integers.

Let
K
be a CM field and let
F\subset K
and
\sigma\colon K\rightarrow K
as in Notation
2.1
. Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
\Lambda
a free
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module of rank
n+1
. Let

h\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{K}

be a
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinear form which is
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear in its first argument and which satisfies
h(y,x)=\sigma(h(x,y))
for each
x,y\in\Lambda
. Note that for
\xi\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we have
h(x,\xi y)=\sigma(\xi)h(x,y)
. In particular,
h
is
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-bilinear. For an embedding
\varphi\colon F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, define

\displaystyle V_{\varphi}\coloneqq\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F},\varphi}\mathbb{R}.

Then
V_{\varphi}
is naturally a complex vector space of dimension
n+1
, equipped with a hermitian form

h_{\varphi}\colon V_{\varphi}\times V_{\varphi}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

induced by
h
. Central in this paper is:

Definition 2.2
.

A
hermitian lattice
is a pair

{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)

where
K
is a CM field and where
\Lambda
is a free
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module of finite rank equipped with a non-degenerate
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-bilinear form
h\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{K}
which is
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear in its first argument and which satisfies
h(y,x)=\sigma(h(x,y))
for each
x,y\in\Lambda
. We say that a hermitian lattice
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
is
admissible
if the following conditions hold:

1.

The different ideal
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}
(see e.g.
[
Neu99
, Chapter III, §2]
) is generated by an element
\eta\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
that satisfies
\sigma(\eta)=-\eta
.

2.

For one embedding
\tau\colon F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the hermitian form
h_{\tau}
on the complex vector space
V_{\tau}=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F},\tau}\mathbb{R}
has signature
(n,1)
. For all other embeddings
\varphi\neq\tau
of
F
into
\mathbb{R}
, the hermitian form
h_{\varphi}\colon V_{\varphi}\times V_{\varphi}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
has signature
(n+1,0)
.

The
rank
of
{\mathscr{L}}
is the rank of
\Lambda
as finite free
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module.

We will provide some examples of admissible hermitian lattices in Section
2.4
and in Section
7
.

2.2

Unitary ball quotients

Let
n
be a positive integer. Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1\geq 2
. Let
F\subset K
and
\sigma\colon K\rightarrow K
be as in Notation
2.1
.
For an element
x\in K
, we will sometimes use the notation
\overline{x}=\sigma(x)
. Let
\tau\colon F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the embedding such that the hermitian form
h_{\tau}
induced by
h
has signature
(n,1)
, see Section
2.1
. Define

V\coloneqq V_{\tau}=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F},\tau}\mathbb{R}.

To simplify notation, we put
h=h_{\tau}
. Thus,

h\colon V\times V\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

denotes the hermitian form of signature
(n,1)
induced by the form
h\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

Let
m
be the largest positive integer for which the
m
-th cyclotomic field
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{m})
can be embedded in
K
, where
\zeta_{m}=e^{2\pi i/m}\in\mathbb{C}
. Let
\zeta\in K
be a primitive
m
-th root of unity in
K
, and define

\mu_{K}=\langle\zeta\rangle\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{\ast}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}.

By the Dirichlet unit theorem,
\mu_{K}
coincides with the torsion subgroup of
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}
.

Define
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
as the unitary group of
\Lambda
(i.e., the group of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear isomorphisms
\phi\colon\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda
that satisfy
h(\phi(x),\phi(y))
for each
x,y\in\Lambda
) and let
L
be the quotient of
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
by
\mu_{K}
; thus, we have

\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)=U(\Lambda)(\mathcal{O}_{K})\quad{\textnormal{ and }}\quad L=\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)/\mu_{K}.

An element
r\in\Lambda
with
h(r,r)=1
is called a
short root
. Let
{\mathscr{R}}\subset\Lambda
be the set of short roots. For
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
, define isometries
\phi_{r}^{i}\colon V\rightarrow V
as follows:

\phi_{r}(x)=x-(1-\zeta)h(x,r)\cdot r,\quad\phi_{r}^{i}(x)=x-(1-\zeta^{i})h(x,r)\cdot r,\quad i\in(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{\ast}.

Note that
\phi^{i}_{r}\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
for
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
, and that
\phi_{r}^{i}=\phi_{r}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{r}
(
i
times). In particular,
\phi_{r}^{m}=\textnormal{id}
. Let
\mathbb{P}(V)
be the space of complex lines in
V
, and let

\mathbb{C}H^{n}\coloneqq\left\{\ell=[v]\in\mathbb{P}(V)\mid h(v,v)<0\right\}\subset\mathbb{P}(V)

be the space of negative lines in
V
. Define

H_{r}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}:h(x,r)=0\}\;{\textnormal{ for }}\;r\in{\mathscr{R}},\quad{\textnormal{ and }}\quad{\mathscr{H}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{r\in{\mathscr{R}}}H_{r}\;\;\;\subset\;\;\;\mathbb{C}H^{n}.

Here, for a point
x\in{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
, the condition
h(x,r)=0
in fact means that
h(x^{\prime},r_{\tau})=0
for the image
r_{\tau}\in V_{\tau}=V
of
r
under the canonical embedding
\Lambda\hookrightarrow\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F},\tau}\mathbb{R}=V_{\tau}=V
and any non-zero representative
x^{\prime}\in V
of
x\in{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The family of hyperplanes
(H_{r})_{r\in{\mathscr{R}}}
is locally finite. In particular, the hyperplane arrangement
{\mathscr{H}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is a closed subset of
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
.

Proof.

See
[
Bea09
, Lemma 5.3]
for the proof in case
{\mathscr{L}}=(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3}),\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{4,1})
. The general case, is similar; we will prove it for convenience of the reader. Let us first fix some notation. Let
\mathbb{C}^{n,1}=(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},h_{n,1})
be the complex vector space
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
equipped with the hermitian form
h_{n,1}
defined as
h_{n,1}(x,y)=-x_{0}\bar{y}_{0}+x_{1}\bar{y}_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{y}_{n}
. We define a function
h_{+}\colon\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
as
h_{+}(x,y)=x_{1}\bar{y}_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{y}_{n}
, so that
h_{n,1}(x,y)=h_{+}(x,y)-x_{0}\bar{y}_{0}
. We put
\left\|x\right\|=\sqrt{h_{+}(x,x)}
for
x\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
. If we identify
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the affine hyperplane
z_{0}=0
in
\mathbb{C}^{n,1}
, then
\left\|\phantom{-}\right\|
induces the standard hermitian norm on
\mathbb{C}^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n,1}
.

Next, we fix an isomorphism
\varphi\colon V\cong\mathbb{C}^{n,1}
and let
M\subset\mathbb{C}^{n,1}
be the image of
\Lambda
under
\varphi
. We define
\Delta_{1}\subset M
as
\Delta_{1}=\varphi({\mathscr{R}})
, so that
\Delta_{1}
is set of elements
\delta\in M
such that
h_{n,1}(\delta,\delta)=1
. Let
{\mathbb{H}}^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}
be the space of complex lines in
\mathbb{C}^{n,1}
which are negative for
h_{n,1}
. Then we have an isomorphism

\displaystyle\psi\colon{\mathbb{H}}^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}\xlongrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\colon\left\|z\right\|^{2}=\left|z_{1}\right|^{2}+\cdots+\left|z_{n}\right|^{2}<1\right\},

\displaystyle\psi\left([z_{0}\colon\cdots\colon z_{n}]\right)=(z_{1}/z_{0},\dotsc,z_{n}/z_{0}).

For
\delta\in M
, write
\delta=(\delta_{0},\dotsc,\delta_{n})
, and define

H_{\delta}\coloneqq\left\{(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\in\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid-\bar{\delta}_{0}+x_{1}\bar{\delta}_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{\delta}_{n}=0\right\}.

Under the composition of isomorphisms
\mathbb{C}H^{n}\cong\mathbb{H}^{n,1}_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
, the hyperplane
H_{r}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
for
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
is identified with the hyperplane
H_{\delta}\subset\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
for
\delta\in\Delta_{1}
. For
\delta\in\Delta_{1}
and
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
, let
{\widetilde{x}}=(1,x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n,1}
and define

\left|h(x,\delta)\right|\coloneqq\left|h_{n,1}({\widetilde{x}},\delta)\right|=\left|-\delta_{0}+x_{1}\bar{\delta}_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{\delta}_{n}\right|.

Observe that
H_{\delta}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\colon\left|h(x,\delta)\right|=0\right\}
.

We need to show that the family of hyperplanes
(H_{\delta})_{\delta\in\Delta_{1}}
is locally finite in
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
To prove this, let
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
. We want to show that for
\varepsilon
small enough, the ball
B(x,\varepsilon)
meets only finitely many of the hyperplanes
H_{\delta}
. Since
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
, we have that
\left\|x\right\|<1
. We choose
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
with
0<\varepsilon<1
so that
\left\|x\right\|<1-2\varepsilon
.

Claim 2.4
.

There exists
M\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that for each
\delta\in\Delta_{1}
with
H_{\delta}\cap B(x,\varepsilon)\neq\emptyset
, we have
\left\|\delta\right\|\leq M
.

Proof of Claim
2.4
.

Let
\delta\in\Delta_{1}
.
Since
\left\|\delta\right\|^{2}=\left|\delta_{1}\right|^{2}+\cdots+\left|\delta_{n}\right|^{2}
and
h_{n,1}(\delta,\delta)=-\left|\delta_{0}\right|^{2}+\left|\delta_{1}\right|^{2}+\cdots+\left|\delta_{n}\right|^{2}=1
, we get
\left\|\delta\right\|^{2}=1+\left|\delta_{0}\right|^{2}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\delta_{0}\right|}{\left\|\delta\right\|}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\left|\delta_{0}\right|^{-2}+1}}.

(12)

If
\left\|\delta\right\|
approaches infinity, then
\left|\delta_{0}\right|=\sqrt{\left\|\delta\right\|^{2}-1}
approaches infinity and hence
\frac{\left|\delta_{0}\right|}{\left\|\delta\right\|}
approaches
1
in view of (
12
). In particular, there exists
M>0
such that

1\geq\frac{\left|\delta_{0}\right|}{\left\|\delta\right\|}\geq 1-\varepsilon\quad\text{ for all }\quad\delta\in\Delta_{1}\quad\text{ such that }\quad\left\|\delta\right\|>M.

We fix this
M>0
.

Next, let
\delta\in\Delta_{1}
such that
H_{\delta}\cap B(x,\varepsilon)\neq\emptyset
. We claim that
\left\|\delta\right\|\leq M
.
To see this, let
x^{\prime}\in H_{\delta}\cap B(x,\varepsilon)
be a point in the intersection. Define
{\widetilde{x}}=(1,x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n,1}
and
{\widetilde{x}}^{\prime}=(1,x_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,x_{n}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{C}^{n,1}
. Then
h(x^{\prime},\delta)=h_{n,1}({\widetilde{x}}^{\prime},\delta)=0
and
\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\|<\varepsilon
. This gives:

\displaystyle\left|h(x,\delta)\right|=\left|h_{n,1}({\widetilde{x}}-{\widetilde{x}}^{\prime},\delta)\right|=\left|h_{+}({\widetilde{x}}-{\widetilde{x}}^{\prime},\delta)\right|\leq\left\|x-x^{\prime}\right\|\cdot\left\|\delta\right\|<\varepsilon\cdot\left\|\delta\right\|.

Moreover, we have
h(x,\delta)=h_{n,1}({\widetilde{x}},\delta)=h_{+}({\widetilde{x}},\delta)-\bar{\delta}_{0}
, and

\left|h_{+}({\widetilde{x}},\delta)\right|=\left|x_{1}\bar{\delta}_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{\delta}_{n}\right|\leq\left\|x\right\|\cdot\left\|\delta\right\|<(1-2\varepsilon)\cdot\left\|\delta\right\|.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that
\left\|\delta\right\|>M
. By our choice of
M
, we get
\left|\delta_{0}\right|\geq(1-\varepsilon)\left\|\delta\right\|
. Then

\left|h_{+}({\widetilde{x}},\delta)\right|\leq\left\|x\right\|\cdot\left\|\delta\right\|<(1-2\varepsilon)\cdot\left\|\delta\right\|\leq(1-2\varepsilon)\cdot\frac{\left|\delta_{0}\right|}{(1-\varepsilon)}<\left|\delta_{0}\right|.

Therefore,
\left|h_{+}({\widetilde{x}},\delta)-\bar{\delta}_{0}\right|\geq\left|\delta_{0}\right|-\left|h_{+}({\widetilde{x}},\delta)\right|
so that

\displaystyle\varepsilon\cdot\left\|\delta\right\|

\displaystyle>\left|h(x,\delta)\right|=\left|h_{+}({\widetilde{x}},\delta)-\bar{\delta}_{0}\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\delta_{0}\right|-\left|h_{+}({\widetilde{x}},\delta)\right|>(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\left\|\delta\right\|-(1-2\varepsilon)\cdot\left\|\delta\right\|=\varepsilon\cdot\left\|\delta\right\|.

This yields the desired contradiction. Thus
\left\|\delta\right\|\leq M
. This proves Claim
2.4
.
∎

By Claim
2.4
, for each
\delta\in\Delta_{1}
such that
H_{\delta}\cap B(x,\varepsilon)\neq\emptyset
, we have
\left\|\delta\right\|\leq M
. For such
\delta
, the equality
\left\|\delta\right\|^{2}=1+\left|\delta_{0}\right|^{2}
then implies

\left|\delta_{0}\right|\leq\left\|\delta\right\|=\left|\delta_{1}\right|^{2}+\cdots+\left|\delta_{n}\right|^{2}\leq M.

Thus, the set of
\delta\in\Delta_{1}
such that
H_{\delta}\cap B(x,\varepsilon)\neq\emptyset
is contained in the compact subset
K\coloneqq\left\{(z_{0},z_{1},\dotsc,z_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\mid\left|z_{0}\right|^{2}\leq\left|z_{1}\right|^{2}+\cdots+\left|z_{n}\right|^{2}\leq M\right\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
.
As the set of
\delta\in\Delta_{1}
such that
H_{\delta}\cap B(x,\varepsilon)\neq\emptyset
is discrete, it is therefore finite.
∎

2.3

Orthogonal hyperplanes and complex reflections

Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1\geq 2
. We continue with the notation of Sections
2.1
and
2.2
.

The following result is a key technical ingredient in our gluing construction.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1\geq 2
.
Let
r,t\in\Lambda
with
h(r,r)=h(t,t)=1
,
H_{r}\neq H_{t}
and
H_{r}\cap H_{t}\neq\emptyset
. Then
h(r,t)=0
.

The proof of Theorem
2.5
shall be provided in Section
12
. It depends on the results of Section
11
, that describe the structure of the quotient
L\setminus\mathbb{C}H^{n}
as a certain moduli space of abelian varieties. We highlight that Sections
11
and
12
are independent of Sections
2
–
9
.

Theorem
2.5
implies that if
H_{r_{1}},\dotsc,H_{r_{k}}
for
r_{i}\in{\mathscr{R}}
are mutually distinct, and if their common intersection is non-empty, then
\cap_{i=1}^{k}H_{r_{i}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is a totally geodesic subspace of codimension
k
. Moreover, for any
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
, the element
\phi_{r}\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
generates a finite subgroup
\langle\phi_{r}\rangle\subset\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
of order
m
, and that the restriction of the quotient map
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\rightarrow L
to this subgroup
\langle\phi_{r}\rangle\subset\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
is injective.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\phi\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
. Assume
\phi\colon\mathbb{C}H^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}
has order
m
and restricts to the identity on
H_{r}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
for some
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
. Then
\phi=a\cdot\phi_{r}^{i}
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}/m
and
a\in\mu_{K}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}
be the hyperbolic space attached to the standard hermitian space
\mathbb{C}^{n,1}
of dimension
n+1
. It is classical that

\displaystyle\text{Stab}_{U(n,1)}(\mathbb{H}^{n-1}_{\mathbb{C}})=U(n-1,1)\times U(1),

(13)

where
U(1)=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}\colon\left|z\right|^{2}=1\}
.
Define
W\coloneqq\langle r\rangle^{\perp}\subset V
, so that
V=W\oplus\langle r\rangle
. For
\xi\in U(1)
, define
\phi_{r}^{\xi}\in\textnormal{Isom}(V)
as
\phi_{r}^{\xi}(x)=x-(1-\xi)h(x,r)r
, and consider the embedding

\textnormal{Isom}(W)\times U(1)\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Isom}(V)
defined as
(\varphi,\xi)\mapstochar\rightarrow\varphi\circ\phi_{r}^{\xi}=\phi_{r}^{\xi}\circ\varphi.

By (
13
), we get that

\displaystyle\textnormal{Stab}_{\textnormal{Isom}(V)}(H_{r})=\textnormal{Isom}(W)\times U(1)\subset\textnormal{Isom}(V).

(14)

Consider the element
\phi\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
in the statement of the lemma.
Since
\phi
fixes
H_{r}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
pointwise, (
14
) implies that

\phi=a\cdot\phi_{r}^{\xi}
for some
a\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast},\xi\in U(1)
.
Since
\phi\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
, we get
a\in\mu_{K}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
, and as
\phi^{m}=\textnormal{id}\in\textnormal{Isom}(\mathbb{C}H^{n})
, we get
\xi\in\mu_{K}
.
∎

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
r,t\in{\mathscr{R}}
. The following are equivalent:

1.

One has
\phi_{r}=\phi_{t}\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
.

2.

There exist
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}/m-\{0\}
such that
\phi_{r}^{i}=\phi_{t}^{j}\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
.

3.

There exist
a,b\in\mathcal{O}_{K}-\{0\}
with
\left|a\right|^{2}=\left|b\right|^{2}
such that
a\cdot r=b\cdot t\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

4.

One has
H_{r}=H_{t}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
.

Proof.

[1\implies 2]:
This is clear.

[2\implies 3]:
Assume
2
.
Consider the equality

\zeta^{i}\cdot r=\phi_{r}^{i}(r)=\phi_{t}^{j}(r)=r-(1-\zeta^{j})\cdot h(r,t)\cdot t\in\Lambda.

Thus, there exist
a,b\in\mathcal{O}_{K}-\{0\}
with
a\cdot t=b\cdot r.
We have
\left|a\right|^{2}=h(a\cdot t,a\cdot t)=h(b\cdot r,b\cdot r)=\left|b\right|^{2}
. This implies
3
.

[3\implies 1]:
Assume
3
. Write
\xi=b/a\in K^{\ast}
; then
t=\xi\cdot r
with
\left|\xi\right|^{2}=1
, so that

\zeta^{i}\cdot r=\phi_{r}^{i}(r)=\phi_{t}^{j}(r)=\phi_{\xi r}^{j}(r)=r-(1-\zeta^{j})h(r,\xi r)\xi r=\zeta^{j}\cdot r\in V,

from which we conclude that
i=j
. Moreover, for
x\in V
, one gets

\phi_{t}(x)=\phi_{\xi\cdot r}(x)=x-(1-\zeta)\cdot h(x,\xi\cdot r)\cdot\xi\cdot r=x-(1-\zeta)\cdot h(x,r)\cdot r=\phi_{r}(x).

Hence
\phi_{r}=\phi_{t}
, proving
1
. We conclude that
[1\iff 2\iff 3]
.

[3\implies 4]:
This is clear.

[4\implies 2]:
Assume that
H_{r}=H_{t}
. Then
\phi_{t}=\xi\cdot\phi_{r}^{i}
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}/m
and
\xi\in\mu_{K}
, see Lemma
2.6
. Note that
i\neq 0
. Assume for a contradiction that
\xi\neq 1
; then the equality

t=\phi_{t}(t)=\xi\cdot\phi_{r}^{i}(t)=\xi\cdot t-\xi\cdot(1-\zeta^{i})h(t,r)\cdot r

shows that if we define
a=1-\xi
and
b=(\zeta^{i}-1)\cdot\xi\cdot h(t,r)
, we get

a\cdot t=b\cdot r
and
a,b\neq 0
since
\xi\neq 1
.
We have
\left|a\right|^{2}=h(a\cdot t,a\cdot t)=h(b\cdot r,b\cdot r)=\left|b\right|^{2}
hence
3
holds, and hence
1
by the above, so that
a\cdot\phi_{r}^{i}=\phi_{t}=\phi_{r}
which implies
a=1
which is absurd. We conclude that
a=1
, so that
\phi_{t}=\phi_{r}^{i}
which proves
2
since
i\in\mathbb{Z}/m-\left\{0\right\}
. This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Definition 2.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}=\{H_{r}\mid r\in{\mathscr{R}}\}
. For
x\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}(x)

\displaystyle=\left\{H\in{\mathcal{H}}\mid x\in H\right\},

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\langle\phi_{r}^{i}\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda){\textnormal{ with }}r\in{\mathscr{R}},i\in\mathbb{Z}/m\mid x\in H_{r}\rangle,

\displaystyle G(x)

\displaystyle=\langle[\phi_{r}^{i}]\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)/\mu_{K}=L{\textnormal{ with }}r\in{\mathscr{R}},i\in\mathbb{Z}/m\mid x\in H_{r}\rangle,

The hyperplanes
H\in{\mathcal{H}}(x)
are called the
nodes
of
x
. We say that
x
has
k
nodes
if the cardinality of
{\mathcal{H}}(x)
is
k
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
. The composition
G(x)\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\rightarrow L
is injective, hence defines an isomorphism
G^{\prime}(x)\xrightarrow{\sim}G(x)
.

Proof.

Assume
{\mathcal{H}}(x)=\left\{H_{r_{1}},\dotsc,H_{r_{k}}\right\}
, so that
G(x)=\langle\phi_{r_{1}},\dotsc,\phi_{r_{k}}\rangle
by Lemma
2.10
. Assume that for some
n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}/m
, the element
\psi\coloneqq\phi_{r_{1}}^{n_{1}}\cdots\phi_{r_{k}}^{n_{k}}\in G(x)
maps to zero under the map
G(x)\rightarrow L
. Then
\psi=\xi
for some
\xi\in\mu_{K}\subset\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
. Let
W\subset V
be the orthogonal complement of
\langle r_{1}\rangle\oplus\cdots\oplus\langle r_{k}\rangle
, so that

V=\langle r_{1}\rangle\oplus\cdots\oplus\langle r_{k}\rangle\oplus W

as complex hermitian vector spaces. Since the signature of
h\colon V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is
(n,1)
, the subspace
W
is non-zero. Moreover,
\psi
is the identity on
W
, hence
\xi=1\in\mu_{K}
. It follows that
\psi=\textnormal{id}
, which shows that the map
G(x)\rightarrow L
is injective as desired.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
x\in{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
be an element with
k
nodes (see Definition
2.8
). Let
{\mathcal{H}}(x)=\left\{H_{r_{1}},\dotsc,H_{r_{k}}\right\}
for some
r_{i}\in{\mathscr{R}}
. Then
G(x)=\langle[\phi_{r_{1}}],\dotsc,[\phi_{r_{k}}]\rangle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{k}.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.9
, it suffices to prove that
G^{\prime}(x)=\langle\phi_{r_{1}},\dotsc,\phi_{r_{k}}\rangle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{k}.
For this, let
r,t\in{\mathscr{R}}
. Then, for
z\in\Lambda
, one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi_{r}^{i}(\phi_{t}^{j}(z))=z&-(1-\zeta^{j})h(z,t)\cdot t-(1-\zeta^{i})h(z,r)\cdot r\\
&+(1-\zeta^{i})(1-\zeta^{j})h(z,t)h(t,r)\cdot r.\end{split}

(15)

Suppose that
H_{r},H_{t}\in{\mathcal{H}}(x)
, with
H_{r}\neq H_{t}
. By Theorem
2.5
, we have
h(r,t)=0
; by equation (
15
), this implies that
\phi_{r}^{i}\circ\phi_{t}^{j}=\phi_{t}^{j}\circ\phi_{r}^{i}
for each
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}/m
. We conclude that the group
G^{\prime}(x)
is abelian.

Write
{\mathcal{H}}(x)=\left\{H_{r_{1}},\dotsc,H_{r_{k}}\right\}
for some
r_{1},\dotsc,r_{k}\in{\mathscr{R}}
. By the above, we have
\langle\phi_{r_{1}},\dotsc,\phi_{r_{k}}\rangle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{k}
. Thus, to prove the lemma, it suffices at this point to prove that
G^{\prime}(x)=\langle\phi_{r_{1}},\dotsc,\phi_{r_{k}}\rangle
. Let
\phi_{r}\in L
with
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
such that
h(x,r)=0
. By definition,
G^{\prime}(x)
is generated by such elements. We claim that
\phi_{r}\in\langle\phi_{r_{1}},\dotsc,\phi_{r_{k}}\rangle
. To see this, observe that
H_{r}\in{\mathcal{H}}(x)
. Thus
H_{r}=H_{r_{i}}
for some
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
. By Proposition
2.7
, we have
\phi_{r}=\phi_{r_{i}}
and this proves what we want.
∎

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\phi=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\phi_{r_{j}}^{i_{j}}\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
for some set
\{r_{j}\}
of mutually orthogonal short roots
r_{j}\in{\mathscr{R}}
, where
i_{j}\not\equiv 0\bmod m
for each
j
. Then
\left(\mathbb{C}H^{n}\right)^{\phi}=\cap_{j=1}^{k}H_{r_{j}}
.

Proof.

Let
y\in V
be representing an element in
\left(\mathbb{C}H^{n}\right)^{\phi}
. Since the
r_{i}
are orthogonal, and
\phi(y)=\lambda
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
, we have

\phi(y)=y-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left(1-\zeta^{i_{j}}\right)h(y,r_{j})r_{j}=\lambda y,

hence
(1-\lambda)y=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left(1-\zeta^{i_{j}}\right)h(y,r_{j})r_{j}\in V
. But
y
spans a negative definite subspace of
V
while the
r_{j}
span a positive definite subspace, so that we must have
1-\lambda=0=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left(1-\zeta^{i_{j}}\right)h(y,r_{j})r_{j}
. Since the
r_{j}
are mutually orthogonal, they are linearly independent; since
\zeta^{i_{j}}\neq 1
we find
h(y,r_{j})=0
for each
j
. Conversely, if
x\in\cap H_{r_{j}}
, then
\phi_{r_{j}}^{i_{j}}(x)=x
for each
j
. The lemma follows.
∎

2.4

Examples

Observe that the condition on the different ideal
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K} \subset\mathcal{O}_{K}
in Definition
2.2
above is satisfied in several natural cases.

Example 2.12
.

Suppose that
K/\mathbb{Q}
is an imaginary quadratic extension, or that
K=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{m})
is a cyclotomic field for some integer
m\geq 3
. We claim that
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}=(\eta)\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}
for some element
\eta\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
such that
\sigma(\eta)=-\eta
.
To see this, note first that if
K/\mathbb{Q}
is an imaginary quadratic field extension with discriminant
\Delta
, then
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}=(\sqrt{\Delta})
and the assertion is immediate.
Thus, assume
K=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{m})
with
\zeta_{m}=e^{2\pi i/m}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Consider the subfield
F\coloneqq\mathbb{Q}(\mu)\subset\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{m})=K
, where
\mu\coloneqq\zeta_{m}+\zeta_{m}^{-1}
. Since
\mathcal{O}_{K}=\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{m}]
we have
\mathcal{O}_{K}=\mathcal{O}_{F}[\zeta_{m}]
. Let
f(x)\coloneqq x^{2}-\mu x+1\in\mathcal{O}_{F}[x]
and notice that
f(x)
is the minimal polynomial of
\zeta_{m}
over
F
. We have
f^{\prime}(\zeta_{m})=2\zeta_{m}-\mu=\zeta_{m}-\zeta_{m}^{-1}
hence
{\mathfrak{D}}_{M/F}=\left(f^{\prime}(\zeta_{m})\right)=\left(\zeta_{m}-\zeta_{m}^{-1}\right)
, see
[
Neu99
, Chapter III, Proposition 2.4]
.
By
[
Lia76
]
, we know that
\mathcal{O}_{F}=\mathbb{Z}[\mu]
. Hence, if
g(x)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
is the minimal polynomial of
\mu
over
\mathbb{Q}
, then
{\mathfrak{D}}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=(g^{\prime}(\mu))
by
[
Neu99
, Chapter III, Proposition 2.4]
again. Since
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}={\mathfrak{D}}_{K/F}{\mathfrak{D}}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}
(c.f.
[
Neu99
, Chapter III, Proposition 2.2]
), we obtain

{\mathfrak{D}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}={\mathfrak{D}}_{K/F}{\mathfrak{D}}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}=\left((\zeta_{m}-\zeta_{m}^{-1})\cdot g^{\prime}(\mu)\right)

proving the claim.

Example 2.13
.

Let
K
be a CM field, and let
F\subset K
be the maximal totally real subfield of
K
.
Let
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
such that
\tau(\lambda)>0
for some embedding
\tau\colon F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\psi(\lambda)<0
for all other
\psi\neq\tau\in\textnormal{Hom}(F,\mathbb{R})
(such an element exists by Lemma
2.14
below). If we define
\Lambda\coloneqq(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{n+1},h),
where
h
is the hermitian form given by the diagonal matrix
{\textnormal{diag}}(-\lambda,1,\dotsc,1)
, then
\Lambda
satisfies Condition
2
in Definition
2.2
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
K
be a CM field, and let
F\subset K
be the maximal totally real subfield of
K
.
There exists an element
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
with
\tau(\lambda)>0
for some embedding
\tau\colon F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\psi(\lambda)<0
for all other
\psi\neq\tau\in\textnormal{Hom}(F,\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\lambda}\in\mathcal{O}_{F}-\mathbb{Z}
. Note that we can write
\textnormal{Hom}(F,\mathbb{R})=\left\{\tau,\psi_{2},\dotsc,\psi_{g}\right\}
with
\tau(\tilde{\lambda})<\psi_{2}(\tilde{\lambda})<\cdots<\psi_{g}(\tilde{\lambda})
. Let
r\in\mathbb{Q}
be a rational number such that
\tau(\tilde{\lambda})<r<\psi_{2}(\tilde{\lambda})
. Possibly after replacing
\tilde{\lambda}
by
n\cdot\tilde{\lambda}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we may assume that
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. The element
\lambda=r-\tilde{\lambda}\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
satisfies the requirements.
∎

3

Anti-unitary involutions

The goal of this section is to study anti-unitary involutions on
\Lambda
.

3.1

Anti-unitary involutions and copies of real hyperbolic space

Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1\geq 2
. We continue with the notation of Sections
2.1
and
2.2
.

Definition 3.1
.

An
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-linear bijection
\alpha\colon\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda
is
anti-unitary
if for all
x,y\in\Lambda
and
b\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
, one has

\alpha(b\cdot x)=\sigma(b)\cdot\alpha(x)
and
h(\alpha(x),\alpha(y))=\sigma(h(x,y))\in\mathcal{O}_{K}.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}\subset\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)^{\prime}
be the set of anti-unitary involutions
\alpha\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
.
The group
\mu_{K}
acts on
{\mathscr{A}}
by multiplication; define

P{\mathscr{A}}\coloneqq\mu_{K}\setminus{\mathscr{A}}.

Example 3.2
.

Consider Example
2.13
. Thus,
K
is a CM field,
F\subset K
is the maximal totally real subfield of
K
,
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
is an element such that
\tau(\lambda)>0
for some embedding
\tau\colon F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\psi(\lambda)<0
for all other
\psi\neq\tau\in\textnormal{Hom}(F,\mathbb{R})
(cf. Lemma
2.14
),
\Lambda=(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{n+1},h)
where
h
is the hermitian form given by
{\textnormal{diag}}(-\lambda,1,\dotsc,1)
. To obtain examples of anti-unitary involutions
\alpha\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
, we proceed as follows. For each
\epsilon=(\epsilon_{0},\dotsc,\epsilon_{n})\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n+1}
, define

\alpha_{\epsilon}\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda\quad\text{ as }\quad\alpha_{\epsilon}\left(x_{0},\dotsc,x_{n}\right)=\left(\epsilon_{0}\bar{x}_{0},\dotsc,\epsilon_{n}\bar{x}_{n}\right).

For
x,y\in\Lambda,b\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
, one has
\alpha_{\epsilon}(b\cdot x)=\bar{b}\cdot\alpha_{\epsilon}(x)
and

h(\alpha_{\epsilon}(x),\alpha_{\epsilon}(y))=\sigma(h(x,y))
because
\sigma(\lambda)=\lambda
. Hence
\alpha_{\epsilon}
is an anti-unitary involution for each
\epsilon\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n+1}
.

Let
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)^{\prime}
be the group of unitary and anti-unitary
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-linear bijections
\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda
. Then

\displaystyle\mu_{K}\subset\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\subset\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)^{\prime}\quad{\textnormal{ and we define }}\quad L^{\prime}\coloneqq\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)^{\prime}/\mu_{K}.

(16)

Let
x\in K^{\ast}
. Observe that

\displaystyle\left(x\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}{\textnormal{ and }}x\cdot\sigma(x)=1\right)\quad\iff\quad\left(x\in\mu_{K}\right).

(17)

Indeed, for an embedding
\varphi\colon K\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, one has
\left|\varphi(x)\right|^{2}=\varphi(x\cdot\sigma(x))
;
if
x\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}
, then
\left|\varphi(x)\right|=1
for each
\varphi
if and only if
x
is a root of
1

[
Mil08
, Corollary 5.6]
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\textnormal{Isom}({\mathbb{C}}H^{n})
be the group of isometries
f\colon\mathbb{C}H^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{C}H^{n}
. The natural homomorphism
L^{\prime}\rightarrow\textnormal{Isom}({\mathbb{C}}H^{n})
is injective.

Proof.

This follows readily from (
17
). ∎

Thus, any
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
defines an anti-holomorphic involution

\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}H^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}

that determines
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
uniquely (whence the abuse of notation).
We define

\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\coloneqq(\mathbb{C}H^{n})^{\alpha}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}.

For each element
\alpha\in{\mathscr{A}}
, the hermitian form
h
on
V
induces a non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form

h|_{V^{\alpha}}\colon V^{\alpha}\times V^{\alpha}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

on the real vector space

\displaystyle V^{\alpha}=\Lambda^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F},\tau}\mathbb{R}.

(18)

The form
h|_{V^{\alpha}}
has signature
(n,1)
. The following lemma is readily proved:

Lemma 3.4
.

For
\alpha\in{\mathscr{A}}
, let
\mathbb{P}(V^{\alpha})
be the space of real lines in
V^{\alpha}
, and let
\mathbb{R}H(V^{\alpha})\subset\mathbb{P}(V^{\alpha})
be the subspace of lines in
V^{\alpha}
negative for
h|_{V^{\alpha}}
. The canonical isomorphism
\mathbb{P}(V^{\alpha})\cong\mathbb{P}(V)^{\alpha}
restricts to an isomorphism
{\mathbb{R}}H(V^{\alpha})\cong{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha}
. ∎

We conclude that for each
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
, the space

\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}=(\mathbb{C}H^{n})^{\alpha}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}

is isometric to the real hyperbolic space of dimension
n
.

3.2

Anti-unitary involutions and arithmetic lattices in
\textnormal{PO}(n,1)

Continue with the above notation. Define

L_{\alpha}\coloneqq\textnormal{Stab}_{L}(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha})\subset L\quad\quad{\textnormal{(the stabilizer of $\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}$ in $L$).}}

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
. Then

L_{\alpha}=N_{L}(\alpha)\coloneqq\left\{\gamma\in L\mid\gamma\circ\alpha=\alpha\circ\gamma\in L^{\prime}\right\}\subset L.

Proof.

The inclusion
N_{L}(\alpha)\subset L_{\alpha}
is clear. For the other inclusion, let
g\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
with associated element
[g]\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)/\mu_{K}=L
. Assume that
[g]\in L_{\alpha}
for some element
\alpha\in{\mathscr{A}}
with class
[\alpha]\in P{\mathscr{A}}
. Notice that
V=V^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
. Hence each
z\in V
can be written as
z=x+i\cdot y
where
x,y\in V^{\alpha}
. Since
[g]\in L_{\alpha}
, we get
[gx]\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
for each
[x]\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
. In particular, there exists an element
\xi\in\mu_{K}
such that

\alpha(gx)=\xi\cdot(gx)\in V

for each
x\in V^{\alpha}
.
Consequently, for
[z]=[x+i\cdot y]\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
with
x,y\in V^{\alpha}
, we have

[g(\alpha z)]=[gx-i\cdot gy]=[\xi\cdot gx-i\cdot\xi\cdot gy]=[\alpha(gx)-i\cdot\alpha(gy)]=[\alpha(gz)].

This shows that
[g]\circ[\alpha]=[\alpha]\circ[g]
as elements of
\textnormal{Isom}(\mathbb{C}H^{n})
. Thus, in view of Lemma
3.3
, we get
[g]\circ[\alpha]=[\alpha]\circ[g]
as elements of
L^{\prime}
, and we are done.
∎

Let
\alpha\in{\mathscr{A}}
, consider the lattice
\Lambda^{\alpha}\subset V^{\alpha}
(see (
18
)) and the space of negative lines
\mathbb{R}H(V^{\alpha})\subset\mathbb{P}(V^{\alpha})
. We have
{\textnormal{Isom}}(\mathbb{R}H(V^{\alpha}))\cong{\textnormal{Isom}}(\mathbb{R}H^{n})={\textnormal{PO}}(n,1)
by Lemma
3.3
. Let
{\textnormal{O}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
be the group of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-linear isometries
\gamma\colon\Lambda^{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda^{\alpha}
, and define
{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})={\textnormal{O}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})/\left\{\pm 1\right\}
. There are canonical embeddings

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\textnormal{Isom}}(\mathbb{R}H(V^{\alpha}))={\textnormal{PO}}(V^{\alpha})\quad{\textnormal{ and }}\quad{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\textnormal{PO}}(V^{\alpha}).

(19)

Explicitly, the embedding
L_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{PO}(V^{\alpha})
in (
19
) is defined as follows. We consider
K
as a subfield of
\mathbb{C}
via the embedding
\tau
. This identifies
\zeta\in K
with a primitive
m
-th root of unity in
\mathbb{C}
. Let
\zeta_{2m}=e^{2\pi i/2m}\in\mathbb{C}
. Let
[g]\in L_{\alpha}
with representative
g\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
. Then
g\alpha g^{-1}=\zeta^{i}\cdot\alpha
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. This implies that
g(V^{\alpha})=V^{\zeta^{i}\alpha}
. Let
\xi\in\langle\zeta_{2m}\rangle\subset\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
such that
\xi^{2}=\zeta^{-i}
. Then multiplication by
\xi
defines an isomorphism of complex vector spaces
\xi\colon V\xrightarrow{\sim}V
that identifies
V^{\zeta^{i}\alpha}
with
V^{\alpha}
. Sending
[g]\in L_{\alpha}
to the class
[\xi\circ g]\in\textnormal{PO}(V^{\alpha})
of the composition
\xi\circ g
, which is an isometry
V^{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\sim}V^{\zeta^{i}\alpha}\xrightarrow{\sim}V^{\alpha}
, gives a well-defined embedding
L_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow\textnormal{PO}(V^{\alpha})
which coincides with the first map appearing in equation (
19
).

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
\alpha\in{\mathscr{A}}
. The subgroups
L_{\alpha}\subset\textnormal{PO}(V^{\alpha})
and
{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})\subset\textnormal{PO}(V^{\alpha})
are commensurable. In particular,
L_{\alpha}\subset\textnormal{PO}(V^{\alpha})
is an arithmetic lattice.

Proof.

We follow the ideas of the proof of
[
HR18
, Theorem 4.10]
. Let
g\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
such that
[g]\in L_{\alpha}
. Recall that
\zeta\in K
is a primitive
m
-th root of unity in
K
that generates the torsion subgroup
\mu_{K}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}
, see Section
2.2
. Define

L_{\alpha}^{I}\coloneqq\left\{[g]\in L_{\alpha}\mid h\alpha h^{-1}=\zeta^{i}\cdot\alpha{\textnormal{ with $i\in 2\mathbb{Z}$, for some (equivalently, any) }}h\in[g]\right\}.

Note that
L_{\alpha}^{I}\subset L_{\alpha}
is a normal subgroup of
L_{\alpha}
. Also note that for each
[g]\in L_{\alpha}^{I}
there is an element
h\in[g]
, unique up to sign, such that
h\alpha h^{-1}=\alpha
.
Consequently, there is a natural embedding

\displaystyle f\colon L_{\alpha}^{I}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F}).

(20)

In fact, one has
L_{\alpha}\cap{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})=L_{\alpha}^{I}\subset{\textnormal{PO}}(V^{\alpha}),

and this intersection has index at most two in
L_{\alpha}
. Indeed, either
L_{\alpha}^{I}=L_{\alpha}
or there exists an element
[g]\in L_{\alpha}
such that for some
h\in[g]
one has
h\alpha h^{-1}=\zeta^{i}\cdot\alpha
for some odd
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, and for any two such elements
[g_{i}]\in L_{\alpha}
(
i=1,2
), one has
[g_{1}g_{2}]\in L_{\alpha}^{I}\subset L_{\alpha}
.

It remains to show that
L_{\alpha}^{I}
has finite index in
{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
. To prove this, let
i\colon\Lambda^{\alpha}\hookrightarrow\Lambda
denote the inclusion. The induced map
i\colon\Lambda^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{K}\hookrightarrow\Lambda
has finite cokernel; let
d
be its order. There is a map
\varphi\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{K}
such that
i\circ\varphi=d
and
\varphi\circ i=d
, and such that the induced map
\varphi_{K}\colon\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}K\rightarrow\Lambda^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}K
is
d
times the inverse
i^{-1}
of
i\colon\Lambda^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}K\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}K
. For an element
M\in{\mathrm{O}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
, let
M_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}
be the induced map
\Lambda^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{K}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Define

\displaystyle{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}\coloneqq\left\{[M]\in{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})\mid i\circ M_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\circ\varphi=d\cdot\overline{M}{\textnormal{ for }}\overline{M}\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\right\}.

We claim that the embedding
f\colon L_{\alpha}^{I}\hookrightarrow{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
defined in (
20
) factors as

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}^{I}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F}).

(21)

To prove this, let
[g]\in L_{\alpha}^{I}
with
g\in[g]
such that
g\alpha=\alpha g
. Let
M_{g}=g|_{\Lambda^{\alpha}}
be the restriction
\Lambda^{\alpha}\rightarrow\Lambda^{\alpha}
of
g
to
\Lambda^{\alpha}
. Then
f([g])=[M_{g}]\in{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
. As we have
i\circ(M_{g})_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\circ\varphi=g\circ i\circ\varphi=d\cdot g
as maps
\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
, we get
[M_{g}]\in{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}
. This proves the factorization (
21
).

Next, we claim that the first inclusion in (
21
) is an equality. To see this, let
[M]\in{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}
. Let
\overline{M}\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
such that
\overline{M}(\Lambda^{\alpha})=\Lambda^{\alpha}
and
\overline{M}|_{\Lambda^{\alpha}}=M
. It suffices to prove that the class
[\overline{M}]\in L
is contained in
L_{\alpha}^{I}\subset L_{\alpha}\subset L
. Since
[\overline{M}]\in L
stabilizes
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
, we have
[\overline{M}]\in L_{\alpha}=N_{L}(\alpha)
by Lemma
3.5
. Consequently,
\overline{M}\alpha(\overline{M})^{-1}=\zeta^{i}\cdot\alpha
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. As
\overline{M}(\Lambda^{\alpha})=\Lambda^{\alpha}
, we get
i\equiv 0\bmod m
. Therefore,
[\overline{M}]\in L_{\alpha}^{I}
, proving the claim. We conclude that

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}^{I}={\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}\subset{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F}).

(22)

To finish the proof, it remains to show
{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}\subset{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
is a subgroup of finite index. Consider the submodule
\varphi(\Lambda)\subset\Lambda^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{K}
, and note that

\displaystyle{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}=\left\{[M]\in{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})\mid M_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}(\varphi(\Lambda))=\varphi(\Lambda)\right\}.

(23)

Let

H(\Lambda^{\alpha})\coloneqq\textnormal{Ker}\left({\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})\rightarrow\textnormal{PGL}(\Lambda^{\alpha}/d\Lambda^{\alpha})\right).

Thus,
H(\Lambda^{\alpha})\subset{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
is the subgroup of
[M]\in{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
such that there exists
\varepsilon\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}
, such that for each
x\in\Lambda^{\alpha}
, we have
M\cdot x=\varepsilon\cdot x+dy
for some
y\in\Lambda^{\alpha}
. Thus, if
x\in\varphi(\Lambda)
, we get that
M_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\cdot x=\varepsilon\cdot x+dy\in\varphi(\Lambda)
. Consequently,
M_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}(\varphi(\Lambda))=\varphi(\Lambda)
, so that
[M]\in{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}
by (
23
). We conclude that

H(\Lambda^{\alpha})\subset{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}\subset{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F}).

This implies that
{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}\subset{\textnormal{PO}}(\Lambda^{\alpha})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
is a subgroup of finite index.
∎

We will also need the following finiteness result. For a finite group
G
and a
G
-group
A
, let
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,A)
denote the first cohomology group of
G
with coefficients in
A
in the sense of nonabelian group cohomology. See
[
Ser73
, Chapitre I, §5.1]
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Suppose that
{\mathscr{A}}\neq\emptyset
. Let
\alpha_{0}\in{\mathscr{A}}
, and define
G=\textnormal{Gal}(K/F)
.

1.

The group
G
acts on
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
, and there is a bijection
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\backslash{\mathscr{A}}\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda))
.

2.

The sets
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\backslash{\mathscr{A}}
and
C{\mathscr{A}}=L\backslash P{\mathscr{A}}
are finite.

Proof.

1. We can let
G
act on
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
via the involution
\sigma\colon\gamma\mapstochar\rightarrow\alpha_{0}\circ\gamma\circ\alpha_{0}
.
Consider

\displaystyle{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda))

\displaystyle={\mathrm{Z}}^{1}(G,\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda))/_{\sim}=\left\{{\textnormal{$1$-cocycles }}G\rightarrow\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda),f\colon s\mapstochar\rightarrow a_{s}\right\}/_{\sim}

where
f\sim f^{\prime}
if there exists
b\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
such that
f^{\prime}(s)=b^{-1}f(s)^{s}b\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
for all
s\in G
. See
[
Ser73
, Chapitre I, §5.1]
. If
g
denotes the generator of
G
, the map

f\mapstochar\rightarrow f(g)
defines a bijection between the set of
1
-cocycles
{\mathrm{Z}}^{1}(G,\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda))
and the set of elements
\gamma\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
that satisfy
\gamma\cdot\sigma(\gamma)=\textnormal{id}
. This yields a bijection

{\mathscr{A}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathrm{Z}}^{1}(G,\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)),\quad\alpha\mapstochar\rightarrow\alpha\circ\alpha_{0},

with inverse given by
\gamma\mapstochar\rightarrow\gamma\circ\alpha_{0}
. Let
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathscr{A}}
and
g\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
. Then
g^{-1}\beta g=\alpha
if and only if
g^{-1}\left(\beta\circ\alpha_{0}\right)\sigma(g)=\alpha\circ\alpha_{0}
, hence item
1
is proved.

2. Since the canonical map
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\backslash{\mathscr{A}}\rightarrow L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}
is surjective, it suffices to prove that
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\backslash{\mathscr{A}}
is finite. By item
1
, we need to show that
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda))
is finite. Note that
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)=\textnormal{Aut}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}(\Lambda)=U(\Lambda)(\mathcal{O}_{K})
arises as the group of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-rational points of a linear algebraic group
U(\Lambda)_{K}\subset\textnormal{GL}(\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}K)
over
K
. Moreover, the involution
\sigma\colon\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\rightarrow\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
defined in item
1
extends to an involution
\sigma\colon U(\Lambda)_{K}\rightarrow U(\Lambda)_{K}
, which implies that
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
is an
arithmetic
G
-group
in the sense of
[
BS64
, Section 3.4]
. In particular, the set
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(G,\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda))
is finite by
[
BS64
, Proposition 3.8]
. The proof is finished.
∎

3.3

Anti-unitary involutions and the hyperplane arrangement

Continue with the above notation.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\alpha\in{\mathscr{A}}
and
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
. Then
\alpha\circ\phi_{r}^{i}=\phi_{\alpha(r)}^{-i}\circ\alpha
.

Proof.

Indeed,

\alpha(\phi_{r}^{i}(x))=\alpha(x-(1-\zeta^{i})h(x,r)\cdot r)=\phi_{\alpha(r)}^{-i}(\alpha(x))
for
x\in\Lambda
. ∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
x\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha}
and write
{\mathcal{H}}(x)=\left\{H_{r_{1}},\dotsc,H_{r_{k}}\right\}
,
r_{i}\in{\mathscr{R}}
.

1.

Let
i\in\{1,\dotsc,k\}
. Then
\alpha(H_{r_{i}})=H_{\alpha(r_{i})}=H_{r_{j}}
for some
j\in\{1,\dotsc,k\}
.

2.

Let
i,j\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
such that
H_{\alpha(r_{i})}=H_{r_{j}}
. Then is some element
\lambda_{i}\in K^{\ast}
with
\left|\lambda_{i}\right|=1
, such that
\alpha(r_{i})=\lambda_{i}\cdot r_{j}
.

Proof.

1. For any
\beta\in{\mathscr{A}}
and
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
, we have

\beta(H_{r})=H_{\beta(r)}.

Since
x\in H_{r_{i}}
, we have
x=\alpha(x)\in\alpha(H_{r_{i}})=H_{\alpha(r_{i})}
for every
i
. In particular, we have
H_{\alpha(r_{i})}\in{\mathcal{H}}(x)
(see Definition
2.8
), so that
H_{\alpha(r_{i})}=H_{r_{j}}
for some
j\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
.

2. This follows from Proposition
2.7
.
∎

Definition 3.10
.

Let
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha}
. Write
{\mathcal{H}}(x)=\left\{H_{r_{1}},\dotsc,H_{r_{k}}\right\}
, see Definition
2.8
. By Lemma
3.9
, the involution
\alpha
induces an involution on the set
{\mathcal{H}}(x)
. Define
\alpha\colon I\rightarrow I
as the resulting involution on the set
I=\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha}
. Write
{\mathcal{H}}(x)=\left\{H_{r_{1}},\dotsc,H_{r_{k}}\right\}
, and let
g=\phi_{r_{1}}^{i_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{r_{k}}^{i_{k}}\in G(x)
for some
i_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}/m
. The following are equivalent:

1.

We have
g\circ\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
. Equivalently:
g\circ\alpha\in L^{\prime}
is an involution.

2.

For each
j\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
, we have
i_{j}\equiv i_{\alpha(j)}\bmod m
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
and Definition
3.10
, for each
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
, there exists
\xi_{i}\in K^{\ast}
with
\left|\lambda_{i}\right|=1
such that
\alpha(r_{i})=\lambda_{i}\cdot r_{\alpha(i)}
. In particular,
\phi_{\alpha(r_{i})}=\phi_{r_{\alpha(i)}}
, see Proposition
2.7
. Therefore, we have:

\displaystyle g\circ\alpha\circ g\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=\phi_{r_{1}}^{i_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{r_{k}}^{i_{k}}\circ\alpha\circ\phi_{r_{1}}^{i_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{r_{k}}^{i_{k}}\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=\phi_{r_{1}}^{i_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{r_{k}}^{i_{k}}\circ\phi_{r_{1}}^{-i_{\alpha(1)}}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{r_{k}}^{-i_{\alpha(k)}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{r_{1}}^{i_{1}-i_{\alpha(1)}}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{r_{k}}^{i_{k}-i_{\alpha(k)}}\in G(x).

The proposition follows.
∎

4

Attaching the hyperbolic pieces

In this section, we construct a topological space by gluing together arithmetic hyperbolic pieces. Afterwards, in Section
5
, we provide this topological space first with a path metric and then with a complete hyperbolic orbifold structure.

4.1

Definition of the attachment

Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1
, for some
n\geq 1
. Continue with the notation of Sections
2
and
3
. Define

\widetilde{Y}\coloneqq\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}.

We will glue the different copies of hyperbolic space
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
by defining an equivalence relation
\sim
on
\widetilde{Y}
.

Definition 4.1
.

Define a relation
\sim
on
{\widetilde{Y}}
as follows. Let

(x,\alpha),(y,\beta)\in{\widetilde{Y}}

with
\alpha,\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}
. Then
(x,\alpha)\sim(y,\beta)
if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

We have
x=y\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
.

2.

If
\alpha\neq\beta
, then
x=y\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
lies in
{\mathscr{H}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, and
\beta\circ\alpha\in G(x)=G(y)\subset L
.

Remark 4.2
.

We will see below (see Lemma
4.6
) that the gluing rules can be rephrased as follows: we glue
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
and
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}
along their intersection, provided that for some (equivalently, any)
x\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}
, the real structures
\alpha
and
\beta
differ by a product of reflections in hyperplanes
H_{r}\subset{\mathscr{H}}
that pass through
x
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The relation
\sim
on
{\widetilde{Y}}
defined above is an equivalence relation.

Proof.

Let
(x,\alpha),(y,\beta),(z,\gamma)\in{\widetilde{Y}}
. Clearly
(x,\alpha)\sim(x,\alpha)
. If
(x,\alpha)\sim(y,\beta)
and
(x,\alpha)\neq(y,\beta)
, then
x=y\in{\mathscr{H}}
and
\beta\circ\alpha\in G(x)=G(y)
. Thus, since
G(x)
is a group, we get
\alpha\circ\beta=(\beta\circ\alpha)^{-1}\in G(x)=G(y)
, hence
(y,\beta)\sim(x,\alpha)
. Finally, assume
(x,\alpha)\sim(y,\beta)
and
(y,\beta)\sim(z,\gamma)
. We claim that
(x,\alpha)\sim(z,\gamma)
. We may assume that
