(x,\alpha),(y,\beta),(z,\gamma)\in{\widetilde{Y}}
are pairwise distinct. This implies that
x=y=z
and that
\beta\circ\alpha\in G(x)=G(y)
and
\gamma\circ\beta\in G(y)=G(z)
. Thus, we get
\gamma\circ\beta\circ\beta\circ\alpha=\gamma\circ\alpha\in G(x)=G(z)
, proving the claim and hence the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

The action of
L
on
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
induces an action of
L
on
\widetilde{Y}
which is compatible with the equivalence relation
R
. Therefore,
L
acts naturally on
Y
. Moreover,

L\setminus\widetilde{Y}=\coprod_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}

for any set
C{\mathscr{A}}\subset P{\mathscr{A}}
of representatives for the quotient
L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}
.

Proof.

If
\phi\in L
, then
\phi\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\right)=\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\phi\alpha\phi^{-1}}
hence
L
acts on
\widetilde{Y}=\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
, and

L\setminus\widetilde{Y}=L\setminus\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}=\coprod_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}.

Now suppose that
(x,\alpha)\sim(y,\beta)\in\widetilde{Y}
and let
\phi\in L
. Then
\phi\cdot(x,\alpha)=(\phi x,\phi\alpha\phi^{-1})\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\phi\alpha\phi^{-1}}
and similarly,
\phi\cdot(y,\beta)\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\phi\beta\phi^{-1}}
. We claim that

(\phi x,\phi\alpha\phi^{-1})\sim(\phi y,\phi\beta\phi^{-1}).

For this, we may and do assume that
(x,\alpha)\neq(y,\beta)
, hence
x=y\in{\mathscr{H}}
and
\beta=g\circ\alpha
for some
g\in G(x)
as in Definition
4.1
. We get

\phi\beta\phi^{-1}\circ\phi\alpha\phi^{-1}=\phi\circ(\beta\circ\alpha)\circ\phi^{-1}\in\phi G(x)\phi^{-1}=G(\phi(x)).

This proves the lemma.
∎

Definition 4.5
.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1\geq 2
.
Define

Y({\mathscr{L}})={\widetilde{Y}}/\sim

as the quotient of
\widetilde{Y}
by the equivalence relation
\sim
introduced in Definition
4.1
, and equip
Y({\mathscr{L}})
with the quotient topology. Consider the natural action of the group
L
on
Y({\mathscr{L}})
, cf. Lemma
4.4
. Define

M({\mathscr{L}})\coloneqq L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}}).

Let
C{\mathscr{A}}\subset P{\mathscr{A}}
be a set of representatives for the action of
L
on
P{\mathscr{A}}
by conjugation. We call
M({\mathscr{L}})
the
attachment of the spaces
L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}
.

4.2

Basic properties of the attachment

Lemma 4.6
.

The following assertions are true.

1.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}
such that
(x,\alpha)\sim(x,\beta)
. Then
(y,\alpha)\sim(y,\beta)
for every
y\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}
.

2.

The natural map
\widetilde{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}
descends to a
L
-equivariant map
Y({\mathscr{L}})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}
.

Proof.

Let us prove item
1
. Since
(x,\alpha)\sim(x,\beta)
, there exists
g\in G(x)
such that
\beta=g\circ\alpha
. Write
{\mathcal{H}}(x)=\left\{H_{r_{1}},\dotsc,H_{r_{k}}\right\}
, so that

G(x)=\langle\phi_{r_{1}},\dotsc,\phi_{r_{k}}\rangle,\quad\quad g=\phi_{r_{1}}^{n_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{r_{k}}^{n_{k}},\quad\quad n_{1},\dotsc,n_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}/m.

Let
I\subset\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
be the subset of
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
such that
n_{i}\not\equiv 0\bmod m
. Then, since
\beta=g\circ\alpha
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}=\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cap(\mathbb{C}H^{n})^{g}=\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cap\bigcap_{i\in I}H_{r_{i}}

(24)

where the second equality holds by Lemma
2.11
. In particular, item
1
follows.
Item
2
is clear: if
(x,\alpha)\sim(y,\beta)\in{\widetilde{Y}}
, then
x=y\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

In the above notation, the following assertions are true.

1.

For each
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
, the image of the natural continuous map

L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})

is closed in
M({\mathscr{L}})
.

2.

Let
C{\mathscr{A}}\subset P{\mathscr{A}}
be a set of representatives for the action of
L
on
P{\mathscr{A}}
by conjugation.
The composition

\coprod_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)\xlongrightarrow{\sim}L\setminus\left(\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})=M({\mathscr{L}})

is an open embedding of topological spaces.

Proof.

1. Let
\varphi\colon L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})
denote the natural map. Let
p\colon{\widetilde{Y}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
and
q\colon Y({\mathscr{L}})\rightarrow L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})=M({\mathscr{L}})
be the respective quotient maps; it suffices to show that
p^{-1}(q^{-1}(\varphi(L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha})))
is closed in
{\widetilde{Y}}
, and hence that

\displaystyle p^{-1}(q^{-1}(\varphi(L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha})))\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}

(25)

is closed in
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}
for each
\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}
. We first consider the case
\beta=\alpha
.
Note that

p^{-1}(q^{-1}(\varphi(L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha})))\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}=\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}

which is indeed closed in
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
. For
\beta\neq\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
, assume that (
25
) is non-empty, and let
z
be a point in the intersection. Then there exists
g\in L
such that for
y\coloneqq g\cdot x
and
\gamma\coloneqq g\alpha g^{-1}
, we have
(y,\gamma)\sim(z,\beta)
. In particular,
y=z\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\gamma}\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}
, and by Lemma
4.6
and its proof (see equation (
24
)), we have

p^{-1}(q^{-1}(\varphi(L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha})))\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}=L_{\beta}\cdot\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\gamma}\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}\right)=L_{\beta}\cdot\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}\cap H_{r_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap H_{r_{p}}\right)

for suitable
r_{1},\dotsc,r_{p}\in{\mathscr{R}}
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle L_{\beta}\cdot\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}\cap\bigcap_{i=1}^{p}H_{r_{i}}\right)=\bigcup_{g\in L_{\beta}}\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}\cap H_{gr_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap H_{gr_{p}}\right).

(26)

Since the set
\left\{H_{r}\right\}_{r\in{\mathscr{R}}}
of subsets of
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is locally finite (see Lemma
2.3
), the set

\left\{\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}\cap H_{gr_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap H_{gr_{p}}\right\}_{g\in L_{\beta}}

of subsets of
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}
is locally finite. In particular, the set on the right hand side of the equality in (
26
) is closed in
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}
, and we are done.

2. Let
{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneqq\sqcup_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathscr{H}})\subset{\widetilde{Y}}
and let
{\widetilde{Y}}_{0}\coloneqq{\widetilde{Y}}-{\mathscr{H}}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
p\colon{\widetilde{Y}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
be the quotient map. We claim that the restriction
\psi\coloneqq p|_{{\widetilde{Y}}_{0}}
, which is a map

\psi\colon{\widetilde{Y}}_{0}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}}),

is an open embedding. Note that
\psi
is injective, because if
\psi(x,\alpha)=\psi(y,\beta)
then
(x,\alpha)\sim(y,\beta)
which implies that
(x,\alpha)=(y,\beta)
as
x,y\not\in{\mathscr{H}}
. Moreover, for any open subset
U\subset{\widetilde{Y}}_{0}
, the subset
\psi(U)
is open in
Y({\mathscr{L}})
because
p^{-1}(\psi(U))=U\subset{\widetilde{Y}}_{0}\subset{\widetilde{Y}}
and
{\widetilde{Y}}_{0}
is open in
{\widetilde{Y}}
by Lemma
2.3
. We are done.
∎

5

Hyperbolic orbifold structure of the attachment

Section
5
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.2
. The key is the following result.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1\geq 2
. With the notation of Section
4
, the following assertions are true.

1.

Each path-connected component
Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
of the space
Y({\mathscr{L}})
admits a path metric such that the natural map
Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is a local isometric embedding.

2.

The above path metrics on the path-connected components of
Y({\mathscr{L}})
induce complete path metrics on the path-connected components of
M({\mathscr{L}})=L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})
.

3.

Each
x\in M({\mathscr{L}})
admits an open neighbourhood
U\subset M({\mathscr{L}})
which is isometric to the quotient of a connected open
W\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n}
by a finite group of isometries.

5.1

Preliminaries on path metrics

In this section, we provide some preliminary results regarding pull-back metrics and quotient metrics. These results are certainly well-known; we state and prove them for lack of suitable reference.

In this paper, a continuous map of topological spaces
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
is called
proper
if the inverse image
f^{-1}(K)
of any compact subset
K\subset Y
is compact.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
X
and
Y
be topological spaces such that
Y
is locally compact and Hausdorff. Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a proper continuous map. Then
f
is closed.

Proof.

This is well-known (see e.g.
[
Lee13
, Theorem A.57]
).
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
a local embedding of path-connected topological spaces. Assume
Y
is given a path metric
d_{Y}
, and
X
is Hausdorff. For a path
\gamma\colon[a,b]\rightarrow X
, define its length
\ell(\gamma)
as
\ell(\gamma)=\ell(f\circ\gamma)
, where the latter denotes the length of the path
f_{\ast}(\gamma)\colon[a,b]\rightarrow Y
,
f_{\ast}(\gamma)=f\circ\gamma
.
For
x,y\in X
, let
d_{X}(x,y)
be the infimum of the lengths of paths which join
x
and
y
. Then the following assertions are true.

1.

d_{X}
defines a path metric on
X
which is compatible with the topology of
X
.

2.

With respect to the path metric
d_{X}
, the map
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
is a local isometric embedding.

3.

For each
x,y\in X
, we have
d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))\leq d_{X}(x,y)
. In particular, the map
f
is Lipschitz continuous.

Proof.

1. It is clear that
d_{X}
is a pseudometric. It is a path metric because
X
is Hausdorff. We claim that the topology that
d_{X}
induces on the set underlying
X
agrees with the topology of
X
. To see this, let
x\in X
and let
x\in U\subset X
be an open neighbourhood such that the induced map
U\rightarrow f(U)
is a homeomorphism. It suffices to show that the topology of
U
agrees with the topology given by the metric restriction
d_{U}=d_{X}|_{U}
. This follows from the fact that the topology of
f(U)\subset Y
agrees with the topology given by the metric restriction
d_{f(U)}=d_{Y}|_{f(U)}
.

2. Let
x\in X
and let
U\subset X
be an open neighbourhood of
x
such that the induced map
f\colon U\rightarrow f(U)
is a homeomorphism. Then the paths in
U
are in canonical bijection with the paths in
f(U)
, and also their lengths coincide by construction. Thus
f\colon U\rightarrow f(U)
is an isometry.

3. Let
x,y\in X
. It suffices to show that for any path
\gamma\colon[a,b]\rightarrow X
from
x
to
y
, we have
\ell(\gamma)\geq d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))
. Notice that
f_{\ast}(\gamma)\colon[a,b]\rightarrow Y
is a path on
Y
of length
\ell(f_{\ast}(\gamma))=\ell(\gamma)
. Hence
\ell(\gamma)=\ell(f_{\ast}(\gamma))\geq d_{Y}(f(x),f(y))
as desired.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
X
be a metric space and
Y
a locally compact path metric space.
Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a uniformly continuous map. Assume that
f
is proper and that
Y
is complete. Then
X
is complete.

Proof.

This is well-known; we provide a proof for convenience of the reader. Let
\left\{x_{n}\right\}_{n}
be a Cauchy sequence in
X
. We must show that
\left\{x_{n}\right\}_{n}
has a limit
x\in X
.
Since
f
is uniformly continuous,
\left\{f(x_{n})\right\}_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence in
Y
. In particular, for some
N>0
, the elements
f(x_{n})
for
n\geq N
are all contained in the closed ball
\overline{B}_{\epsilon}(x_{N})=\left\{y\in Y\mid d(y,x_{N})\leq\epsilon\right\}\subset Y
of radius
\epsilon
for some
\epsilon>0
(where
d
is the metric on
Y
). Since
Y
is a complete locally compact path metric space, the closed balls in
Y
are compact (Hopf–Rinow theorem, see e.g.
[
Gro07
, Section 1.9]
). Thus, replacing
\left\{x_{n}\right\}_{n}
by
\left\{x_{n}\right\}_{n\geq N}
, we may assume that
\left\{f(x_{n})\right\}_{n}
is contained in a compact subset
K\subset Y
. Since
f
is proper, the subset
f^{-1}(K)\subset X
is compact. Therefore, each
x_{n}\in X
is contained in the compact subset
f^{-1}(K)\subset X
, hence the sequence
\left\{x_{n}\right\}_{n}
of points
x_{n}\in f^{-1}(K)
converges to some point
x\in f^{-1}(K)
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
X
be a path-connected topological space equipped with a metric
d_{X}
, and let
\Gamma
be a group that acts isometrically on
X
with closed orbits. Define a pseudometric
d
on the quotient space
Y=\Gamma\setminus X
as follows: for orbits
[x_{1}],[x_{2}]\in Y
of points
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
, put
d([x_{1}],[x_{2}])=\inf_{\gamma\in\Gamma}d_{X}(x_{1},\gamma x_{2})
. The following holds.

1.

The pseudometric
d
is a metric, and if
d_{X}
is a path metric, then so is
d
.

2.

If the metric space
X
is complete, then so is
Y
.

Proof.

For the first assertion, see
[
Gro07
, Section 1.16]
. For the second assertion, assume that
X
is complete. Let
\left\{[x_{n}]\right\}_{n}
be a Cauchy sequence in
Y
. We aim to show that
\left\{[x_{n}]\right\}_{n}
has a limit in
Y
. Up to replacing
\left\{[x_{n}]\right\}_{n}
by a subsequence
\left\{[x_{n_{i}}]\right\}_{i}
that satisfies
d([x_{n_{i}}],[x_{n_{i+1}}])\leq 2^{-i}
, we may assume that
d([x_{n}],[x_{n+1}])\leq 2^{-n}
. Let
p_{1}\in[x_{1}]
such that
d_{X}(p_{1},[x_{2}])=d([x_{1}],[x_{2}])\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Recursively choose
p_{n}\in[x_{n}]
for
n\geq 2
such that
d_{X}(p_{n-1},p_{n})\leq d_{X}(p_{n-1},[x_{n}])+2^{-(n-1)}
. This gives

\displaystyle d_{X}(p_{n-1},p_{n})

\displaystyle\leq d_{X}(p_{n-1},[x_{n}])+2^{-(n-1)}

\displaystyle=d_{X}([x_{n-1}],[x_{n}])+2^{-(n-1)}\leq 2^{-(n-1)}+2^{-(n-1)}=2^{-(n-2)}

for each
n\geq 2
. Hence our sequence
\left\{p_{n}\right\}_{n}
of elements in
X
is Cauchy; since
X
is complete, it has a limit
p\in X
. The orbit
[p]\in Y
of
p\in X
is the limit of the sequence
\left\{[x_{n}]\right\}_{n}
, and we are done.
∎

5.2

Path metric of the attachment

Let

p\colon{\widetilde{Y}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})={\widetilde{Y}}/\sim

be the projection map, and let

{\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}}\colon{\widetilde{Y}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}

be the map defined as
{\widetilde{P}}(x,\alpha)=x
.
By Lemma
4.6
, we obtain continuous maps

{\mathscr{P}}\colon Y({\mathscr{L}})\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}\quad{\textnormal{ and }}\quad\overline{{\mathscr{P}}}\colon M({\mathscr{L}})=L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}}){\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{L}}\setminus\mathbb{C}H^{n}

such that the following diagram commutes:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 27.90973pt\raise-58.02223pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{{\mathscr{P}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.40973pt\raise-63.86111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 51.40973pt\raise-63.86111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{L\setminus\mathbb{C}H^{n}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(27)

Lemma 5.6
.

For each compact subset
Z\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
there are only finitely many
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
with
Z\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

The subgroup
L^{\prime}\subset\textnormal{Isom}(\mathbb{C}H^{n})
(see (
16
) and Lemma
3.3
) acts properly discontinuously on
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
. So if
S
is the set of
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
such that
\alpha Z\cap Z\neq\emptyset
, then
S
is finite. Moreover, if
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
is such that
Z\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\neq\emptyset
, then there exists
z\in Z
with
\alpha(z)=z
, so that
\alpha\in S
.
In particular, there are only finitely many such
\alpha
.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

The map
\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}\colon\widetilde{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is proper with finite fibres. In particular, the map
\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}
is closed.

Proof.

Let
Z\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
be a compact set.
Then
Z
meets only finitely many of the spaces
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
, see Lemma
5.6
. Each
Z\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
is closed in
Z
as
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
is closed in
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, so
Z\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
is compact for each
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
. We conclude that
{\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}}^{-1}(Z)=\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}(Z\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha})
is compact.
In particular,
\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}
is proper. Let
z\in{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
. Applying Lemma
5.6
to the case where
Z=\left\{z\right\}\subset{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
is a singleton shows that
z\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha}
for only finitely many
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
. Since
{\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}}^{-1}(z)=\{(x,\alpha)\in{\widetilde{Y}}\mid x=z\}
, this proves that
{\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}}^{-1}(z)
is finite. As
{\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}}
is proper, it is closed by Lemma
5.2
.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

The map
{\mathscr{P}}\colon Y({\mathscr{L}})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is proper with finite fibers, hence in particular,
{\mathscr{P}}
is closed. Moreover, the map
\overline{{\mathscr{P}}}\colon M({\mathscr{L}})\rightarrow L\setminus\mathbb{C}H^{n}
has finite fibres.

Proof.

Consider diagram (
27
).
Since
p\colon{\widetilde{Y}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
is surjective and
{\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}}
is proper with finite fibres, the same holds for
{\mathscr{P}}
, and hence
{\mathscr{P}}
is closed by Lemma
5.2
.

Next, we show that the induced map
\overline{{\mathscr{P}}}\colon M({\mathscr{L}})\rightarrow L\backslash\mathbb{C}H^{n}
has finite fibres. To see this, let
x\in{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
with image
[x]\in L\setminus{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
. Let
\pi\colon{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}\rightarrow L\setminus{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
be the quotient map. Then
\pi^{-1}([x])=L\cdot x=\left\{\gamma\cdot x\right\}_{\gamma\in L}
and hence
{\mathscr{P}}^{-1}(\pi^{-1}([x]))=\cup_{\gamma\in L}(\gamma\cdot{\mathscr{P}}^{-1}(x))
. Let
q\colon Y({\mathscr{L}})\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})
be the quotient map. Then
\overline{{\mathscr{P}}}^{-1}([x])=q(q^{-1}(\overline{{\mathscr{P}}}^{-1}([x])))=q\left({\mathscr{P}}^{-1}(\pi^{-1}([x]))\right)=q\left(\cup_{\gamma}\gamma\cdot{\mathscr{P}}^{-1}(x)\right)=q({\mathscr{P}}^{-1}(x))
, and this is a finite set because the map
{\mathscr{P}}\colon Y({\mathscr{L}})\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
has finite fibres.
∎

Our next goal is to prove that each point
x\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
has a neighbourhood
V\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
that maps homeomorphically onto a finite union
\cup_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
. Hence
x
has an open neighourhood
x\in U\subset V
that identifies with an open set in a union of copies of
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
in
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
under the map
{\mathscr{P}}
. This allows us to define a metric on
Y({\mathscr{L}})
by pulling back the metric on
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, so that
Y({\mathscr{L}})
is piecewise isometric to
{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}
.

Fix a point
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
and a point
(x,\alpha)\in\widetilde{Y}
such that
p(x,\alpha)=f
. Let
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{\ell}
be the elements in
P{\mathscr{A}}
such that
(x,\alpha_{i})\sim(x,\alpha)
for each
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,\ell\right\}
(since the group
G(x)
is finite by Lemma
2.10
, these are finite in number). Consider the quotient map

p\colon\widetilde{Y}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})

and define

Y_{f}\coloneqq p\left(\coprod_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\right)\subset Y({\mathscr{L}}).

(28)

Lemma 5.9
.

The following assertions are true.

1.

The stabilizer
L_{f}
of
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
acts on the set
\left\{\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{\ell}\right\}
by conjugation.

2.

The stabilizer
L_{f}
of
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
preserves the subset
Y_{f}\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
.

Proof.

Let
\psi\in L_{f}
and
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,\ell\right\}
. Notice that
\psi\cdot f=f
implies that
\psi\cdot(x,\alpha_{i})\sim(x,\alpha)
hence
\psi\alpha_{i}\psi^{-1}=\alpha_{j}
for some
j\in\left\{1,\dotsc,\ell\right\}
. This proves the first assertion.

To prove the second assertion, let
g=p(y,\alpha_{i})\in Y_{f}
for some
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,\ell\right\}
and
y\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}
. Then
\psi\cdot p(y,\alpha_{i})=p(\psi(y),\psi\alpha_{i}\psi^{-1})=p(\psi(y),\alpha_{j})
for some
j\in\left\{1,\dotsc,\ell\right\}
by the above. Hence
\psi\cdot p(y,\alpha_{i})\in Y_{f}
, proving what we want.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Consider the quotient map
p\colon{\widetilde{Y}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
.
Let
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
and let
(x,\alpha)\in\widetilde{Y}
such that
p(x,\alpha)=f
. Let
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{\ell}
be the elements in
P{\mathscr{A}}
such that
(x,\alpha_{i})\sim(x,\alpha)
for each
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,\ell\right\}
. The following assertions are true.

1.

The set
Y_{f}
defined in (
28
) is closed in
Y({\mathscr{L}})
.

2.

We have
{\mathscr{P}}\left(Y_{f}\right)=\cup_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, and the map

{\mathscr{P}}_{f}\colon Y_{f}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}

(29)

induced by
{\mathscr{P}}
is a homeomorphism.

3.

The set
Y_{f}\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
contains an
L_{f}
-equivariant open neighbourhood
U_{f}
of
f
in
Y({\mathscr{L}})
, where
L_{f}\subset L
is the stabilizer of
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
, such that the natural map

L_{f}\setminus U_{f}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})=M({\mathscr{L}})

is injective.

Proof.

1. This follows from the proof of Lemma
4.7
.
1
.

2. We have

{\mathscr{P}}_{f}(Y_{f})={\mathscr{P}}\left(p\left(\coprod_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\right)\right)=\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}\left(\coprod_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\right)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}.

To prove injectivity of (
29
), let
(y,\alpha_{i}),(z,\alpha_{j})\in\widetilde{Y}
and suppose that
y=z\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
. Then indeed,
(y,\alpha_{i})\sim(z,\alpha_{j})
because
\sim
is an equivalence relation by Lemma
4.3
.

Finally, we prove that
{\mathscr{P}}_{f}
is closed. Let
Z\subset Y_{f}
be a closed set. Then
Z
is closed in
Y
by Part 1, hence
p^{-1}(Z)
is closed in
\widetilde{Y}
, hence
\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}\left(p^{-1}(Z)\right))
is closed in
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, so that

{\mathscr{P}}_{f}(Z)={\mathscr{P}}(Z)=\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}\left(p^{-1}\left(Z\right)\right)=\left(\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}\left(p^{-1}\left(Z\right)\right)\right)\cap\left(\cup_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\right)

is closed in
\cup_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}
.

3. Let
x={\mathscr{P}}(f)\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
. Since
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is locally compact, there exists a compact set
Z\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
and an open set
U\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
with
x\in U\subset Z
. Since
Z
is compact, it meets only finitely many of the
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
(Lemma
5.6
). Consequently, the same holds for
U
; define
V={\mathscr{P}}^{-1}(U)\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
and put

{\mathscr{B}}=\{\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}:U\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}\neq\emptyset\}.

Also define, for
\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}
,
Z_{\beta}\coloneqq p\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\beta}\right)\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
. Then

f\in V\subset\bigcup_{\beta\in{\mathscr{B}}}Z_{\beta}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in{\mathscr{B}}\\
\beta(x)=x\end{subarray}}Z_{\beta}\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in{\mathscr{B}}\\
\beta(x)\neq x\end{subarray}}Z_{\beta}.

Since each
Z_{\beta}
is closed in
Y({\mathscr{L}})
by the proof of Lemma
4.7
.
1
, there is an open subset
V^{\prime}\subset V
with

f\in V^{\prime}\subset\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in{\mathscr{B}}\\
\beta(x)=x\end{subarray}}Z_{\beta}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in{\mathscr{B}}\\
\beta(x)=x\\
(x,\beta)\sim(x,\alpha)\end{subarray}}Z_{\beta}\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in{\mathscr{B}}\\
\beta(x)=x\\
(x,\beta)\not\sim(x,\alpha)\end{subarray}}Z_{\beta}.

Since each
Z_{\beta}
is closed in
Y({\mathscr{L}})
, there exists an open subset
V^{\prime\prime}\subset V^{\prime}
with

f\in V^{\prime\prime}\subset\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in{\mathscr{B}}\\
\beta(x)=x\\
(x,\beta)\sim(x,\alpha)\end{subarray}}Z_{\beta}\subset\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}\\
\beta(x)=x\\
(x,\beta)\sim(x,\alpha)\end{subarray}}Z_{\beta}=Y_{f}.

We have
L_{f}\cdot Y_{f}=Y_{f}
by Lemma
5.9
, and the subset
U_{f}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq L_{f}\cdot V^{\prime\prime}\subset L_{f}\cdot Y_{f}=Y_{f}
is an
L_{f}
-equivariant open neighbourhood of
f
in
Y({\mathscr{L}})
.

We define
U_{f}\subset U_{f}^{\prime\prime}
as follows. Let
x\in U_{x}\subset{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
be an
L_{x}
-equivariant open neighbourhood such that
L_{x}\setminus U_{x}\subset L\setminus{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
. Then
U_{x}\cap{\mathscr{P}}(Y_{f})
is open in
{\mathscr{P}}(Y_{f})
. We define
V_{x}\subset Y_{f}
as the inverse image of
U_{x}\cap{\mathscr{P}}(Y_{f})
under the homeomorphism
Y_{f}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathscr{P}}(Y_{f})
, and put

U_{f}\coloneqq U_{f}^{\prime\prime}\cap V_{x}.

Then
V_{x}
is open in
Y_{f}
, and hence
U_{f}=U_{f}^{\prime\prime}\cap V_{x}
is open in
U_{f}^{\prime\prime}\subset Y_{f}
, and therefore in
Y({\mathscr{L}})
. Moreover, since
U_{x}
is
L_{x}
-equivariant and
L_{f}\subset L_{x}
, we see that
V_{x}
is
L_{f}
-equivariant, and hence
U_{f}
is
L_{f}
-equivariant as well.

We claim that the natural map

L_{f}\setminus U_{f}\rightarrow L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})

is injective. To prove this, let
(y,\alpha_{i}),(z,\alpha_{j})\in{\widetilde{Y}}
such that
p(y,\alpha_{i}),p(z,\alpha_{j})\in U_{f}\subset Y_{f}
, and let
\psi\in L
such that
\psi\cdot(y,\alpha_{i})=(z,\alpha_{j})
. It suffices to show that
\psi\in L_{f}
. Observe that
(\psi(y),\psi\alpha_{i}\psi^{-1})\sim(z,\alpha_{j})
. In particular,
\psi(y)=z
, and since
y,z\in U_{x}
and the map
L_{x}\setminus U_{x}\rightarrow L\setminus{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
is injective, we get that
\psi\in L_{x}
. Consequently, we have
x,z\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\psi\alpha_{i}\psi^{-1}}\cap{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{j}}
,
and the fact that
(z,\psi\alpha_{i}\psi^{-1})\sim(z,\alpha_{j})
implies, in view of Lemma
4.6
, that
(x,\psi\alpha_{i}\psi^{-1})\sim(x,\alpha_{j})
. But then
\psi\alpha_{i}\psi^{-1}=\alpha_{k}
for some
k\in\left\{1,\dotsc,\ell\right\}
, which implies that
\psi\in L_{f}
, and we are done.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

The topological space
Y({\mathscr{L}})
is Hausdorff.

Proof.

Let
f,f^{\prime}\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
be elements such that
f\neq f^{\prime}
. First suppose that
f\not\in Y_{f^{\prime}}
. Since
Y_{f^{\prime}}
is closed in
Y({\mathscr{L}})
by Lemma
5.10
, there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
f
such that
U\cap Y_{f^{\prime}}=\emptyset
. Thus,
U\cap U_{f^{\prime}}\subset U\cap Y_{f^{\prime}}=\emptyset
, where
U_{f^{\prime}}\subset Y_{f^{\prime}}
is the open neighbourhood of
f^{\prime}
constructed in item 3 of Lemma
5.10
.

Next, suppose that
f\in Y_{f^{\prime}}
. Lift
f
and
f^{\prime}
to elements
(x,\alpha),(y,\beta)\in\widetilde{Y}
. Assume first that
x=y
. This means that
{\mathscr{P}}(f)={\mathscr{P}}(f^{\prime})
. Since
{\mathscr{P}}:Y_{f^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is injective, this implies that
f=f^{\prime}
, contradiction. So we have
x\neq y\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
. But
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is Hausdorff, so there are open subsets
\left(U\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n},V\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}\right)
such that
x\in U
,
y\in V
and
U\cap V=\emptyset
. Then
{\mathscr{P}}^{-1}(U)\cap{\mathscr{P}}^{-1}(V)=\emptyset
and we are done.
∎

Definition 5.12
.

Define a path metric
d_{i}
on each path-connected component
Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
of
Y({\mathscr{L}})
by pulling back the metric on
{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
via the map
{\mathscr{P}}\colon Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
as in Lemma
5.3
; note that
{\mathscr{P}}
is a local topological embedding by Lemma
5.10
. For each path-connected component
Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
, the map
{\mathscr{P}}\colon Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
is a Lipschitz continuous local isometric embedding, see Lemma
5.3
.

Proposition 5.13
.

Consider the path metrics
d_{i}
on the path-connected components of
Y({\mathscr{L}})
, see Definition
5.12
.
Let
M({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
be a path-connected component of
M({\mathscr{L}})
, and let
Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
be a path-connected component of
Y({\mathscr{L}})
that surjects onto
M({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
(so that
M({\mathscr{L}})_{i}=L_{i}\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
, where
L_{i}\subset L
denotes the stabilizer of
Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
in
L
). For orbits
[x_{1}],[x_{2}]\in M({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
of points
x_{1},x_{2}\in Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
, define
d_{i}([x_{1}],[x_{2}])=\inf_{\gamma\in L}(d_{i}(x_{1},\gamma x_{2}))
. Then this defines a path metric on
M({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.5
, it suffices to show that
L
acts by isometries on
Y({\mathscr{L}})
with closed orbits. To prove this, we first claim that
L
acts isometrically on
Y({\mathscr{L}})
. For a curve
\gamma
on
Y({\mathscr{L}})
or on
{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
, let
\ell(\gamma)
denote its length. By construction (cf. Lemma
5.3
and Definition
5.12
), we have
\ell(\gamma)=\ell({\mathscr{P}}\circ\gamma)
for each
\gamma\colon[a,b]\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
. Let
x,y\in Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
; it suffices to show that for each curve
\gamma\colon[a,b]\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
with
\gamma(a)=x
and
\gamma(b)=y
, the length of
\gamma
equals the length of
g_{\ast}(\gamma)=g\circ\gamma
. This holds indeed, for the
L
-equivariance of the map
{\mathscr{P}}
(cf. Lemma
4.6
) implies that:

\ell(g_{\ast}(\gamma))=\ell({\mathscr{P}}\circ g\circ\gamma)=\ell(g_{\ast}({\mathscr{P}}\circ\gamma))=\ell({\mathscr{P}}\circ\gamma)=\ell(\gamma).

Next, we check that the
L
-orbits are closed in
Y({\mathscr{L}})
. Let
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
with representative
(x,\alpha)\in\widetilde{Y}
. By equivariance of
p\colon\widetilde{Y}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
, we have
p^{-1}\left(L\cdot f\right)=L\cdot\left(p^{-1}f\right)
, and since
p
is a quotient map, it therefore suffices to show that
L\cdot\left(p^{-1}f\right)
is closed in
{\widetilde{Y}}
. Moreover,
p^{-1}(f)=\{(x,\beta)\in{\widetilde{Y}}\colon(x,\beta)\sim(x,\alpha)\}
is a finite set, so it suffices to show that the orbit
L\cdot(x,\beta)
is closed in
\widetilde{Y}
for each
\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}
. Since
L
is discrete, it suffices to show that
L
acts properly on
\widetilde{Y}
. So let
Z\subset\widetilde{Y}
be any compact set: we claim that
\{g\in L:gZ\cap Z\neq\emptyset\}
is a finite set. Indeed, for each
g\in L
, one has
\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}\left(gZ\cap Z\right)\subset g\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}(Z)\cap\widetilde{{\mathscr{P}}}(Z)
, and the latter is non-empty for only finitely many
g\in L
, by properness of the action of
L
on
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
.

Since the metric on
Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
is a path metric, the same holds for the metric on
M({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
, see Lemma
5.5
. This proves the proposition.
∎

Proposition 5.14
.

The path metrics on the path-connected components of
Y({\mathscr{L}})
and
M({\mathscr{L}})
defined in Definition
5.12
and Proposition
5.13
are complete.

Proof.

Let
Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
be a path-connected component with image
M({\mathscr{L}})_{i}\subset M({\mathscr{L}})
.
Consider the map
{\mathscr{P}}\colon Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
. By Lemma
5.3
, the map
{\mathscr{P}}
is Lipschitz continuous, and in particular uniformly continuous. Moreover, by Corollary
5.8
, the map
{\mathscr{P}}
is proper. Consequently, the metric space
Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
is complete because
{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
is complete, see Lemma
5.4
. By Lemma
5.5
, the metric space
M({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
is therefore complete as well, and we are done.
∎

5.3

Orbifold structure of the attachment

The next step is to prove that the space
M({\mathscr{L}})=L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})
(see Definition
4.5
) is locally isometric to quotients of open sets in
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
by finite groups of isometries.

Definition 5.15
.

Let
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
with representative
(x,\alpha)\in\widetilde{Y}
. Thus,
x
is an element in
{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
, and
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
is the class of an anti-unitary involution such that
\alpha(x)=x
.

1.

The
nodes
of
f
are by definition the nodes of
(x,\alpha)
(see Definition
2.8
). Thus, these are the hyperplanes
H\in{\mathcal{H}}(x)
, i.e. the hyperplanes
H_{r}\in{\mathcal{H}}
defined by short roots
r\in{\mathscr{R}}
such that
x\in H_{r}
(equivalently, such that
h(x,r)=0
).

2.

The number of nodes of
f
is the cardinality of
{\mathcal{H}}(x)
.

3.

The anti-unitary involution
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
induces an involution on the set
{\mathcal{H}}(x)
by Lemma
3.9
.
Let
H\in{\mathcal{H}}(x)
be a node of
x
. We call
H
a
real node
of
x
if
\alpha(H)=H
. We call
(H,\alpha(H))
a
pair of complex conjugate nodes
of
x
if
\alpha(H)\neq H
.

4.

If
k
is the number of nodes of
f
, we generally write
k=2a+b
, with
a
the number of pairs of complex conjugate nodes of
x
, and
b
the number of real nodes of
x
. We say that
f

has
k
nodes
(resp.
a
pairs of complex conjugate nodes and
b
real nodes
) if
x
has
k
nodes (resp.
a
pairs of complex conjugate nodes and
b
real nodes).

Fix again a point
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
and a point
(x,\alpha)\in\widetilde{Y}
lying above
f
. Let
k=2a+b
be the number of nodes of
f
. Thus
x\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha}
, and there exist
r_{1},\dotsc,r_{k}\in{\mathscr{R}}
such that

{\mathcal{H}}(x)=\left\{H_{r_{1}},\dotsc,H_{r_{k}}\right\},\quad G(x)=\langle\phi_{r_{1}},\dotsc,\phi_{r_{\ell}}\rangle\cong(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{k}.

For
\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}
, recall that
(x,\beta)\sim(x,\alpha)
if and only if
\alpha\circ\beta\in G(x)
. We relabel the
r_{i}
so that they satisfy the following condition:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha(H_{r_{i}})=\begin{cases}H_{r_{i+1}}{\textnormal{ for }}i{\textnormal{ odd and }}i\leq 2a,\\
H_{r_{i-1}}{\textnormal{ for }}i{\textnormal{ even and }}i\leq 2a,{\textnormal{ and }}\\
H_{r_{i}}{\textnormal{ for }}i\in\left\{2a+1,\dotsc,k\right\}.\end{cases}\end{split}

(30)

In other words,
H_{r_{i}}
is a real node if and only if
i>2a
, and
\left(H_{r_{i}},H_{r_{i+1}}\right)
is a pair of complex conjugate nodes if and only if
i<2a
is odd.

Lemma 5.16
.

Continue with the notation from above.

1.

Let
\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}
be such that
(x,\beta)\sim(x,\alpha)
. Then

\beta=\prod_{i=1}^{a}\left(\phi_{r_{2i-1}}\circ\phi_{r_{2i}}\right)^{j_{i}}\circ\prod_{i=2a+1}^{k}\phi_{r_{i}}^{j_{i}}\circ\alpha

for some
j_{1},\dotsc,j_{a},j_{2a+1},\dotsc,j_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}/m
. In particular, there are
m^{a+b}
such
\beta
.

2.

There is an isometry
\mathbb{C}H^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
identifying
x
with the origin,
\phi_{r_{i}}
with the map

{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}),\;\;\;(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{i},\dotsc,t_{n})\mapstochar\rightarrow(t_{1},\dotsc,\zeta t_{i},\dotsc,t_{n}),

and
\alpha
with the map defined by

t_{i}\mapstochar\rightarrow\begin{cases}\bar{t}_{i+1}\;\;\;\textnormal{for $i$ odd and $i\leq 2a$}\\
\bar{t}_{i-1}\;\;\;\textnormal{for $i$ even and $i\leq 2a$}\\
\bar{t}_{i}\;\;\;\;\;\;\textnormal{for $i>2a$}.\end{cases}

(31)

Proof.

1. This follows readily from Proposition
3.11
.

2.
Define

T=\langle x\rangle\oplus\langle r_{1}\rangle\oplus\cdots\oplus\langle r_{k}\rangle\subset V,\quad W=T^{\perp}=\{w\in V\mid h(w,t)=0\;\;\forall t\in T\}.

Observe that
\alpha(W)=W
. Since
W\subset\langle x\rangle^{\perp}
, the hermitian space
(W,h|_{W})
is positive definite. Let
\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{n-k}\}\subset W
be an orthonormal basis such that
\alpha(w_{i})=w_{i}
(such a basis exists because
h|_{W}
restricts to a positive definite symmetric bilinear form on
W^{\alpha}
that in turn induces a positive definite hermitian form on
W^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
such that the canonical isomorphism
W^{\alpha}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}=W
preserves the hermitian forms). By equation (
30
) and Lemma
3.9
, there exist
\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{k}\in K^{\ast}
with
\left|\lambda_{i}\right|=1
, so that:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha(r_{i})=\begin{cases}\lambda_{i}\cdot r_{i+1}{\textnormal{ for }}i{\textnormal{ odd and }}i\leq 2a,\\
\lambda_{i}\cdot r_{i-1}{\textnormal{ for }}i{\textnormal{ even and }}i\leq 2a,{\textnormal{ and }}\\
\lambda_{i}\cdot r_{i}{\textnormal{ for }}i\in\left\{2a+1,\dotsc,k\right\}.\end{cases}\end{split}

(32)

Let
\rho_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
such that
\lambda_{i}=\rho_{i}^{2}
and
\left|\rho_{i}\right|=1
.
We consider the following basis of
V
:

\displaystyle V=\langle x^{\prime},r_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,r_{k}^{\prime},w_{1},\dotsc,w_{n-k}\rangle,\quad\quad r_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\rho_{i}\cdot r_{i},\quad x^{\prime}\coloneqq\frac{x}{\sqrt{-h(x,x)}}.

(33)

We have
\alpha(r_{i}^{\prime})=r_{\alpha(i)}^{\prime}
. Moreover,
h(x^{\prime},x^{\prime})=(-h(x,x))^{-1}\cdot h(x,x)=-1
and
h(r_{i}^{\prime},r_{i}^{\prime})=\left|\rho_{i}\right|^{2}\cdot h(r_{i},r_{i})=1
. Thus, the basis (
33
) induces an isomorphism of hermitian vector spaces

\displaystyle(V,h)\xlongrightarrow{\sim}(\mathbb{C}^{n+1},H),

(34)

where
H(x,y)=-x_{0}\bar{y}_{0}+x_{1}\bar{y}_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{y}_{n}
, and the isometry
{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
induced by (
34
) has all the required properties.
∎

Definition 5.17
.

1.

Define
L_{f}=\textnormal{Stab}_{L}(f)
to be the subgroup of
L
fixing
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
. This contains the group
G(x)\cong(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{k}
.

2.

Define
B_{f}
as the subgroup of
G(x)
generated by the order
m
complex reflections associated to the real nodes of
f
. Hence
B_{f}=\langle\phi_{r_{i}}\rangle_{i>2a}\cong(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{b}
.

We also need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.18
.

Write
m=2^{e}\cdot d
with
d\not\equiv 0\bmod 2
.
Let
T\coloneqq\{t\in\mathbb{C}:t^{m}\in\mathbb{R}\}
. Let
\langle\zeta_{m}\rangle
act on
T
by multiplication. Then each element in
\langle\zeta_{m}\rangle\setminus T
has a unique representative of the form
\zeta_{2^{e+1}}^{\epsilon}\cdot r\in T
with
r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

Let
T_{1}=\left\{t\in T\colon\left|t\right|=1\right\}
. It suffices to show that each element in
\langle\zeta_{m}\rangle\setminus T_{1}
has a unique representative of the form
\zeta_{2^{e+1}}^{\epsilon}
for
\epsilon\in\left\{0,1\right\}
. Now notice that
T_{1}=\langle\zeta_{2m}\rangle
and that
\zeta_{2m}=\zeta_{2^{e+1}d}
. In particular, we have an isomorphism

\displaystyle\langle\zeta_{2m}\rangle/\langle\zeta_{m}\rangle=\langle\zeta_{2^{e+1}}\rangle/\langle\zeta_{2^{e}}\rangle

\displaystyle\xlongrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{Z}/2,

\displaystyle\zeta_{2^{e+1}}

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow 1,

and the result follows.
∎

We obtain the key to Theorem
5.1
. Consider the quotient map
p\colon\widetilde{Y}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
, and the subset
Y_{f}\subset M({\mathscr{L}})
, see (
28
).

Proposition 5.19
.

Consider the above notation; in particular, we consider
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
and a point
(x,\alpha)\in\widetilde{Y}
lying above
f
.
The following assertions are true.

1.

If
f
has no nodes, then
G(x)=B_{f}
is trivial, and
Y_{f}=\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\cong{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
.

2.

If
f
has only real nodes, then
B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
is isometric to
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
.

3.

If
f
has
a
pairs of complex conjugate nodes (
k=2a
), and no other nodes, then
B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}=Y_{f}
is the union of
m^{a}
copies of
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
, any two of which meet along a totally geodesic
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{2c}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
for some integer
c
with
0\leq c\leq a
.

4.

More generally, if
f
has
2a
complex conjugate nodes and
b
real nodes, then there is an isometry between
B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
and the union of
m^{a}
copies of
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
identified along common
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{2c}(\mathbb{R})^{\prime}s
(that is, the set
Y_{f}
of case
3
above) such that
[f]\in B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
is identified with the origin in any of the copies of
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
.

5.

Assume
f
has
2a
complex conjugate nodes and
b
real nodes, and consider the above isometry between
B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
and the union
\cup_{i}B_{i}
of copies
B_{i}
of
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
L_{f}
acts transitively on these copies of
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
(i.e., for each
i\neq j
and each
p\in B_{j}
there exists
\phi\in L_{f}
such that
\phi\cdot p\in B_{i}
).
If
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})=B_{i}
is any one of them, and if
\Gamma_{f}=(L_{f}/B_{f})_{{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})}
is its stabilizer in the group
L_{f}/B_{f}
, then the natural map

\Gamma_{f}\setminus{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\left(L_{f}/B_{f}\right)\setminus\left(B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}\right)=L_{f}\setminus Y_{f}

is an isometry of path metrics. Moreover, the action of
\Gamma_{f}
on
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
fixes
0\in\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
, and
0
is sent to
[f]\in L_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
under the induced map
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow L_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
.

Proof.

1. This is clear.

2. Suppose next that
f
has
k
real nodes, with
1\leq k\leq n
. Then in the local coordinates
t_{i}
of Lemma
5.16
.
2
, we have that
\alpha:{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
is defined by
\alpha(t_{i})=\bar{t}_{i}
. Part
1
of the same lemma shows that any
\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}
fixing
x
such that
(x,\alpha)\sim(x,\beta)
is of the form

{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}),\;\;\;(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{i},\dotsc,t_{n})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\bar{t}_{1}\zeta^{j_{1}},\dotsc,\bar{t}_{k}\zeta^{j_{k}},\bar{t}_{k+1},\dotsc,\bar{t}_{n}).

Since
f
has
k
real nodes and no complex conjugate nodes, we have (writing
j=(j_{1},\dotsc,j_{k})
and
\alpha_{j}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\phi_{r_{i}}^{j_{i}}\circ\alpha
):

Y_{f}\cong\bigcup_{j=(j_{1},\dotsc,j_{k})\in(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{k}}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{j}}\cong\left\{(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}):t_{1}^{m},\dotsc,t_{k}^{m},t_{k+1},\dotsc,t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

As before, write
m=2^{e}\cdot d
with
d
odd. Each of the
2^{k}
subsets

\displaystyle K_{f,\epsilon_{1},\dotsc,\epsilon_{k}}\coloneqq\left\{(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}):\zeta_{2^{e+1}}^{-\epsilon_{1}}t_{1},\dotsc,\zeta_{2^{e+1}}^{-\epsilon_{k}}t_{k}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\textnormal{ and }t_{k+1},\dotsc,t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\right\},

indexed by
\epsilon_{1},\dotsc,\epsilon_{k}\in\{0,1\}
, is isometric to the closed region in
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
bounded by
k
mutually orthogonal hyperplanes. By Lemma
5.18
, their union
U
is a fundamental domain for
B_{f}
, in the sense that it maps homeomorphically and piecewise-isometrically onto
B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
. Under its path metric,
U=\cup K_{f,\epsilon_{1},\dotsc,\epsilon_{k}}
is isometric to
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
by the following map:

U\rightarrow{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R}),\;\;\;(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{k})\mapstochar\rightarrow\left((-\zeta_{2^{e+1}})^{-\epsilon_{1}}t_{1},\dotsc,(-\zeta_{2^{e+1}})^{-\epsilon_{k}}t_{k},t_{k+1},\dotsc,t_{n}\right).

This identifies
B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
with the standard
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\subset{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

3. Now suppose
f
has
a
pairs of complex conjugate nodes

H_{r_{1}},\dotsc,H_{r_{2a}}
such that (
30
) holds,
where
1\leq a\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor
. There are now
m^{a}
anti-isometric involutions
\alpha_{j}
fixing
x
and such that
(x,\alpha_{j})\sim(x,\alpha)
: they are given in the coordinates
t_{i}
as follows, taking
j=(j_{1},\dotsc,j_{a})\in(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{a}
:

\alpha_{j}\colon(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\bar{t}_{2}\zeta^{j_{1}},\bar{t}_{1}\zeta^{j_{1}},\dotsc,\bar{t}_{2a}\zeta^{j_{a}},\bar{t}_{2a-1}\zeta^{j_{a}},\bar{t}_{2a+1},\dotsc,\bar{t}_{n}).

(35)

Thus, any fixed-point set
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{j}}
is identified with the copy
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}}
of
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
defined as

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}}=\left\{(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\colon t_{i}=\bar{t}_{i-1}\zeta^{j_{i}}\textnormal{ for $i\leq 2a$ even, }t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\textnormal{ for $i>2a$}\right\},

(36)

and we have

Y_{f}\cong\bigcup_{j}\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}}=\left\{(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\colon t_{i}^{m}=\bar{t}_{i-1}^{m}\textnormal{if $i\leq 2a$ even, }t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\textnormal{ if }i>2a\right\}.

These
m^{a}
copies of
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
meet at the origin of
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
; in fact, for
j\neq j^{\prime}
, the space
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}}
meets
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}
in a
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{2c}(\mathbb{R})
if
c
is the number of pairs
(j_{i},j^{\prime}_{i})
with
j_{i}=j^{\prime}_{i}
.

4. We proceed to treat the general case: we assume that
f
has
k=2a+b>0
nodes, among which
a
pairs of complex conjugate nodes and
b
real nodes. In the local coordinates
t_{i}
of Lemma
5.16
.
2
, any anti-unitary involution fixing
x
equivalent to
\alpha
is of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{j}\colon&(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\mapstochar\rightarrow\\
&(\bar{t}_{2}\zeta^{j_{1}},\bar{t}_{1}\zeta^{j_{1}},\dotsc,\bar{t}_{2a}\zeta^{j_{a}},\bar{t}_{2a-1}\zeta^{j_{a}},\bar{t}_{2a+1}\zeta^{j_{2a+1}},\dotsc,\bar{t}_{k}\zeta^{j_{k}},\bar{t}_{k+1},\dotsc,\bar{t}_{n}),\end{split}

where

j=(j_{1},\dotsc,j_{a},j_{2a+1},\dotsc,j_{k})\in(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{a+b}.

We now have
B_{f}\cong(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{b}
acting by multiplying the
t_{i}
for
2a+1\leq i\leq k
by powers of
\zeta
, and there are
m^{a+b}
anti-unitary involutions
\alpha_{j}
. We have

\displaystyle Y_{f}\cong\bigcup_{j\in(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{a+b}}^{m}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{j}}\cong

\displaystyle\left\{(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid t_{2}^{m}=\bar{t}_{1}^{m},\dotsc,t_{2a}^{m}=\bar{t}_{2a-1}^{m},t_{2a+1}^{m},\dotsc,t_{k}^{m},t_{k+1},\dotsc,t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

We look at subsets
K_{f,\epsilon_{1},\dotsc,\epsilon_{k}}\subset Y_{f}
again, this time defined as

\displaystyle K_{f,\epsilon_{1},\dotsc,\epsilon_{k}}=

\displaystyle\left\{t\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid t_{i}^{m}=\bar{t}_{i-1}^{m}\textnormal{ $i\leq 2a$ even, }\zeta_{2^{e+1}}^{-\epsilon_{i}}t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\textnormal{ $2a<i\leq k$, }t_{i}\in\mathbb{R},i>k\right\}.

As before, the natural map

U\coloneqq\bigcup_{\epsilon}K_{f,\epsilon}\rightarrow B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
is an isometry. Define

\widetilde{Y}_{f}=\left\{(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C}):t_{i}^{m}=\bar{t}_{i-1}^{m}\textnormal{ for $i\leq 2a$ even, }t_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\textnormal{ for }i>2a\right\}.

Under its path metric,
U=\cup_{\epsilon}K_{f,\epsilon_{1},\dotsc,\epsilon_{k}}
is isometric to
\widetilde{Y}_{f}

by the following map:

\displaystyle U\rightarrow\widetilde{Y}_{f},\;\;\;

\displaystyle(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{k})\mapstochar\rightarrow

\displaystyle\left(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{2a},(-\zeta_{2^{e+1}})^{-\epsilon_{1}}t_{2a+1},\dotsc,(-\zeta_{2^{e+1}})^{-\epsilon_{k}}t_{k},t_{k+1},\dotsc,t_{n}\right).

Hence
B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}\cong U\cong\widetilde{Y}_{f}
. Since
\widetilde{Y}_{f}
is what
Y_{f}
was in case
3
, we are done.

5. For
j=(j_{1},\dotsc,j_{a})\in(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{a}
, define an involution
\alpha_{j}\colon\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
as in (
35
). Then the above shows that we have an isometry

\displaystyle\begin{split}B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}&\cong\bigcup_{j\in(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{a}}\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}}\\
&=\left\{(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\colon t_{i}^{m}=\bar{t}_{i-1}^{m}\textnormal{if $i\leq 2a$ even, }t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\textnormal{ if }i>2a\right\}.\end{split}

(37)

where
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}}\subset\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
is the copy of
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
defined in (
36
).
The transitivity of the action of
L_{f}
on these copies
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}}
of
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
follows from the fact that
G(x)\subset L_{f}
contains transformations multiplying
t_{1},\dotsc,t_{2a}
by powers of
\zeta
, hence the map
(t_{i}\mapstochar\rightarrow\zeta^{u}t_{i},t_{i-1}\mapstochar\rightarrow t_{i-1})
maps those
(t_{i-1},t_{i})
with
t_{i}=\bar{t}_{i-1}\zeta^{j_{i}}
to those
(t_{i-1},t_{i})
with
t_{i}=\bar{t}_{i-1}\zeta^{j_{1}+u}
.

Let
B
be one of the copies of
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
, and let
\Gamma_{f}
be the stabilizer of
B
in the group
L_{f}/B_{f}
. It remains to prove that the canonical map
\pi\colon\Gamma_{f}\setminus B\rightarrow L_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
is an isometry. The surjectivity of
\pi
follows from the transitivity of
L_{f}
on the
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})^{\prime}s
. As
\pi
is a piecewise isometry, we only need to prove injectivity. This will follow from the following elementary lemma.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let a group
G
act on a set
X
, which is a union
X=\cup_{i\in I}Y_{i}
of subsets
Y_{i}\subset X
. Assume that for each
i\in I
and
g\in G
, we have
gY_{i}=Y_{j}
for some
j\in I
.
Suppose that for all
y\in X
, the stabilizer
\textnormal{Stab}_{G}(y)
of
y
in
G
acts transitively on the sets
Y_{i}
containing
y
, in the sense that for each
i
and
j
such that
y\in Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}
, there exists
g\in{\mathrm{Stab}}_{G}(y)
such that
gY_{i}=Y_{j}
. Let
i_{0}\in I
and let
G_{i_{0}}\subset G
be the stabilizer of
Y_{i_{0}}
. Then the natural map
G_{i_{0}}\setminus Y_{i_{0}}\rightarrow G\setminus X
is injective.

Proof of Lemma
5.20
.

Let
x,y\in Y_{i_{0}}
and
g\in G
such that
g\cdot x=y
. Let
j\in I
such that
gY_{i_{0}}=Y_{j}
. Then
y\in Y_{i_{0}}\cap Y_{j}
. Thus, there exists
h\in\text{Stab}_{G}(y)
such that
hY_{j}=Y_{i_{0}}
, and
hg\cdot x=h\cdot y=y
. Define
f\coloneqq hg
. Then
f\in G_{i_{0}}
and
f\cdot x=y
. ∎

We apply the lemma to the case
X=B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
and
G=B_{f}\setminus L_{f}
.
Consider the isometry (
37
).
Note that for each
j_{1}\in\left\{1,\dotsc,m^{a}\right\}
and each
\phi\in L_{f}
, we have
\phi(\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j_{1}}})=\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j_{2}}}
for some
j_{2}\in\left\{1,\dotsc,m^{a}\right\}
. Let
y\in B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
. To finish the proof of Proposition
5.19
, it suffices to prove that
\text{Stab}_{L_{f}/B_{f}}(y)
acts transtivitely on the copies of
{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
containing
y
.
In terms of the isometry (
37
), we have

y\in B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}\cong\bigcup_{j\in(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{a}}\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}},

hence there exists
j^{\prime}=(j_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,j_{a}^{\prime})\in(\mathbb{Z}/m)^{a}
such that

y=(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\in\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}\subset\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C}).

In particular,
t_{i}=\bar{t}_{i-1}\zeta^{j_{i}^{\prime}}
for
i\leq 2a
even, and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for
i>2a
.

If all
t_{i}
for
i\leq 2a
are non-zero, then is contained in
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}
but not in any
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}}
with
j\neq j^{\prime}
, so there is nothing to prove. Let us suppose that
t_{1}=t_{2}=0
and the other
t_{i}
are non-zero. Then
y
is contained in those
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}}
with
j_{i}=j_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\geq 2
; there are
m
of them. The group
\text{Stab}_{L_{f}/B_{f}}(y)
contains transformations that multiply
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
by powers of
\zeta
and leave the other
t_{i}
invariant. Thus,
\text{Stab}_{L_{f}/B_{f}}(y)
acts transitively on the set of
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\alpha_{j}}
containing
y
, because if
t_{2}=\bar{t}_{1}\zeta^{j_{1}}
, then
\zeta^{(j_{1}^{\prime}-j_{1})}t_{2}=\bar{t}_{1}\zeta^{j_{1}^{\prime}}
. The general case is similar.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

1
. The path metrics on the path-connected components
Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}
of
Y({\mathscr{L}})
were provided in Definition
5.12
; they were constructed in such a way that the map
{\mathscr{P}}\colon Y({\mathscr{L}})_{i}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
is a local isometric embedding.

2
. The path metrics on the path-connected components of
M({\mathscr{L}})
were defined in Proposition
5.13
. Completeness follows from Proposition
5.14
.

3
. Let
[f]\in M({\mathscr{L}})
be the image of some element
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
. Then
[f]
has a connected open neighborhood isometric to the quotient of a connected open set
W
in
\mathbb{R}H^{n}
by a finite group of isometries
\Gamma_{f}
. To see this, define as before
L_{f}\subset L
as the stabilizer of
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
, define
Y_{f}\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
as in equation (
28
), and define an open neighbourhood
f\in U_{f}\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
with
U_{f}\subset Y_{f}
as in Lemma
5.10
.
3
. Thus, by construction, the natural map
L_{f}\setminus U_{f}\rightarrow L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})
is injective, hence an open embedding.
By Proposition
5.19
.
5
, we know that
L_{f}\setminus Y_{f}
is isometric to
\Gamma_{f}\setminus\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
for some finite group
\Gamma_{f}
of isometries of
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
that fixes the origin
0\in\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. Thus,
L_{f}\setminus U_{f}\subset M({\mathscr{L}})
is isometric to some open set
W^{\prime}
in
\Gamma_{f}\setminus\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. Define
W^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
as the preimage of
W^{\prime}
, and let
W\subset W^{\prime\prime}
be the connected component of
W^{\prime\prime}
that contains
0\in W^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
\Gamma_{f}
preserves
W^{\prime\prime}
, and hence also
W
since
\Gamma_{f}
fixes
0\in W^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. This concludes the proof of Proposition
5.1
.
∎

5.4

Uniformizing the attachment

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
X
be a path metric space such that for each
x\in X
there exist an open neighbourhood
U
of
x
in
X
and a connected hyperbolic manifold
M
together with a finite group
G
of isometries of
M
such that
U
and
G\setminus M
are isometric. Then
X
admits a hyperbolic orbifold structure whose path metric is the given one.

Proof.

See
[
Lan20
]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

1. We want to prove that
L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})
is naturally a real hyperbolic orbifold. In view of Proposition
5.1
, this follows from Lemma
5.21
.

Consider the canonical open embedding of topological spaces

\displaystyle\coprod_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})=L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}}),

(38)

see item
2
in Lemma
4.7
. Let us show that this map is in fact an open immersion of hyperbolic orbifolds. We begin with the following:

Claim:
Let
f=p(x,\alpha)\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
be an element with no nodes (cf. Definition
2.8
). Consider the stabilizer
L_{f}\subset L
of
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
in the group
L
, and the stabilizer
L_{\alpha,x}\subset L_{\alpha}
of
x\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
in the group
L_{\alpha}
. Then
L_{f}=L_{\alpha,x}\subset L
.

Proof of the Claim:
To prove that
L_{f}=L_{\alpha,x}
, we first observe that the quotient map
p\colon\widetilde{Y}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
induces an isomorphism between
L_{(x,\alpha)}
, the stabilizer of
(x,\alpha)\in\widetilde{Y}
and
L_{f}
, the stabilizer of
f=[x,\alpha]\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
. So it suffices to show that
L_{(x,\alpha)}=L_{\alpha,x}
. By Lemma
3.5
, the subgroup
L_{\alpha}\subset L
coincides with the normalizer
N_{L}(\alpha)\subset L
of
\alpha
in
L
, which implies that
L_{\alpha,x}=L_{(x,\alpha)}
because

\displaystyle L_{\alpha,x}=\left\{g\in L_{\alpha}:gx=x\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{g\in N_{L}(\alpha):gx=x\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{g\in L:g\cdot(x,\alpha)=(g(x),g\alpha g^{-1})=(x,\alpha)\right\}

\displaystyle=L_{(x,\alpha)}.

The claim is proved. Item
1
of the theorem can be deduced from it as follows. Let
f=p(x,\alpha)\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
have no nodes. We have
Y_{f}=\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
, hence

L_{f}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}=L_{f}\setminus Y_{f}=\Gamma_{f}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}\quad{\textnormal{with}}\quad\Gamma_{f}=L_{f}\setminus B_{f}=L_{f}.

By construction, an orbifold chart of the space
L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})
is given by

W\rightarrow L_{f}\setminus W\subset L_{\alpha}\setminus(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}})\subset L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})

for an invariant open subset
W
of
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
containing
x
. Because
L_{f}=L_{\alpha,x}
by the above claim, this is also an orbifold chart for
\sqcup_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)
at the point
(x,\alpha)
.

Let
{\mathscr{Z}}\subset M({\mathscr{L}})
be the complement of the image of the open immersion (
38
). We claim that
{\mathscr{Z}}
has measure zero. Let
[f]\in{\mathscr{Z}}
be the image of some element
f\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
, and suppose that
f
is the image of
(x,\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\subset{\widetilde{Y}}
.
Define
Y_{f}\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
as in equation (
28
), so that by Lemma
5.10
, we have
Y_{f}\cong\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}

where
\alpha_{1},\dotsc,\alpha_{\ell}
are the elements in
P{\mathscr{A}}
such that
(x,\alpha_{i})\sim(x,\alpha)
for each
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,\ell\right\}
.
The fact that
{\mathscr{Z}}
has measure zero is a local computation; by construction of the orbifold chart (see Proposition
5.19
), it thus follows from the fact that locally around
x\in\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
, the space
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
is glued to the spaces
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}
along a finite union of geodesic subspaces in
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
each of which has measure zero as a subspace of
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
.

2. The real hyperbolic orbifold
M({\mathscr{L}})=L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})
is complete by item 1, so the uniformization of the connected components of
M({\mathscr{L}})
follows from the uniformization theorem for complete
(G,X)
-orbifolds, see
[
Thu80
, Proposition 13.3.2]
,
[
MMA91
]
or
[
BH99
, Corollary 2.16, Chapter III.
\mathcal{G}
, page 603]
. This proves item 2.

3. We show that the complete real hyperbolic orbifold
M({\mathscr{L}})
has finite volume. By the above, we know that the measure of
{\mathscr{Z}}\subset M({\mathscr{L}})
is zero, where
{\mathscr{Z}}\subset M({\mathscr{L}})
is the complement of the image of the open immersion (
38
). Consequently, the volume of
M({\mathscr{L}})
equals the volume of
M({\mathscr{L}})-{\mathscr{Z}}
. As we have an isomorphism of hyperbolic orbifolds
\sqcup_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)\cong M({\mathscr{L}})-{\mathscr{Z}}
by item 1, we get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{vol}}(M({\mathscr{L}}))

\displaystyle={\mathrm{vol}}(M({\mathscr{L}})-{\mathscr{Z}})={\mathrm{vol}}\left(\coprod_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle={\mathrm{vol}}\left(\coprod_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\right)=\sum_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}}{\mathrm{vol}}\left(L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}\right).

Since the set
C{\mathscr{A}}\cong L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}
is finite by Proposition
3.7
, and since the volume of
L_{\alpha}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha}
is finite by Theorem
3.6
, we conclude that the volume of
M({\mathscr{L}})
is finite. This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.2
.
∎

6

Preliminaries on totally geodesic immersions

The goal of this section is to provide some preliminary results regarding totally geodesic immersions of complete connected hyperbolic orbifolds.
The results in this section are well-known; we state and prove them for lack of suitable reference.

Let
M
and
{\widetilde{M}}
be Riemannian manifolds, with respective Levi–Civita connections
\nabla
and
{\widetilde{\nabla}}
. Let
\phi\colon M\rightarrow{\widetilde{M}}
be an isometric immersion.
Let
TM
be the tangent bundle of
M
and
NM
the normal bundle of
\phi
. For a vector bundle
E\rightarrow M
, let
\Gamma(M,E)
denote the space of global sections. The second fundamental form
{\mathrm{II}}\colon\Gamma(M,TM)\times\Gamma(M,TM)\rightarrow\Gamma(M,NM)
is defined as follows (cf.
[
Lee18
, Chapter 9]
). For a vector field
X
on an open neighbourhood of the image of
\phi
, let
X|_{M}\in\Gamma(M,\phi^{\ast}(T{\widetilde{M}}))
be the induced section of
\phi^{\ast}(T{\widetilde{M}})
over
M
, and let
X|_{M}^{\perp}\in\Gamma(M,NM)
be the projection of the latter onto the normal bundle. Let
X,Y\in\Gamma(M,TM)\subset\Gamma(M,\phi^{\ast}(T{\widetilde{M}}))
be vector fields on
M
, and let
X_{W},Y_{W}\in\Gamma(W,T{\widetilde{M}})
be arbitrary extensions of
X,Y
over an open neighbourhood
\phi(M)\subset W\subset{\widetilde{M}}
. Consider
{\widetilde{\nabla}}^{W}_{X_{W}}(Y_{W})\in\Gamma(W,T{\widetilde{M}})
, where
{\widetilde{\nabla}}^{W}
is the restriction of
{\widetilde{\nabla}}
to
W\subset{\widetilde{M}}
. We define
{\mathrm{II}}(X,Y)\coloneqq{\widetilde{\nabla}}^{W}_{X_{W}}(Y_{W})|_{M}^{\perp}\in\Gamma(M,NM)
.

Let
\phi\colon M\rightarrow{\widetilde{M}}
be an isometric immersion. Recall that
\phi
is a
totally geodesic immersion
if
\phi
carries the geodesics on
M
to geodesics on
{\widetilde{M}}
. By
[
Lee18
, Proposition 8.12]
,
\phi
is a totally geodesic immersion if and only if the second fundamental form
{\mathrm{II}}\colon\Gamma(M,TM)\times\Gamma(M,TM)\rightarrow\Gamma(M,NM)
of
\phi
vanishes identically.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\phi\colon M\rightarrow{\widetilde{M}}
be an isometric immersion of Riemannian manifolds. Assume that for each
p\in M
, there exist open neighbourhoods
p\in U\subset M
and
\phi(p)\in V\subset{\widetilde{M}}
such that
\phi(U)\subset V
and such that the induced map
\phi_{U,V}\colon U\rightarrow V
is a totally geodesic immersion. Then
\phi
is a totally geodesic immersion.

Proof.

By the above, we need to prove that
{\mathrm{II}}(X,Y)=0\in\Gamma(M,NM)
for each
X,Y\in\Gamma(M,TM)
. Fix vector fields
X
and
Y
on
M
, and let
p\in M
. Let
p\in U\subset M
and
\phi(p)\in V\subset{\widetilde{M}}
be open neighbourhoods such that
\phi(U)\subset V
and such that the induced map
\phi_{U,V}\colon U\rightarrow V
is a totally geodesic immersion. Let
X_{U}
and
Y_{U}\in\Gamma(U,TU)
be the restrictions of
X
and
Y
.
The second fundamental form
{\mathrm{II}}_{U}\colon\Gamma(U,TU)\times\Gamma(U,TU)\rightarrow\Gamma(U,NU)
of the map
\phi_{U,V}\colon U\rightarrow V
vanishes because
\phi_{U,V}
is a totally geodesic immersion. Since
{\mathrm{II}}_{U}(X_{U},Y_{U})={\mathrm{II}}(X,Y)|_{U}\in\Gamma(U,NU)
, we get
{\mathrm{II}}(X,Y)|_{U}=0
, and the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
M={\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{1}}
and
N={\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{2}}
for some
n_{2}\geq n_{1}\geq 1
. Consider a smooth map
\phi\colon M\rightarrow N
. Assume that for each
x\in M
, there exist open neighbourhoods
x\in U\subset M
and
y\coloneqq\phi(x)\in V\subset N
such that
\phi(U)\subset V
and such that the induced map
\phi_{U,V}\colon U\rightarrow V
is a totally geodesic isometric immersion. Then
\phi
is a totally geodesic closed isometric embedding.

Proof.

It is clear that
\phi
is an isometric immersion. By Lemma
6.1
, the map
\phi
is therefore a totally geodesic immersion.
We prove that
\phi
is topologically a closed embedding. Let
x\in\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}
. It suffices to show that
\phi
commutes with the differential
d\phi_{x}\colon T_{x}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{1}}\rightarrow T_{\phi(x)}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{1}}
and the two exponentials
\exp_{x}\colon T_{x}\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}
and
\exp_{\phi(x)}\colon T_{\phi(x)}\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
. Indeed, in that case,
\phi=\exp_{\phi(x)}\circ d\phi_{x}\circ\exp_{x}^{-1}
, which is clearly topologically a closed embedding.

To commute that
\phi
commutes with
d\phi_{x}
and the two exponentials
\exp_{x}
and
\exp_{\phi(x)}
, let
v\in T_{x}\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}
. Then
\exp_{x}(v)=\gamma_{v}(1)
, where
\gamma_{v}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}
is the unique geodesic defined on
\mathbb{R}
with
\gamma_{v}(0)=x
and
\gamma_{v}^{\prime}(0)=v
, where
\gamma^{\prime}_{v}(0)=(d\gamma_{v})_{0}(d/dt)\in T_{x}\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}
is the velocity of
\gamma_{v}
at
0
. We must show that

\phi\left(\exp_{x}(v)\right)=\exp_{\phi(x)}(d\phi_{x}(v)).

Let
w=d\phi_{x}(v)\in T_{\phi(x)}\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
and let
\mu_{w}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
be the unique geodesic defined on
\mathbb{R}
such that
\mu_{w}(0)=\phi(x)
and
\mu^{\prime}_{w}(0)=w
. We get
\exp_{\phi(x)}(w)=\mu_{w}(1)
, hence it suffices to show that

\phi(\gamma_{v}(1))=\mu_{w}(1).
Define a map
\tilde{\mu}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
as
\mu^{\prime}\coloneqq\phi\circ\gamma_{v}
. Then
\tilde{\mu}
is a geodesic, since
\phi
is a totally geodesic immersion. Moreover,
\tilde{\mu}
is defined on
\mathbb{R}
, and we have

\tilde{\mu}(0)=\phi(\gamma_{v}(0)=\phi(x),\quad\quad(\tilde{\mu})^{\prime}(0)=(\phi\circ\gamma_{v})^{\prime}(0)=d\phi_{x}(\gamma_{v}^{\prime}(0))=d\phi_{x}(v)=w.

By the uniqueness of
\mu_{w}
as a geodesic defined on
\mathbb{R}
with
\mu_{w}(0)=\phi(x)
and
\mu_{w}^{\prime}(0)=w
, we get
\tilde{\mu}=\mu_{w}
. Consequently,

\phi(\exp_{x}(v))=\phi(\gamma_{v}(1))=\mu_{w}(1)=\exp_{\phi(x)}(d\phi_{x}(v))

as wanted. This shows that
\phi(\gamma_{v}(1))=\mu_{w}(1)
, finishing the proof of the lemma.
∎

Definition 6.3

(cf. §2.4.1 in
[
Bel+21
]
)
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n_{1},1)
and
\Gamma_{2}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n_{2},1)
be lattices, where
n_{2}\geq n_{1}\geq 1
are integers. Let

i\colon\Gamma_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}

be a morphism of smooth orbifolds. We say that
i
is a
totally geodesic immersion
if for any of the lifts
\widetilde{i}\colon\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
of
i
, there exists a totally geodesic subspace
U\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
such that
\widetilde{i}
factors through an isometry
\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}\xrightarrow{\sim}U\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
. If the map
i
is moreover an embedding, then we call it a
totally geodesic embedding
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
n_{2}\geq n_{1}\geq 1
be integers.
Let
\Gamma_{1}\subset{\textnormal{PO}}(n,1)
and
\Gamma_{2}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n_{2},1)
be lattices. Let

i\colon\Gamma_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}

be a morphism of hyperbolic orbifolds. Assume that for each
\bar{x}\in\Gamma_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}
, there exist connected open neighbourhoods
\bar{x}\in V_{1}\subset\Gamma_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}
and
\bar{y}=i(\bar{x})\in V_{2}\subset\Gamma_{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
with
i(V_{1})\subset V_{2}
, such that
V_{i}\cong U_{i}/\Gamma_{i,x}
for a connected open subset
U_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n_{i}}
and a lift
x_{i}\in U_{i}
of the point
\bar{x}_{i}

(i=1,2)
, and such that the induced map
i\colon V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2}
lifts to a totally geodesic immersion
j\colon U_{1}\rightarrow U_{2}
which is equivariant for a homomorphism

\textnormal{PO}(n_{1},1)\supset\Gamma_{1,x}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2,y}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n_{2},1).

Then any lift
{\widetilde{i}}\colon\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
of
i
is a totally geodesic closed immersion. In particular, the map
i
is a totally geodesic immersion of hyperbolic orbifolds.

Proof.

Let
M_{1}=\Gamma_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}
and
M_{2}=\Gamma_{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
.
Let
{\widetilde{i}}\colon\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}
be a lift of
i\colon M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}
. We need to show that
{\widetilde{i}}
is a totally geodesic closed isometric embedding. To do this, let
x_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{1}}
and
x_{2}=\iota(x_{1})\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{2}}
. Let
x_{1}\in U_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}
be a connected
\Gamma_{1,x_{1}}
-stable open neighbourhood with
V_{1}=U_{1}/\Gamma_{1,x}\subset M_{1}
, let
x_{2}\in U_{2}\subset{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{2}}
be a connected
\Gamma_{2,x_{2}}
-stable open neighbourhood with
V_{2}=U_{2}/\Gamma_{2,x}\subset M_{2}
, such that
{\widetilde{i}}(U_{1})\subset U_{2}
. By Lemma
6.2
, it suffices to show that the map
{\widetilde{i}}_{U_{1},U_{2}}\colon U_{1}\rightarrow U_{2}
induced by
{\widetilde{i}}
is a totally geodesic immersion in a neighbourhood of
x_{1}\in U_{1}
.

Let
\rho\colon\Gamma_{1}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2}
be the homomorphism attached to the morphism of orbifolds
i\colon M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}
and the point
x_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{1}}
. Note that
{\widetilde{i}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{1}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{1}}
is equivariant with respect to
\rho\colon\Gamma_{1}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2}
, and that the induced map
M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}
coincides with the map
i
. Moreover,
\rho(\Gamma_{1,x_{1}})\subset\Gamma_{2,x_{2}}
, the map
{\widetilde{i}}_{U_{1},U_{2}}\colon U_{1}\rightarrow U_{2}
is equivariant for the map
\rho\colon\Gamma_{1,x_{1}}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2,x_{2}}
, and the map
V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2}
induced by
{\widetilde{i}}_{U_{1},U_{2}}\colon U_{1}\rightarrow U_{2}
and
\rho\colon\Gamma_{1,x_{1}}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2,x_{2}}
coincides with the restriction of the map
i\colon M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}
to
V_{1}
.

Let
\bar{x}_{1}\in M_{1}
and
\bar{x}_{2}\in M_{2}
be the images of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. By assumption, there exist connected open neighbourhoods
\bar{x}_{1}\in V_{1}^{\prime}\subset M_{1}
and
\bar{x}_{2}=i(\bar{x}_{1})\in V_{2}^{\prime}\subset M_{2}
with
i(V_{1}^{\prime})\subset V_{2}^{\prime}
, such that
V_{i}^{\prime}\cong U_{i}^{\prime}/\Gamma_{i,x}
for connected opens
U_{i}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n_{i}}
and lifts
x_{i}^{\prime}\in U_{i}^{\prime}
of
\bar{x}_{i}
, and such that
i\colon V_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow V_{2}^{\prime}
lifts to a totally geodesic immersion
j\colon U_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow U_{2}^{\prime}
with
j(x_{1}^{\prime})=x_{2}^{\prime}
which is equivariant for a homomorphism

\Gamma_{1,x_{1}}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2,x_{2}}
.

Let
W_{i}\subset M_{i}
be a connected open neighbourhood of
\bar{x}_{i}
such that
W_{i}\subset V_{i}\cap V_{i}^{\prime}
and
i(W_{1})\subset W_{2}
. Up to replacing
V_{i}
and
V_{i}^{\prime}
by
W_{i}
, and
U_{i}
(resp.
U_{i}^{\prime}
) by the connected component of the inverse image of
W_{i}
containing
x_{i}
(resp.
x_{i}^{\prime}
), we may assume that
V_{i}=V_{i}^{\prime}
for
i=1,2
. Then, for
i=1,2
, we let

x_{i}\in U_{\epsilon_{i}}(x_{i})\subset U_{i},\quad\quad x_{i}^{\prime}\in U_{\epsilon_{i}}(x_{i}^{\prime})\subset U_{i}^{\prime}

be an open geodesic ball of radius
\epsilon_{i}>0
such that we have
j(U_{\epsilon_{1}}(x_{1}^{\prime}))\subset U_{\epsilon_{2}}(x_{2}^{\prime})
and
{\widetilde{i}}_{U_{1},U_{2}}(U_{\epsilon_{1}}(x_{1}))\subset U_{\epsilon_{2}}(x_{2}))
. In particular, by the definition of geodesic ball (see
[
Lee18
, Chapter 6, page 158]
),
U_{\epsilon_{i}}(x_{i})
and
U_{\epsilon_{i}}(x_{i}^{\prime})
are the respective images of open balls
B_{\epsilon_{i}}(0)\subset T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}
and
B_{\epsilon_{i}}^{\prime}(0)\subset T_{x_{i}^{\prime}}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}
under the exponential maps
T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}
and
T_{x_{i}^{\prime}}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}
.
Now
B_{\epsilon_{i}}(0)\subset T_{x_{i}}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}
and
B_{\epsilon_{i}}^{\prime}(0)\subset T_{x_{i}^{\prime}}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}
are open balls in a normed space, and therefore simply connected. In particular, the open geodesic balls
U_{\epsilon_{i}}(x_{i})\subset U_{i}
and
U_{\epsilon_{i}}(x_{i}^{\prime})\subset U_{i}^{\prime}
are simply connected for each
i\in\left\{1,2\right\}
.

Since
U_{\epsilon_{i}}(x_{i})
and
U_{\epsilon_{i}}(x_{i}^{\prime})
are geodesic balls, they are metric balls of the same radius, see
[
Lee18
, Corollary 6.13]
. Therefore, since
\Gamma_{i}
and
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}
act by isometries and fix
x_{i}
and
x_{i}^{\prime}
respectively, these groups preserve these balls. Consequently, up to replacing
U_{i}
and
U_{i}^{\prime}
by
U_{\epsilon}(x_{i})
and
U_{\epsilon}(x_{i}^{\prime})
, we may assume that
U_{i}=U_{\epsilon}(x_{i})
and
U_{i}^{\prime}=U_{\epsilon}(x_{i}^{\prime})
. In particular, the
U_{i}
are simply connected, and by
[
Lan20
, Lemma 2.2]
, for
i=1,2
, there exists an isometry

\displaystyle g_{i}\colon U_{i}\xrightarrow{\sim}U_{i}^{\prime}\quad

\displaystyle\text{such that}\quad\Gamma_{i,x_{i}^{\prime}}=g_{i}\circ\Gamma_{i,x_{i}}\circ g_{i}^{-1}\subset g_{i}\circ{\mathrm{Isom}}(U_{i})\circ g_{i}^{-1}={\mathrm{Isom}}(U_{i}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\text{and }\quad\quad\quad\;g_{i}(x_{i})=x_{i}^{\prime}.

The isometry
g_{i}\colon U_{i}\xrightarrow{\sim}U_{i}^{\prime}
is the restriction of an isometry
g_{i}\colon{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}
, i.e.,
g_{i}
is the restriction of an element
g_{i}\in\textnormal{PO}(n_{i},1)={\mathrm{Isom}}(\mathbb{R}H^{n_{i}})
to
U_{i}\subset{\mathbb{R}}H^{n_{i}}
. To prove that
{\widetilde{i}}_{U_{1},U_{2}}\colon U_{1}\rightarrow U_{2}
is a totally geodesic immersion in a neighbourhood of
x_{1}\in U_{1}
, we may thus assume that
U_{i}=U_{i}^{\prime}
and
x_{i}=x_{i}^{\prime}
for
i=1,2
.
Then
{\widetilde{i}}_{U_{1},U_{2}}
and
j
define two lifts

U_{1}\rightrightarrows U_{2}

of the map
i\colon V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2}
. Since
{\widetilde{i}}_{U_{1},U_{2}}(x_{1})=x_{2}=j(x_{1})
, we have
{\widetilde{i}}_{U_{1},U_{2}}=j
because
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are simply connected. The map
j\colon U_{1}\rightarrow U_{2}
is a totally geodesic immersion, hence the same holds for
{\widetilde{i}}_{U_{1},U_{2}}
.
∎

To prove that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)
is non-arithmetic for each
n\geq 2
, we would like to reduce to the case
n=2
. A key ingredient in this reduction step is the following theorem.

Theorem 6.5

(Bergeron–Clozel)
.

Let
n_{2}\geq n_{1}\geq 2
be integers. Let
\Gamma_{i}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n_{i},1)
be a lattice for
i=1,2
, and let

\Gamma_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{1}}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma_{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n_{2}}

be a totally geodesic immersion (see Definition
6.3
). If the lattice
\Gamma_{2}\subset{\mathrm{PO}}(n_{2},1)
is arithmetic, then the lattice
\Gamma_{1}\subset{\mathrm{PO}}(n_{1},1)
is arithmetic as well.

Proof.

See
[
BC05
, Proposition 15.2.2]
(compare also
[
Bel+21
, Theorem 1.7]
).
∎

7

Standard hermitian lattices

The goal of this section is to study particular hermitian lattices
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
, where the underlying CM field
K
is of the form
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-p})
if
p>2
is prime, or of the form
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
if
p>3
is a prime number such that the class number of
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
is odd.
This analysis will allow us in the next two sections to prove Theorems
1.6
and
1.8
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
p>2
be a prime number. Assume that the class number of
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
is odd. Then, there exists a unit
\lambda
in the ring of integers of the totally real number field
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p}+\zeta_{p}^{-1})
that satisfies the following conditions:

1.

One has that
\tau(\lambda)>0
for some
\tau\in\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p}+\zeta_{p}^{-1}),\mathbb{R})
and
\varphi(\lambda)<0
for each
\varphi\neq\tau\in\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p}+\zeta_{p}^{-1}),\mathbb{R})
.

2.

One has
\lambda\not\equiv-1\bmod(1-\zeta)
.

Moreover, if
p=3
one may take
\lambda=-1
, and if
p=5
one may take
\lambda=\zeta_{5}+\zeta_{5}^{-1}
.

Proof.

Let
K=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
and
F=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p}+\zeta_{p}^{-1})\subset K
. Assume first that
p=3
. Then
F=\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathcal{O}_{F}=\mathbb{Z}
, and one may put
\lambda=-1\in\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast}
.

Next, assume that
p\geq 5
.
For an integer
a
with
1<a<\frac{1}{2}\cdot p
, define

\displaystyle\lambda_{a}\coloneqq\zeta_{p}^{(1-a)/2}\cdot\frac{\zeta_{p}^{a}-1}{\zeta_{p}-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast}.

(39)

Let
C^{+}_{p}\subset\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast}
be the group of units in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
generated by
-1
and the elements
\lambda_{a}
for
1<a<\frac{1}{2}\cdot p
.
By
[
Was97
, Lemma 8.1 and Theorem 2]
, the index of
C^{+}_{p}
in
\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast}
is finite, and we have
h^{+}=[\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast}\colon C^{+}_{p}]
, where
h^{+}
denotes the class number of
F
. Moreover, by
[
Was97
, Theorem 4.10]
, we have that
h^{+}
divides the class number
h
of
K=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
. Since
h
is odd by assumption, we get that
h^{+}
is odd.

Let
\varphi_{1},\dotsc,\varphi_{g}
be the embeddings of
F
into
\mathbb{R}
. Since the class number
h
of
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
is odd, the map

{\mathrm{sgn}}\colon\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{g}\left\{\pm 1\right\},\quad\quad u\mapstochar\rightarrow({\mathrm{sign}}(\varphi_{1}(u)),\dotsc,{\mathrm{sign}}(\varphi_{g}(u)))

is surjective by a classical result of Kummer, see e.g.
[
Nar04
, page 126]
or
[
EMP86
, Corollary 2.2]
. Hence, for each
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\dotsc,\varepsilon_{g})\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{g}
there exists
u\in\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast}
such that
{\mathrm{sgn}}(u)=\varepsilon
. Then
u^{h^{+}}\in C_{p}^{+}
since
h^{+}=[\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast}\colon C^{+}_{p}]
, and
{\mathrm{sgn}}(u^{h^{+}})={\mathrm{sgn}}(u)^{h^{+}}={\mathrm{sgn}}(u)
since
h^{+}
is odd. This shows that the homomorphism
{\mathrm{sgn}}\colon C_{p}^{+}\rightarrow\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{g}
is also surjective. In particular, if we let
T\subset\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast}
be the subgroup of totally positive elements, and
T^{+}_{p}=T\cap C^{+}_{p}
, then

C^{+}_{p}/T^{+}_{p}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\ast}/T\xrightarrow{\sim}\prod_{i=1}^{g}\left\{\pm 1\right\}.

Define generators
\mu_{1},\dotsc,\mu_{g}\in C_{p}^{+}
with
\mu_{1}=-1
, such that for each
i\in\left\{2,\dotsc,g-1\right\}
, one has
{\mathrm{sgn}}(\mu_{i})=(\varepsilon_{1},\dotsc,\varepsilon_{g})\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{g}
with
\varepsilon_{i}=1
and
\varepsilon_{j}=-1
for each
j\neq i
. We claim that there exists
i\in\left\{2,\dotsc,g\right\}
such that
\mu_{i}\not\equiv-1\bmod(1-\zeta_{p})
. Indeed, assume for a contradiction that
\mu_{i}\equiv-1\bmod(1-\zeta_{p})
for each
i\in\left\{2,\dotsc,g\right\}
. Then the reduction homomorphism

C_{p}^{+}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\left(\mathcal{O}_{K}/(1-\zeta_{p})\mathcal{O}_{K}\right)^{\ast}\xrightarrow{\sim}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})^{\ast}

has image equal to
\left\{\pm 1\right\}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\ast}
. But for each
a
with
1<a<\frac{p}{2}
, we have

\lambda_{a}\equiv a\bmod(\zeta_{p}-1),

and since
p\geq 5
, we have
2\not\equiv-1\bmod p
. Thus, the element
\lambda_{2}\in C_{p}^{+}
satisfies
\lambda_{2}\equiv 2\not\equiv-1\bmod(1-\zeta_{p})
, which is a contradiction as wanted.

Let
i\in\left\{2,\dotsc,g\right\}
such that
\mu_{i}\not\equiv-1\bmod(1-\zeta)
, and put
\lambda\coloneqq\mu_{i}
. Then
\lambda
satisfies conditions 1 and 2 in the statement of the lemma.
If
p=5
, then
\lambda=\zeta_{5}+\zeta_{5}^{-1}
satisfies conditions 1 and 2 in the statement of the lemma, and we are done.
∎

Definition 7.2
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer.

1.

For a prime number
p\geq 3
, define the hermitian lattice
{\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{qd}}}^{n}(p)
as

{\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{qd}}}^{n}(p)=(\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-p}),\Lambda_{{\mathrm{qd}}}^{n}(p)),\quad\quad\Lambda_{{\mathrm{qd}}}^{n}(p)=\left((\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-p})})^{n+1},h\right),

where
h
