is the hermitian form with
h(x,y)=-x_{0}\bar{y}_{0}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{y}_{n}
.

2.

For a prime number
p\geq 5
such that the class number of
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
is odd, and
\lambda
a unit in the ring of integers of
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p}+\zeta_{p}^{-1})
that satisfies Conditions
1
and
2
in Lemma
7.1
, define

{\mathscr{L}}_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda)=(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p}),\Lambda_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda)),\quad\quad\Lambda_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda)=(\mathcal{O}_{K}^{n+1},h_{\lambda}),

with
h_{\lambda}
the hermitian form defined as
h_{\lambda}(x,y)=-\lambda\cdot x_{0}\bar{y}_{0}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{y}_{n}
.

Any hermitian lattice
{\mathscr{L}}
of the form
{\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{qd}}}^{n}(p)
or
{\mathscr{L}}_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda)
for some
p,\lambda,n
is called
standard
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}
be a standard hermitian lattice. Then
{\mathscr{L}}
is admissible.

Proof.

See Examples
2.12
and
2.13
.
∎

Any attachment
M({\mathscr{L}})
(cf. Definition
4.5
) with
{\mathscr{L}}
a standard hermitian lattice is called a
standard attachment
.

Let
n\geq 1
and let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be a standard hermitian lattice of rank
n+1
, see Definition
7.2
. Define anti-unitary involutions
\alpha_{n,i}\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
for
i=0,1,\dotsc,n
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\alpha_{n,i}\colon\Lambda\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Lambda,\\
&\alpha_{n,i}(x_{0},\dotsc,x_{n})=\left(\bar{x}_{0},-\bar{x}_{1},\dotsc,-\bar{x}_{i},\bar{x}_{i+1},\dotsc,\bar{x}_{n}\right).\end{split}

(40)

As usual, we let
\mu_{K}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}
denote the group of finite units of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
n\geq 1
and let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be a standard hermitian lattice of rank
n+1
. Then the anti-unitary involutions

\pm\alpha_{n,i}\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
for
i=0,\dotsc,n
defined in (
40
) are pairwise non
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
-conjugate. Furthermore, the
\mu_{K}
-equivalence classes

[\alpha_{n,i}]

(i=0,\dotsc,n)
are pairwise non
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)/\mu_{K}
-conjugate.

Proof.

We first introduce some notation.
Define
{\mathfrak{p}}
as the prime ideal generated by
\sqrt{-p}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
if
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-p})
and by
1-\zeta_{p}
if
K=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
.
Define
W\coloneqq\Lambda/{\mathfrak{p}}\Lambda\cong({\mathbb{F}}_{p})^{n+1}
.
Consider the bilinear form

q\colon W\times W\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{K}/{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathbb{F}}_{p}
defined as
q(x,y)=h(x,y)\bmod{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Define
{\overline{\lambda}}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}/{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathbb{F}}_{p}
as follows: we put
\bar{\lambda}=1
if
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-p})
, and define
\bar{\lambda}
as the image of
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
under the map
\mathcal{O}_{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{K}/{\mathfrak{p}}
if
{\mathscr{L}}={\mathscr{L}}_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda)
.

For an anti-unitary involution
\alpha\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
, define
d(\alpha)\coloneqq\dim(W^{\alpha})
and
t(\alpha)\coloneqq\det(q|_{W^{\alpha}})
. Then
d(\alpha)
and
t(\alpha)
are clearly
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
-conjugacy invariants. Let
i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}
. For
x=(x_{0},0,\dotsc,0,x_{i+1},\dotsc,x_{n})\in W^{\alpha_{i}}
, one has
q(x,x)=-{\overline{\lambda}}\cdot x_{0}^{2}+x_{i+1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}.
Similarly, for
x=(0,x_{1},\dotsc,x_{i},0,\dotsc,0)\in W^{-\alpha_{i}}
, one has
q(x,x)=x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{i}^{2}.

Thus, for
i=0,1,\dotsc,n
, one obtains:

\displaystyle\left(d(\alpha_{i}),t(\alpha_{i})\right)=\left(n+1-i,-{\overline{\lambda}}\right),\quad\quad\left(d(-\alpha_{i}),t(-\alpha_{i})\right)=\left(i,1\right).

Since
\bar{\lambda}\neq-1
, see Condition
2
in Lemma
7.1
, this proves the first assertion.

To prove the second assertion, assume that
[\alpha_{i,n}]
and
[\alpha_{j,n}]
are conjugate. Then there exists
g\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
such that
g\alpha_{i,n}g^{-1}=\zeta^{b}\cdot\alpha_{j,n}
for some root of unity
\zeta\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}
and some
b\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-p})
with
p\geq 5
then
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}=\left\{\pm 1\right\}
and we obtain
i=j
by what has already been proven. If
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-3})
, then
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}=\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
hence again,
i=j
. If
K=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
, then
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}=\langle-\zeta_{p}\rangle
so that
i=j
and the result follows.
∎

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
be a standard hermitian lattice of rank
n+1\geq 2
. Define
L=\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)/\mu_{K}
, let
\alpha_{0},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}
be as in (
40
), and for
i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}
, define
L_{i}={\mathrm{Stab}}_{L}(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}})
and
\Lambda_{i}=\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}
. The inclusions in the diagram

\textstyle{L_{i}}
\textstyle{L_{i}^{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda_{i})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda_{i})(\mathcal{O}_{F}),}

see the proof of Theorem
3.6
, are equalities.

Proof.

Let us first prove that
L_{i}^{I}=L_{i}
. For this, recall that
L_{i}=N_{L}(\alpha_{i})
(see Lemma
3.5
). Thus, to prove that
L_{i}^{I}=L_{i}
, it suffices to show that there are no
g\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
such that
g\circ\alpha_{i}=-\alpha_{i}\circ g
. This is true because of Lemma
7.4
, which implies that the elements
\alpha_{i}
and
-\alpha_{i}\in{\mathscr{A}}
are not
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
-conjugate.

It remains to prove that
\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda_{i})(\mathcal{O}_{F})^{I}=\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda_{i})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
. This comes down to showing that for each
i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}
, every isometry of the
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-lattice
\Lambda_{i}
is induced by an isometry of
\Lambda
. To prove this, let
\Lambda_{i}^{\vee}\subset\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}K
be the
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-lattice dual to
\Lambda_{i}
. Let
\theta=\sqrt{-p}
if
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-p})
and
\theta=\zeta_{p}-\zeta_{p}^{-1}
if
K=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
. Then note that, as sublattices of
\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}K
, we have:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{F}\oplus\left(\theta\mathcal{O}_{F}\right)^{\oplus i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\oplus(n-i)},

\displaystyle\Lambda_{i}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{F}\oplus\left(\theta^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{F}\right)^{\oplus i}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\oplus(n-i)},

\displaystyle\Lambda_{i}\cap\theta\Lambda

\displaystyle=\left|\theta\right|^{2}\mathcal{O}_{F}\oplus\left(\theta\mathcal{O}_{F}\right)^{\oplus i}\oplus(\left|\theta\right|^{2}\mathcal{O}_{F})^{\oplus(n-i)}=\left|\theta\right|^{2}\Lambda_{i}^{\vee}.

Since
\theta^{-1}\left(\Lambda_{i}\cap\theta\Lambda\right)=\theta\mathcal{O}_{F}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\oplus i}\oplus(\theta\mathcal{O}_{F})^{\oplus(n-i)}
, one deduces that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda=\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\Lambda_{i}+\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\theta^{-1}\left(\Lambda_{i}\cap\theta\Lambda\right)=\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\Lambda_{i}+\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\theta\Lambda_{i}^{\vee}.\end{split}

(43)

Now let
\phi\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda_{i})
be an
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-linear isometry of
\Lambda_{i}
. Then
\phi
extends to a
K
-linear isometry
\psi
of
\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}K
which preserves
\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\Lambda_{i}
as well as
\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\theta\Lambda_{i}^{\vee}
. By (
43
), it follows that
\psi(\Lambda)=\Lambda
, and hence
\psi
defines an isometry
\psi\colon\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda
such that
\psi(\Lambda_{i})=\Lambda_{i}
. This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

8

Totally geodesic immersions of standard attachments

We fix an integer
n\geq 3
, and let

\displaystyle{\mathscr{M}}=(K,M)\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)

(44)

be standard hermitian lattices such that
{\mathscr{M}}
has rank two and
{\mathscr{L}}
has rank
n
. In particular, we have either that
M=\Lambda_{{\mathrm{qd}}}^{2}(p)
and
\Lambda=\Lambda_{{\mathrm{qd}}}^{n}(p)
, or that
M=\Lambda_{\zeta_{p}}^{2}(\lambda)
and
\Lambda=\Lambda_{\zeta_{p}}^{n}(\lambda)
for suitable
p
and
\lambda
.
We define anti-unitary involutions
\beta_{i}\colon M\rightarrow M
for
i=0,1,2
and
\alpha_{i}\colon\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
by putting
\beta_{i}=\alpha_{2,i}
and
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{n,i}
, see (
40
). We let
M({\mathscr{M}},\beta_{0})\subset M({\mathscr{M}})
(resp.
M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})\subset M({\mathscr{L}})
) be the connected component containing the image of the natural map
{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{0}}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{M}})
(resp.
{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{0}}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})
).

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem, and deduce Theorem
1.8
from it.

Theorem 8.1
.

Consider the above notation. Assume that each anti-unitary involution of
M
is
\textnormal{Aut}(M)
-conjugate to one of the
\pm\beta_{i}

(i=0,1,2)
. Then the following assertions are true.

1.

The orbifold
M({\mathscr{M}})
is connected, hence equal to
M({\mathscr{M}},\beta_{0})
.

2.

There exists a canonical totally geodesic immersion of complete connected hyperbolic orbifolds

\iota\colon M({\mathscr{M}})=M({\mathscr{M}},\beta_{0})\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0}).

3.

In particular, if the hyperbolic orbifold
M({\mathscr{M}})
is non-arithmetic, then the hyperbolic orbifold
M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
is non-arithmetic as well.

Remark 8.2
.

For
M
as above (i.e. equal to
\Lambda_{{\mathrm{qd}}}^{2}(p)
or
\Lambda_{\zeta_{p}}^{2}(\lambda)
for suitable
p,\lambda
), one can ask whether it is always true that each anti-unitary involution of

M
is
{\mathrm{Aut}}(M)
-conjugate to exactly one of the
\pm\beta_{i}
. This is in any case true if
M=\Lambda_{{\mathrm{qd}}}^{2}(3)=\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{2,1}
, as we will prove below (cf. Theorem
9.5
). In a subsequent paper, see
[
GF23
]
, we will prove that this also holds if
M=\Lambda_{\zeta_{5}}^{2}(\zeta_{5}+\zeta_{5}^{-1})
.

8.1

Continuous map between standard attachments

Continue with the above notation. In particular, we consider the standard hermitian lattices
{\mathscr{M}}
and
{\mathscr{L}}
, see equation (
44
) above. We put

\displaystyle L=\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)/\mu_{K}\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\Gamma=\textnormal{Aut}(M)/\mu_{K},

where, as usual,
\mu_{K}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}
denotes the torsion-subgroup of
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}
.
For
i\in\left\{0,1,\dotsc,n\right\}
, define
\Lambda_{i}\coloneqq\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}
, and put

\displaystyle L_{i}\coloneqq L_{\alpha_{i}}={\mathrm{Stab}}_{L}({\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}})\subset L.

For
i\in\left\{0,1,2\right\}
, define
M_{i}\coloneqq M^{\beta_{i}}
, and put

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}\coloneqq\Gamma_{\beta_{i}}={\mathrm{Stab}}_{\Gamma}({\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}})\subset\Gamma.

Consider the canonical embeddings
\textnormal{Aut}(M)\subset\textnormal{GL}_{3}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
and
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\subset\textnormal{GL}_{n+1}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
, and the embedding

\displaystyle j\colon\textnormal{Aut}(M)\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda),\quad M\mapstochar\rightarrow(M,\textnormal{Id}).

(45)

Observe that
{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
is the space of lines in
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
which are negative for the hermitian form
h(x,y)=-\lambda\cdot x_{0}\bar{y}_{0}+\cdots+x_{n}\bar{y}_{n}
. For an element
x=(x_{0},\dotsc,x_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
such that
h(x,x)<0
, we define
[x_{0}\colon\dotsc\colon x_{n}]\in{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
as the associated negative line. Consider then the totally geodesic embedding

\displaystyle\nu\colon\mathbb{C}H^{2}

\displaystyle\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n},

\displaystyle[x_{0}\colon x_{1}\colon x_{2}]

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow[x_{0}\colon x_{1}\colon x_{2}\colon 0\colon\dotsc\colon 0].

Observe that
\nu
commutes with each pair
(\beta_{i},\alpha_{i})
for
i=0,1,2
, hence induces totally geodesic embeddings

\displaystyle\nu_{i}\colon{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}},\quad\quad i=0,1,2.

(46)

Lemma 8.3
.

For each
i\in\left\{0,1,2\right\}
, there is a natural injective homomorphism

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L_{i}

(47)

that makes the map (
46
) equivariant. In particular, for each
i\in\left\{0,1,2\right\}
, there is a canonical totally geodesic immersion of hyperbolic orbifolds
{\overline{\nu_{i}}}\colon\Gamma_{i}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}\rightarrow L_{i}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{i}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{i}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\overline{\nu_{i}}}}
\textstyle{L_{i}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}.}

Proof.

Let
W=M\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\mathbb{C}
and
V=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\mathbb{C}
, where the tensor products are taken with respect to the embedding
\tau\colon K=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
with
\tau(\zeta_{p})=\zeta_{p}=e^{2\pi i/p}\in\mathbb{C}
. For
i=0,1,2
, define
W_{i}=W^{\beta_{i}}
and
V_{i}=V^{\alpha_{i}}
.
Consider the natural isomorphisms
{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}={\mathbb{R}}H(W_{i})
and
{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}={\mathbb{R}}H(V_{i})
, see Lemma
3.4
. With respect to these identifications, the map
\nu_{i}
is given by the totally geodesic immersion
\nu_{i}\colon{\mathbb{R}}H(W_{i})\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}H(V_{i})
induced by the canonical embedding
W_{i}\hookrightarrow V_{i}
. As
\dim(W_{i})=3
, the composition
\textnormal{SO}(W_{i})\rightarrow{\mathrm{O}}(W_{i})\rightarrow\textnormal{PO}(W_{i})
is an isomorphism. Moreover, the composition

\displaystyle\phi\colon\textnormal{PO}(W_{i})=\textnormal{SO}(W_{i})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\textnormal{SO}(V_{i})\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\textnormal{PO}(V_{i})

(48)

is an embedding. We have
L_{i}=\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda_{i})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
and
\Gamma_{i}=\textnormal{PO}(M_{i})(\mathcal{O}_{F})
by Lemma
7.5
, and the map (
69
) restricts to an embedding

\phi\colon\Gamma_{i}=\textnormal{PO}(M_{i})(\mathcal{O}_{F})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda_{i})(\mathcal{O}_{F})=L_{i}.

that makes the map
\nu_{i}\colon{\mathbb{R}}H(W_{i})\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}H(V_{i})
equivariant. The lemma follows.
∎

We now continue by assuming the following:

Condition 8.4
.

Each anti-unitary involution of
M
is
\textnormal{Aut}(M)
-conjugate to one of the
\pm\beta_{i}

(i=0,1,2)
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Assume Condition
8.4
. Then
M({\mathscr{M}})
is connected.

Proof.

By Condition
8.4
, the natural map
{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{0}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{1}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{2}}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{M}})
is surjective. Moreover, if
\sim
denotes the equivalence relation on
{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{0}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{1}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{2}}
induced by its inclusion into
\coprod_{\beta\in P{\mathscr{B}}}{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta}
, where
P{\mathscr{B}}=\langle-\zeta_{p}\rangle\setminus{\mathscr{B}}
, we have

\left({\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{0}}\coprod{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{1}}\coprod{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{2}}\right)/_{\sim}={\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{0}}\bigcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{1}}\bigcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{2}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}H^{2},

which follows from Lemma
4.6
. Consequently, we have a natural surjective map
\cup_{i=0}^{2}{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{M}})
; since
\cup_{i=0}^{2}{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}
is connected, the lemma follows.
∎

Let
{\mathscr{H}}_{2}\subset{\mathbb{C}}H^{2}
(resp.
{\mathscr{H}}_{n}\subset{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
) be the hyperplane arrangement defined by the norm one vectors in
M
(resp.
\Lambda
). Recall that there are natural open embeddings
\sqcup_{i=0}^{2}\Gamma_{i}\setminus({\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}-{\mathscr{H}}_{2})\hookrightarrow M({\mathscr{M}})
and
\sqcup_{i=0}^{n}L_{i}\setminus({\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}-{\mathscr{H}}_{n})\hookrightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})
, see Theorem
1.2
(and compare Lemma
4.7
).

Proposition 8.6
.

Assume Condition
8.4
.
There is a canonical map of topological spaces
\iota\colon M({\mathscr{M}})=M({\mathscr{M}},\beta_{0})\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
that makes the diagram

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 46.04852pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.16147pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 54.11305pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 94.11209pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 94.11209pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 53.0928pt\raise-26.42558pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\overline{\nu}}}$}}}\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(49)

commute, where
{\overline{\nu}}
is the coproduct of the maps
{\overline{\nu_{i}}}
of Lemma
8.3
.

Proof.

We have
M({\mathscr{M}})=M({\mathscr{M}},\beta_{0})
by Lemma
8.5
.
Let
{\overline{f}}\in M({\mathscr{M}})
. We can lift
{\overline{f}}
to an element
(x,\beta_{i})\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{0}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{1}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{2}}
for some
i\in\left\{0,1,2\right\}
. This yields an element
(\nu_{i}(x),\alpha_{i})\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\alpha_{0}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\alpha_{1}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\alpha_{2}}
. We define
\iota({\overline{f}})
as the image
[(\nu_{i}(x),\alpha_{i})]\in M({\mathscr{L}})
of the element
(\nu_{i}(x),\alpha_{i})
under the composition of maps
{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\alpha_{0}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\alpha_{1}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\alpha_{2}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})
. Notice that
[(\nu_{i}(x),\alpha_{i})]\in M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
.

We check that this gives a well-defined defined map. To prove this, let
(y,\beta_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{0}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{1}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{2}}
be another lift of
{\overline{f}}
. Assume first that
i=j
. In that case, there exists an element
g\in\Gamma_{i}
such that
g\cdot x=y
. Consider the homomorphism
\phi\colon\Gamma_{i}\rightarrow L_{i}
defined in (
47
). By Lemma
8.3
, we get
\phi(g)\cdot\nu_{i}(x)=\nu_{i}(g\cdot x)=\nu_{i}(y)
. Hence
[(\nu_{i}(x),\alpha_{i})]=[(\nu_{i}(y),\alpha_{i})]\in M({\mathscr{L}})
, proving what we want. Next, assume that
i\neq j
. We claim that
[(\nu_{i}(x),\alpha_{i})]=[(\nu_{i}(y),\alpha_{j})]\in M({\mathscr{L}})
. Observe that there exists
g\in\Gamma_{i}
such that
(g\cdot x,\beta_{i})\sim(y,\beta_{j})
. Since
[(\nu_{i}(x),\alpha_{i})]=[(\phi(x)\cdot\nu_{i}(x),\alpha_{i})]=[(\nu_{i}(g\cdot x),\alpha_{i})]
, we may assume that
(x,\beta_{i})\sim(y,\beta_{j})
. This implies that
x=y\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}\cap{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{j}}
and hence that

\nu_{i}(x)=\nu_{i}(y)\in\nu\left({\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}\cap{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{j}}\right)=\nu\left({\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}\right)\cap\nu\left({\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{j}}\right)\subset{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\cap{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{j}}.

Consequently,
(\nu_{i}(x),\alpha_{i})\sim(\nu_{j}(y),\alpha_{j})
, hence
[(\nu_{i}(x),\alpha_{i})]=[(\nu_{i}(y),\alpha_{j})]\in M({\mathscr{L}})
as wanted. Thus
\iota\colon M({\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
is a well-defined map. Moreover,
\iota
is continuous since
\iota
fits inside the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\coprod_{i=0}^{2}{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}}{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M({\mathscr{M}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{M({\mathscr{L}})}

in which the vertical arrows are quotient maps and the upper horizontal arrow is continuous. Diagram (
49
) clearly commutes and the proof is finished.
∎

8.2

Orbifold map between standard attachments

Proposition 8.7
.

Assume Condition
8.4
.
The map
\iota\colon M({\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
defined in Proposition
8.6
is a morphism of real analytic orbifolds.

Proof.

Let
\bar{f}\in M({\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{2})
. Our goal is to show that
\iota\colon M({\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
is a morphism of real analytic orbifolds at the point
\bar{f}
. For this, we lift
\bar{f}
to an element
(x,\beta_{i_{0}})\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{0}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{1}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{2}}
for some
i_{0}\in\left\{0,1,2\right\}
, which induces an element
(\nu_{i_{0}}(x),\alpha_{i_{0}})\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{0}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{1}}\sqcup{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{2}}
with image
[(\nu_{i_{0}}(x),\alpha_{i_{0}})]=\iota({\overline{f}})\in M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
.

We have
\nu(x)\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i_{0}}}
. Let
f\in Y({\mathscr{M}})
be the image of
x
under the natural map
{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\alpha_{i_{0}}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
. Similarly, let
g\in Y({\mathscr{L}})
and
\bar{g}\in M({\mathscr{L}})
be the images of
\nu(x)\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i_{0}}}
under the natural maps
{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i_{0}}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}})
and
{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i_{0}}}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}})
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
A_{f}\coloneqq\textnormal{Stab}_{\Gamma}(f)\subset\Gamma=\textnormal{Aut}(M)/\mu_{K}
and
A_{g}\coloneqq\textnormal{Stab}_{L}(g)\subset L=\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)/\mu_{K}
. Let
B_{f}\subset A_{f}
and
B_{g}\subset A_{g}
be the subgroups generated by reflections in the real roots of
f
and
g
respectively. Then there is a canonical group homomorphism

\displaystyle\pi\colon A_{f}/B_{f}=\textnormal{Stab}_{\Gamma}(f)/B_{f}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\textnormal{Stab}_{L}(g)/B_{g}=A_{g}/B_{g}.

(50)

Proof of Lemma
8.8
.

To prove this, it suffices to show that there is a canonical group homomorphism
\pi^{\prime}\colon A_{f}\rightarrow A_{g}/B_{g}
that sends
B_{f}\subset A_{f}
to zero. Observe that
\textnormal{Stab}_{\Gamma}(f)=\textnormal{Stab}_{\textnormal{Aut}(M)}(f)/\mu_{K}
and
\textnormal{Stab}_{L}(g)=\textnormal{Stab}_{\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)}(g)/\mu_{K}
. Let
\phi\in A_{f}
and choose a representative
\tilde{\phi}\in\textnormal{Stab}_{\textnormal{Aut}(M)}(f)
. Let
M^{\perp}\subset\Lambda
be the orthogonal complement of
M\subset\Lambda
, and observe that
\Lambda=M\oplus M^{\perp}
as hermitian
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-modules.
The element
(\tilde{\phi},\textnormal{Id})\in\textnormal{Aut}(M)\oplus\textnormal{Aut}(M^{\perp})\subset\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
satisfies
(\tilde{\phi},\textnormal{Id})\in\textnormal{Stab}_{\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)}(g)\subset\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
,
and we define

\pi^{\prime}(\phi)\coloneqq[(\tilde{\phi},\textnormal{Id})]\in B_{g}\setminus\textnormal{Stab}_{\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)}(g)/\mu_{K}=B_{g}\setminus A_{g}.

For another choice of lift
\zeta^{i}\cdot\tilde{\phi}\in\textnormal{Stab}_{\textnormal{Aut}(M)}(f)
of
\phi
, we get

[(\zeta^{i}\cdot\tilde{\phi},\textnormal{Id})]=[(\tilde{\phi},\zeta^{-i})]=[(\tilde{\phi},\textnormal{Id})]\quad\quad\text{since}\quad\quad(\textnormal{Id},\zeta^{-i})\in B_{g}\subset\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda).

Thus,
\pi^{\prime}(\phi)
does not depend on the chosen lift of
\phi
, and we get a group homomorphism

\pi^{\prime}\colon A_{f}\rightarrow A_{g}/B_{g}
;
the latter sends
B_{g}
to zero, which allows us to define (
50
) as the canonical map
A_{f}/B_{f}\rightarrow A_{g}/B_{g}
through which
\pi^{\prime}
factors.
∎

Lemma 8.9
.

Consider the points
x\in\mathbb{C}H^{2}
and
\nu(x)\in\mathbb{C}H^{n}
. There are isometries
\kappa_{2}\colon\mathbb{C}H^{2}\xrightarrow{\sim}{{\mathbb{B}}}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
and
\kappa_{n}\colon\mathbb{C}H^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
identifying
x
and
\nu(x)
with the respective origins in
\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
and

, and that have the following properties:

1.

The isometries
\kappa_{2}
and
\kappa_{n}
commute with
\nu
and the embedding

to

.

2.

of norm one. Let

be the image of

. For

(i+1)
-th coordinate. Then

are the nodes of
\nu(x)
. Moreover,
\kappa_{2}
(resp.
\kappa_{n}
) identifies
\phi_{r_{1}}
(resp.
\phi_{t_{1}},\dotsc,\phi_{t_{n}}
) with the map
(t_{1},t_{2})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\zeta\cdot t_{1},t_{2})
(resp. maps
(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\mapstochar\rightarrow(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{i-1},\zeta\cdot t_{i},t_{i+1},\dotsc,t_{n})
),
and
\beta_{j_{0}}
(resp.
\alpha_{j_{0}}
) with the map
(t_{1},t_{2})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\bar{t}_{1},\bar{t}_{2})
(resp.
(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\bar{t}_{1},\dotsc,\bar{t}_{n})
).

3.

Assume that
x
has one pair of complex conjugate nodes
H_{r_{1}},H_{r_{2}}
. Let
t_{i}\in\Lambda
be the image of
r_{i}
. For
i\geq 2
, define
t_{i}=(0,0,\dotsc,0,1,0,\dotsc,0)\in\Lambda
, where the
1
is placed on the
(i+2)
-th coordinate. Then
H_{t_{1}},H_{t_{2}},\dotsc,H_{t_{n}}
are the nodes of
\nu(x)
. Moreover, the map
\kappa_{2}
(resp.
\kappa_{n}
) identifies
\phi_{r_{1}}
(resp.
\phi_{r_{2}}
, resp.
\phi_{t_{1}},\dotsc,\phi_{t_{n}}

(t_{1},t_{2})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\zeta\cdot t_{1},t_{2})
(resp.
(t_{1},t_{2})\mapstochar\rightarrow(t_{1},\zeta t_{2})
, resp. maps
(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\mapstochar\rightarrow(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{i-1},\zeta\cdot t_{i},t_{i+1},\dotsc,t_{n})
), and
\beta_{j_{0}}
(resp.
\alpha_{j_{0}}
) with the map

(resp.

).

Proof of Lemma
8.9
.

See the proof of Lemma
5.16
.
2
.
∎

To show
\iota\colon M({\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
is a morphism of real analytic orbifolds at the point

, there are four cases to consider:
x\in{\mathbb{C}}H^{2}
either has zero, one or two real nodes, or

has one pair of complex conjugate nodes (see Definitions

and

).

Case I:

The element
x\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i_{0}}}

This case follows from Proposition

(cf. Lemma
4.7
.
2
).

Case II:

The element

has one real node and no other nodes.

Consider the notation in item 2 of Lemma

. Under the coordinates

of Lemma

, any

to an involution of the form

. Thus if the

Define

for

with

. In the coordinates of Lemma

, we have

Next, define
K_{f,\epsilon}=\left\{(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{C})\mid i^{-\epsilon}\cdot t_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}
and

With respect to the natural embedding
\nu\colon\mathbb{C}H^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, one has for each

, that

\nu\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\xi_{i}}\right)\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\chi_{(i,m,\dotsc,m)}}.

Therefore, the maps defined above fit together in the following commutative diagram:

(51)

Notice that embedding
B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}\hookrightarrow B_{g}\setminus Y_{g}
on top of diagram (
51
) is equivariant with respect to the homomorphism
\pi
defined in (
50
). As in the proof of Proposition
5.1
.
3
, see also Lemma
5.10
.
3
,
we choose an
A_{f}
(resp.
A_{g}
)-equivariant open neighbourhood
f\in U_{f}\subset Y({\mathscr{M}})
with
U_{f}\subset Y_{f}
(resp.
g\in U_{g}\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
with
U_{g}\subset Y_{g}
) such that
A_{f}\setminus U_{f}\subset\Gamma\setminus Y({\mathscr{M}})
(resp. such that
A_{g}\setminus U_{g}\subset L\setminus Y({\mathscr{L}})
).

Define an open subset
U_{f}^{\prime}\subset U_{f}\subset Y_{f}
as
U_{f}^{\prime}\coloneqq U_{f}\cap\nu^{-1}(U_{g})
, where we view
\nu
as the canonical map
Y_{f}\hookrightarrow Y_{g}
induced by
\nu\colon{\mathbb{C}}H^{2}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
. Notice that
U_{f}^{\prime}\subset Y({\mathscr{M}})
is again an open neighbourhood of
f\in Y({\mathscr{M}})
. We claim that
U_{f}^{\prime}
is
A_{f}
-equivariant. To prove this, it suffices to show that
\nu^{-1}(U_{g})
is
A_{f}
-equivariant. To see this, let
\psi\in A_{f}
and
h\in\iota^{-1}(U_{g})
; we need to show that
\psi\cdot h\in\iota^{-1}(U_{g})
.
Lift
\psi
to an element
\psi^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{Stab}}_{\textnormal{Aut}(M)}(f)
. Via the canonical embedding
j\colon\textnormal{Aut}(M)\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
defined in (
45
), we obtain an element
j(\psi^{\prime})\in\textnormal{Stab}_{\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)}(g)
and we let
\overline{j(\psi^{\prime})}\in A_{g}
denote its image in
A_{g}
under the quotient map
\textnormal{Stab}_{\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)}(g)\rightarrow A_{g}
. Then

\nu(\psi\cdot h)=\nu(\psi^{\prime}\cdot h)=j(\psi^{\prime})\cdot\nu(h)=\overline{j(\psi^{\prime})}\cdot\nu(h)\in U_{g},

where
\overline{j(\psi^{\prime})}\cdot\nu(h)\in U_{g}
because
U_{g}
is
A_{g}
-equivariant. Hence
\psi\cdot h\in\nu^{-1}(U_{g})
, so that
U_{f}^{\prime}
is
A_{f}
-equivariant, as claimed.

Possibly up to replacing
U_{f}
by
U_{f}^{\prime}
, we may assume that
\nu\colon Y_{f}\hookrightarrow Y_{g}
satisfies
\nu(U_{f})\subset U_{g}
, and in particular, that
\iota(A_{f}\setminus U_{f})\subset A_{g}\setminus U_{g}
. If we define
V_{f}\coloneqq B_{f}\setminus U_{f}
and
V_{g}\coloneqq B_{g}\setminus U_{g}
, then, in view of diagram (
51
), we obtain an isometric embedding

V_{f}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow V_{g}

which is equivariant for the homomorphism
\pi\colon A_{f}/B_{f}\rightarrow A_{g}/B_{g}
defined in (
50
) above, and which fits into the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{g}\setminus Y_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})}
\textstyle{V_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M({\mathscr{M}})}
\textstyle{A_{f}\setminus U_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(A_{f}/B_{f})\setminus V_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{(A_{g}/B_{g})\setminus V_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{g}\setminus U_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M({\mathscr{L}}).}

Thus, the map
\iota\colon M({\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
is a morphism of real analytic orbifolds at the point
\bar{f}\in M({\mathscr{M}})
, which is what we wanted to prove.

Case III:

The element
x\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i_{0}}}
has two real nodes and no other nodes.

This case is similar to Case II and we leave the proof to the reader.

Case IV:

The element
x\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i_{0}}}
has one pair of complex conjugate nodes and no other nodes.

Consider the coordinates
\mathbb{C}H^{2}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
in item 3 of Lemma
8.9
.
As in the proof of Proposition
5.19
.
3
(see also Case II above), we have that
B_{f}
is trivial and

Y_{f}=B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}\cong\left\{(t_{1},t_{2})\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{2}(\mathbb{C}):t_{2}^{m}=\bar{t}_{1}^{m}\right\}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{i}},

where
\xi_{i}\colon\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
is defined as
\xi_{i}(t_{1},t_{2})=(\bar{t}_{2}\zeta^{i},\bar{t}_{1}\zeta^{i})
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}/m
.
Similarly, in view of the proof of Proposition
5.19
.
4
, we can describe
B_{g}\setminus Y_{g}
as follows:

\displaystyle Y_{g}

\displaystyle\cong\left\{(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid t_{2}^{m}=\bar{t}_{1}^{m},t_{3}^{m},\dotsc,t_{n}^{m}\in\mathbb{R}\right\},\quad\quad{\textnormal{ and }}

\displaystyle B_{g}\setminus Y_{g}

\displaystyle\cong\left\{(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})\in{{\mathbb{B}}}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\mid t_{2}^{m}=\bar{t}_{1}^{m},t_{3},\dotsc,t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{i}},

where
\xi_{i}\colon\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
is defined as
\xi_{i}(t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n})=(\bar{t}_{2}\zeta^{i},\bar{t}_{1}\zeta^{i},\bar{t}_{3},\dotsc,\bar{t}_{n})
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}/m
. By Proposition
5.19
.
5
, the group
A_{f}=\textnormal{Stab}_{\Gamma}(f)
(resp.
A_{g}=\textnormal{Stab}_{L}(g)
) acts transitively on the copies of
\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
(resp.
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
). Define

S_{f}\coloneqq{\textnormal{Stab}}_{A_{f}/B_{f}}({\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}})
and
S_{g}\coloneqq{\textnormal{Stab}}_{A_{g}/B_{g}}({\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}})
as the respective stabilizers of
\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}
and
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}
. The map
Y_{f}=B_{f}\setminus Y_{f}\rightarrow B_{g}\setminus Y_{g}
induced by
\nu\colon Y_{f}\hookrightarrow Y_{g}
is equivariant for the map
\pi\colon A_{f}/B_{f}\rightarrow A_{g}/B_{g}
constructed in Lemma
8.8
, hence we obtain a map

A_{f}\setminus Y_{f}=(B_{f}\setminus A_{f})\setminus(B_{f}\setminus Y_{f})\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow(B_{g}\setminus A_{g})\setminus(B_{g}\setminus Y_{g})=A_{g}\setminus Y_{g}

that fits into the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{Y_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{g}\setminus Y_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{i}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{f}\setminus Y_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{g}\setminus Y_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{S_{f}\setminus\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{S_{g}\setminus\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}.}

As in the proof of Proposition
5.1
.
3
, see also Lemma
5.10
.
3
,
we choose an
A_{f}
(resp.
A_{g}
)-equivariant open neighbourhood
f\in U_{f}\subset Y({\mathscr{M}})
with
U_{f}\subset Y_{f}
(resp.
g\in U_{g}\subset Y({\mathscr{L}})
with
U_{g}\subset Y_{g}
) such that
A_{f}\setminus U_{f}\subset M({\mathscr{M}})
(resp. such that
A_{g}\setminus U_{g}\subset M({\mathscr{L}})
). As in Case II, we may assume that the canonical map
\nu\colon Y_{f}\hookrightarrow Y_{g}
satisfies
\nu(U_{f})\subset U_{g}
, and in particular, that
\iota(A_{f}\setminus U_{f})\subset A_{g}\setminus U_{g}
.

Finally, define
V_{f}\coloneqq U_{f}\cap\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}
and
V_{g}\coloneqq(B_{g}\setminus U_{g})\cap\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}
. Then
V_{f}
and
V_{g}
are open subsets of
\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}
and
\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}
respectively, and we have
a commutative diagram of the following form:

\textstyle{\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}}
\textstyle{V_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})_{\xi_{1}}}
\textstyle{S_{f}\setminus V_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{A_{f}\setminus U_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{A_{g}\setminus U_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{S_{g}\setminus V_{g}}

Consequently,
\iota\colon M({\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
is a morphism of real analytic orbifolds at
\bar{f}\in M({\mathscr{M}})
. This finishes Case IV, and thereby the proof of Proposition
8.7
.
∎

The following is a corollary of the proof of Proposition
8.7
.

Corollary 8.10
.

Assume Condition
8.4
.
The map
\iota\colon M({\mathscr{M}})\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
defined in Proposition
8.6
is a totally geodesic immersion of complete connected real hyperbolic orbifolds, see Definition
6.3
.

Proof.

Let
i_{0}\in\left\{0,1,2\right\}
.
Let
x\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{2}_{\beta_{i_{0}}}
with image
\bar{f}\in M({\mathscr{M}})
. Consider
\nu(x)\in{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}_{\beta_{i_{0}}}
and let
\bar{g}\in M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
be its image in
M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
. By the proof of Proposition
8.7
, there exist open neighbourhoods
W_{f}\subset M({\mathscr{M}})
and
W_{g}\subset M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})
of
\bar{f}
and
\bar{g}
respectively, such that
W_{f}=S_{f}\setminus V_{f}
for an open neighbourhood
V_{f}\subset\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
of the origin
0\in\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and a finite group of isometries
S_{f}
that preserves
V_{f}
, and
W_{g}=S_{g}\setminus V_{g}
for an open neighbourhood
V_{g}\subset\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
of the origin
0\in\mathbb{B}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
and a finite group of isometries
S_{g}
that preserves
V_{g}
, such that
\iota(W_{f})\subset W_{g}
and such that the map
\iota\colon W_{f}\rightarrow W_{g}
lifts to a totally geodesic immersion
V_{f}\hookrightarrow V_{g}
which is equivariant for a group homomorphism
\pi\colon S_{f}\rightarrow S_{g}
. Replacing
V_{f}
by its connected component that contains
0\in\mathbb{B}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we may assume that
V_{f}
is connected. Replacing
V_{g}
by its connected component containing the image of
V_{f}
, we may assume that
V_{g}
is connected as well. The corollary follows then from Lemma
6.4
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.1
.

For item
1
, see Lemma
8.5
. For item
2
, see Corollary
8.10
. Item
3
follows from item
2
and Theorem
6.5
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

Note that the
\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]^{2,1}_{\lambda})
-conjugacy classes of the anti-unitary involutions
(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\epsilon_{0}\bar{x}_{0},\epsilon_{1}\bar{x}_{1},\epsilon_{2}\bar{x}_{0})
for
\epsilon_{i}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}
are the same as the
\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}]^{2,1}_{\lambda})
-conjugacy classes of the elements
\pm\alpha_{2,i}
, where the latter are defined in (
40
). Thus, the result is a direct consequence of Theorem
8.1
.
3
.
∎

9

Uniformization of real moduli spaces

In this section, we first prove Theorem
1.11
, and then prove Theorem
1.6
.

9.1

Cubic surfaces

Write
{\mathscr{C}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
for the space of non-zero
cubic forms with real coefficients in four variables which are stable in the sense of geometric invariant theory, and let
{\mathscr{C}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathscr{C}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
be the open subspace of cubic forms whose associated cubic surface is smooth. The orbifold quotients

{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}=\textnormal{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\mathscr{C}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\supset\textnormal{GL}_{4}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\mathscr{C}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}={\mathscr{M}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}

are the respective moduli spaces of stable and smooth real cubic surfaces. By
[
ACT10
, Theorem 1.2]
, there are a non-arithmetic lattice
P\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(4,1)
, a homeomorphism

\displaystyle{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\cong P\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{4},

(52)

and a
P\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}
-invariant union
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{4}
of two- and three-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{R}H^{4}
such that (
10
) restricts to an orbifold isomorphism

{\mathscr{M}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}\cong P\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{4}-{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}\right)
. Let us explain this result in some detail, before we relate it to the uniformization of
M({\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4})
, see Theorems
1.2
and
1.6
.

As in
[
ACT10
]
, let
{\mathscr{C}}
be the space of all non-zero cubic forms
in four complex variables,
\Delta
the discriminant locus, and
{\mathscr{C}}_{0}={\mathscr{C}}-\Delta
the set of forms defining smooth cubic surfaces. Write
G=\textnormal{GL}(4,\mathbb{C})/\langle\zeta_{3}\cdot\textnormal{Id}\rangle
which acts faithfully and properly discontinuously on
{\mathscr{C}}_{0}
; the orbifold
{\mathscr{M}}_{0}=G\setminus{\mathscr{C}}_{0}
is the moduli space of smooth cubic surfaces in
\mathbb{P}^{3}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Define

\displaystyle{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{0}

(53)

as in
[
ACT10
, Section 2.2]
; thus
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
is the space of framed smooth cubic surfaces and (
53
) is the map that forgets the framing.

As before, let
{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
be the set of anti-unitary involutions
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{4,1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{4,1}
, let
P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
be the quotient of
{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
by
\mu_{\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3})}=\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
, and let
C{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}\subset P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
be a set of representatives for the action of the group
L\coloneqq\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{4,1})/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
on
P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
by conjugation. Thus, we have
C{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}\cong L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
.

Let
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
be the preimage of
{\mathscr{C}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}
under the map (
53
). As shown in
[
ACT10
]
, each
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
naturally lifts to an anti-holomorphic involution
\alpha\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\rightarrow{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
. Moreover,
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\alpha}\cap{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\beta}=\emptyset
for
\alpha\neq\beta\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
, and we have

{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}=\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\alpha}.

as subspaces of
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
. Let

g\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{4}

be the period map defined in
[
ACT10
, equation (2.10)]
. For each
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
, the map
g
commutes with the involutions given by
\alpha
on both sides, hence induces a map

g|_{{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\alpha}}\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\alpha}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}H^{4})^{\alpha}=\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}

for each
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
. The resulting real analytic map

g^{\mathbb{R}}\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}=\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}

is constant on
G^{\mathbb{R}}
-orbits, where
G^{\mathbb{R}}\coloneqq\textnormal{GL}(4,\mathbb{R})\subset G
, and its image is contained in
\sqcup_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}\left(\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)
, where
{\mathscr{H}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{4}
denotes as usual the hyperplane arrangement defined by the norm one vectors in
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{4,1}
. By
[
ACT10
, Theorem 3.3]
, the induced map

g^{\mathbb{R}}\colon G^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}\left(\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)

is a diffeomorphism. Taking the quotient by
L\coloneqq\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{4,1})/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
yields an isomorphism of real analytic orbifolds

{\mathscr{M}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}=G^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathscr{C}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}=(L\times G^{\mathbb{R}})\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right)\cong\coprod_{\alpha\in C{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}L_{\alpha}\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}-{\mathscr{H}}\right).

Let
{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\supset{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
be the Fox completion of
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\rightarrow{\mathscr{C}}_{0}
over
{\mathscr{C}}_{s}
, see
[
Fox57
]
. By
[
ACT02a
, (3.17) - (3.19)]
, the period map
g\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{4}
extends to a morphism
g_{s}\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{4}
which factors through
G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}
and induces an
L
-equivariant diffeomorphism
g_{s}\colon G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\cong\mathbb{C}H^{4}
. The latter sends the
k
-nodal cubic surfaces to the locus in
\mathbb{C}H^{4}
where exactly
k
of the hyperplanes of
{\mathscr{H}}
meet. The induced map
{\mathscr{M}}_{s}=G\setminus{\mathscr{C}}_{s}\rightarrow L\setminus\mathbb{C}H^{4}
is an isomorphism of analytic spaces.

By
[
ACT10
, Lemma 11.3]
, for every
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
, the restriction of the period map
g_{s}\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}H^{4}
to
{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}\subset{\mathscr{F}}_{s}
induces a surjective morphism

\displaystyle{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}

(54)

that factors through an isomorphism
G^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}
of real analytic manifolds.

Define
{\widetilde{Y}}=\sqcup_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}
and let

p\colon{\widetilde{Y}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4})
be the quotient map. As in
[
ACT10
, Section 11.1]
, let
{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
be the preimage of
{\mathscr{C}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
in
{\mathscr{F}}_{s}
. Note that, as topological spaces, we have

\left(L\times G^{\mathbb{R}}\right)\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}={\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}.

Theorem 9.1
.

Consider the above notation and assumptions. Let

\displaystyle g_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\colon\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}=\widetilde{Y}

(55)

be the smooth map which is the coproduct of the maps
{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}
defined in (
54
) above. Then the morphism (
55
) induces the following commutative diagram of topological spaces, in which
h_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
and
\overline{h}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
are homeomorphisms:

\textstyle{\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}\end{subarray}}{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{g}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}}
\textstyle{Y({\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{Y({\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathscr{C}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{h}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{M({\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}).}

Proof.

We claim that the composition
p\circ g_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
factors through a morphism
\overline{g}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4})
. Let
f_{\alpha},g_{\beta}\in\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}
. Then
f_{\alpha}
and
g_{\beta}
have the same image in
{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
if and only if
f=g\in{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}\cap{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\beta}
, in which case

g_{s}(f)=g_{s}(g)\eqqcolon x\in\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\beta}
. Note that
\alpha\beta\in L_{f}
, and
g_{s}
induces an isomorphism

L_{f}\cong G(x)

by
[
ACT10
, Lemma 10.3]
. Hence
\alpha\beta\in G(x)
so that
(x,\alpha)\sim(x,\beta)
, proving the claim.

Next, we prove the
G^{\mathbb{R}}
-equivariance of
\overline{g}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
. Suppose that

f\in{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha},g\in{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\beta}
such that
a\cdot f=g\in{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
for some
a\in G^{\mathbb{R}}
. Then
x\coloneqq g_{s}(f)=g_{s}(g)\in\mathbb{C}H^{4}
, so we need to show that
\alpha\beta\in G(x)
. The actions of
G
and
L
on
\mathbb{C}H^{4}
commute, and the same holds for the actions of
G^{\mathbb{R}}
and
L^{\prime}
on
{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
, where
L^{\prime}
is as in Section
3
. It follows that

\alpha(g)=\alpha(a\cdot f)=a\cdot\alpha(f)=a\cdot f=g,

hence
g\in{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}\cap{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\beta}
. This implies in turn that
(\alpha\circ\beta)(g)=g
, hence
\alpha\beta\in L_{g}\cong G(x)
, see
[
ACT10
, Lemma 10.3]
. Therefore,
(x,\alpha)\sim(x,\beta)
, so that
\alpha\beta\in G(x)
as desired.

Let us prove that the resulting map
h_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\colon G^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow Y({\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4})
is injective. To do so, let

f\in{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}
and
g\in{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\beta}
and suppose that
x\coloneqq g_{s}(f)=g_{s}(g)\in\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\beta},

and
\beta=\phi\circ\alpha
for some
\phi\in G(x)
. We have
\phi\in G(x)\cong L_{f}
(cf.
[
ACT10
, Lemma 10.3]
) hence
\beta(f)=\phi\left(\alpha(f)\right)=\phi(f)=f
.
Therefore, we have
f,g\in{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\beta}
; since
g_{s}(f)=g_{s}(g)
, it follows from
[
ACT10
, Lemma 11.3]
that there exists
a\in G^{\mathbb{R}}
such that
a\cdot f=g
. This proves the injectivity of
h_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
.
The surjectivity of
h_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
is straightforward: it follows from the surjectivity of
g_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
, see
[
ACT10
, Lemma 11.3]
.

Finally, we claim that
h_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
is open. Let
U\subset G^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
be open. Let
V
be the preimage of
U
in
\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}
. Then

V=(g_{s}^{\mathbb{R}})^{-1}\left(p^{-1}\left(h_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}(U)\right)\right)
, hence

g_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}\left(V\right)=p^{-1}\left(h_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}(U)\right)
, so that it suffices to show that
g_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}(V)
is an open subset of
\coprod_{\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}}\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}
. This follows, because
g_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
is open, being the coproduct of the maps
{\mathscr{F}}_{s}^{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}H^{4}_{\alpha}
, which are open since they have surjective differential at each point. ∎

Proof of Theorem
1.11
.

Since
{\mathscr{M}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}
is connected, the same holds for
M({\mathscr{L}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4})
in view of Theorem
9.1
. By
[
ACT10
, Theorem 4.1]
, we know that
\left\{\alpha_{4,0},\dotsc,\alpha_{4,4}\right\}
is a set of representatives for the action of the group
L=\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{4,1})/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
on
P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{4}
by conjugation. Here, for
i\in\left\{0,1,2,3,4\right\}
, the anti-unitary involution
\alpha_{4,i}
on
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{4,1}
is defined in (
40
). Hence item 1 follows from Theorem
9.1
in conjunction with Theorem

1.9
(whose proof shall be provided in Section
10
, and only depends on Theorem
1.2
, Lemmas
3.5
and
7.4
, and the proof of Theorem
3.6
). Item 2 is clear.
∎

9.2

Binary sextics with a double root at infinity

We use (and extend) the notation of
[
ACT06
, Section 5]
. Consider the real projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{R}}=\textnormal{Proj}(\mathbb{R}[x,y])
, and fix
\infty\coloneqq[1\colon 0]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty}
be the space of non-zero complex polynomials
F\in\mathbb{C}[x,y]
which are homogeneous of degree six that have a double root at
\infty
such that all other roots of
F
in
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
have multiplicity at most two. Let
{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty}
be the subspace of polynomials
F\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty}
such that apart from
\infty
, all roots of
F
have multiplicity one. For
F\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}
, we have
F(x,y)=y^{2}\cdot F_{1}(x,y)
for some
F_{1}\in\mathbb{C}[x,y]
such that
F_{1}(1,0)\neq 0
, hence
y^{-1}F=yF_{1}
is a two-variable homogeneous polynomial of degree five with distinct roots in
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
, one of which is
\infty
. Consider the weighted projective plane
\mathbb{P}(3,3,5)
and define
C_{F}\subset\mathbb{P}(3,3,5)
as the smooth curve

C_{F}\coloneqq\left\{z^{3}+y^{-1}F(x,y)=0\right\}\subset\mathbb{P}(3,3,5).

Then
C_{F}
is a degree three covering of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
branched along five distinct points on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
(among which
\infty=[1\colon 0]
), hence
C_{F}
is a curve of genus three. The cyclic covering group is generated by
C_{F}\rightarrow C_{F},(x,y,z)\mapstochar\rightarrow(x,y,\zeta_{3}z)
, and the induced action of
\mathbb{Z}/3
on
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})
fixes no non-zero element. Thus,
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})
is a free
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]
-module of rank three. Moreover, the hermitian form

h(x,y)=i\sqrt{3}\int x\wedge\bar{y}

is
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]
-valued on the projection of
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})
to
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{C})_{\bar{\zeta}_{3}}
, and the induced hermitian form

h\colon{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\times{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]

is unimodular. Moreover,
h
is positive definite on the one-dimensional space
{\mathrm{H}}^{1,0}(C_{F})_{\bar{\zeta}_{3}}
and negative definite on the two-dimensional space
{\mathrm{H}}^{0,1}(C_{F})_{\bar{\zeta}_{3}}
. Since

{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}={\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{C})_{\bar{\zeta}_{3}}={\mathrm{H}}^{1,0}(C_{F})_{\bar{\zeta}_{3}}\oplus{\mathrm{H}}^{0,1}(C_{F})_{\bar{\zeta}_{3}},

we conclude that
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
as hermitian modules over
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]
, where
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
is the free
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]
-module of rank three equipped with the hermitian form
h(x,y)=x_{0}\bar{y}_{0}-x_{1}\bar{y}_{1}-x_{2}\bar{y}_{2}
. Let

{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}

be the covering space of pairs
(F,[i])
where
F\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}
and
[i]
is a
\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
-equivalence class of isometries
i\colon{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
.

Lemma 9.2
.

The space
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
is connected. Equivalently, fixing a point
F_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}
, the monodromy representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}({\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}},F_{0})\rightarrow\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,3})/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
associated to
F_{0}
and the local system of
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]
-modules with fibre
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})
is surjective.

Proof.

This follows as in
[
ACT06
, Theorem 2.1]
(cf.
[
ACT02a
, Lemma 7.12]
).
∎

For any
(F,[i])\in{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
, the isometry
i\colon{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda
induces an isometry
i_{\mathbb{C}}\colon{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{C})_{\bar{\zeta}_{3}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}^{1,2}
, and

i_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathrm{H}}^{1,0}(C_{F})_{\bar{\zeta}_{3}}\right)\subset i_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{C})_{\bar{\zeta}_{3}}\right)=\mathbb{C}^{1,2}

is a positive line in
\mathbb{C}^{1,2}
. Define
B^{2}
as the space of positive lines in
\mathbb{C}^{1,2}
; thus

B^{2}=\left\{\text{lines }\ell\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}\text{ such that }\left|x_{0}\right|^{2}-\left|x_{1}\right|^{2}-\left|x_{2}\right|^{2}>0\text{ for }0\neq x\in\ell\right\}.

We get a map, the
period map
,

g\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow B^{2},\quad\quad g(F,[i])=i_{\mathbb{C}}\left({\mathrm{H}}^{1,0}(C_{F})_{\bar{\zeta}_{3}}\right)\in B^{2}.

The map
g
is holomorphic since the Hodge filtration varies holomorphically.

Let

\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty}\coloneqq{\mathrm{Stab}}_{\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}(\infty)\subset\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\quad\quad G\coloneqq\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty}/\langle\zeta_{6}\rangle.

The group
G
acts on
{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty}
by
h\cdot F(x,y)=F(h^{-1}\cdot(x,y))
.
Let
{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{st}}}
be the Fox completion of
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\rightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty}
(cf.
[
Fox57
]
). The map
g
extends to a map
g\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\rightarrow B^{2}
which is constant on
G
-orbits, hence induces a morphism of complex analytic spaces
g\colon G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\rightarrow B^{2}
.

Theorem 9.3

(Picard, Deligne–Mostow)
.

The map
g\colon G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\rightarrow B^{2}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

See
[
Pic83
]
and
[
DM86
]
. See also
[
ACT06
, Section 5]
.
∎

Let
{\mathscr{N}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}(\infty)
be the moduli space of stable real binary sextics that have a double root at
\infty
(see
[
ACT06
, Section 5]
), and let
{\mathscr{N}}_{{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}(\infty)
be the space of stable real sextics with a double root at infinity that have no other double roots. In the above notation, we have
{\mathscr{N}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}(\infty)=G^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty}^{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathscr{N}}_{{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}(\infty)=G^{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}
, where
G^{\mathbb{R}}
is the fixed locus of the natural anti-holomorphic involution of
G
,
{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty}
denotes the space of polynomials
F\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty}
whose coefficients are real, and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}={\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty}\cap{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}
.

Theorem 9.4
.

The following assertions are true.

1.

There are a union of geodesic subspaces
{\mathscr{H}}_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{2}
for
i=0,\dotsc,4
and a homeomorphism
{\mathscr{N}}_{s}^{\mathbb{R}}(\infty)\cong\Gamma_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{2}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{2}
that restricts to an orbifold isomorphism
{\mathscr{N}}_{{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}(\infty)\cong\coprod_{i=0}^{2}{\mathrm{PO}}(\Psi_{i}^{2},\mathbb{Z})\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{2}-{\mathscr{H}}_{i}\right)
.

2.

The lattice
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eis}}^{2}\subset\textnormal{PO}(2,1)
is conjugate to the lattice
\Gamma_{\infty}^{\mathbb{R}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(2,1)
defined in
[
ACT06
, Section 5]
.

Proof.

Using the set-up and results above, together with the results of
[
ACT06
, Section 5]
, one can prove this in a way similar to the way in which we proved Theorem
1.11
in Section
9.1
above; we omit the details.
∎

9.3

Proof of Theorem
1.6

In the proof of Theorem
1.6
, that we shall provide below, key inputs are Theorem
8.1
, Theorem
9.4
, and the following result.

Theorem 9.5
.

Consider the Eisenstein hermitian lattice
{\mathscr{L}}^{2}_{\mathrm{eis}}=(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3}),\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{2,1})
of rank three. Each anti-unitary involution of
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{2,1}
is
\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{2,1})
-conjugate to exactly one of the
\pm\alpha_{2,i}
, where the anti-unitary involutions
\alpha_{2,i}
are defined in equation (
40
). In other words, Condition
8.4
holds in this case.

To prove Theorem
9.5
, the idea is to use the ball quotient uniformization of Theorem
9.3
. More precisely, we will deduce Theorem
9.5
from the following consequence of Theorem
9.3
.
For an anti-unitary involution
\alpha
on
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
, define an anti-holomorphic involution as follows:

\alpha\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\mathscr{F}}_{0},\quad\quad\alpha(F,[i])=(\kappa\cdot F,[\alpha\circ i\circ\kappa^{\ast}]),

where
\kappa\cdot F
denotes the binary sextic whose coefficients are the complex conjugates of the coefficients of
F
, and where
\kappa^{\ast}\colon{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{\kappa F},\mathbb{Z})\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})
is the pull-back of the canonical anti-holomorphic map
\kappa\colon C_{F}\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{\kappa F}
.

Theorem 9.6
.

For each anti-unitary involution
\alpha
on
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
, the fixed locus
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\alpha}
of
\alpha
is non-empty.

Before we prove Theorem
9.6
, we show that it implies Theorem
9.5
. To explain this, let, as above,
{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty}\subset{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty}
be the space of polynomials
F\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty}
whose coefficients are real, and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty}
the open subspace of polynomials
F\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty}
such that apart from
\infty
, all roots of
F
have multiplicity one. Then
{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}
has three connected components
{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty,i}

(i=0,1,2)
such that
{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty,i}
parametrizes real polynomials with exactly
i
pairs of complex conjugate roots (and
4-2i
real roots not equal to
\infty
).

Corollary 9.7
.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
be an anti-unitary involution. Then there exists a real binary sextic with double root at infinity and no other double roots
F\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}
and an isometry
i\colon{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
such that the composition

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{i^{-1}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{\ast}}
\textstyle{{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}}

agrees with
\alpha
up to multiplication by
\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
(i.e.,
[\alpha]=[i\circ\kappa^{\ast}\circ i^{-1}]\in P{\mathscr{A}}
).

Proof.

Indeed, we have that
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\alpha}
is non-empty by Theorem
9.6
. Therefore, there exists
(F,[i])\in{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
such that
(\kappa\cdot F,[\alpha\circ i\circ\kappa^{\ast}])=\alpha(F,[i])=(F,[i])
. This implies that
F\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}
and that
[\alpha\circ i\circ\kappa^{\ast}]=[i]
, i.e., we have
[\alpha]=[i\circ\kappa^{\ast}\circ i^{-1}]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.5
.

Let
{\mathscr{A}}
be the set of anti-unitary involutions of
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
, let
P{\mathscr{A}}
be its quotient by
\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
,
and define
L=\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2})/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
.
Notice that
P{\mathscr{A}}
equals the set of
\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
-equivalence classes of anti-unitary involutions
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{2,1}
, and that
L=\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{2,1})/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
. By Lemma
7.4
, we have that the classes
[\alpha_{2,i}]\in L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}
for
i=0,1,2
are pairwise distinct. Thus, we have
\#(L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}})\geq 3
, and to prove Theorem
9.5
, it suffices to prove that
\#(L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}})\leq 3
.

To this end, consider the space
{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}={\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty,0}\sqcup{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty,1}\sqcup{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty,2}
defined above. For
F\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}
, choose an isometry
i\colon{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
, and let
\alpha(F)\colon\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
be the anti-unitary involution defined as
\alpha(F)=i\circ\kappa^{\ast}\circ i^{-1}
, where
\kappa\colon C_{F}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow C_{F}(\mathbb{C})
is the natural anti-holomorphic involution of the Riemann surface
C_{F}(\mathbb{C})
.
Then this construction yields a well-defined function

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}},\quad\quad F\mapstochar\rightarrow\alpha(F).

(56)

By Corollary
9.7
, the function (
56
) is surjective. Moreover, the
L
-conjugacy class
[\alpha(F)]\in L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}
of
\alpha\in P{\mathscr{A}}
only depends on the connected component of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}
in which
F
lies, hence the map (
56
) factors through a surjective map

\left\{0,1,2\right\}=\pi_{0}\left({\mathcal{P}}_{\infty,{\mathrm{sm}}}^{\mathbb{R}}\right)\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}.

It follows that
\#\left(L\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}\right)\leq 3
, concluding the proof of the theorem.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.6
.

Define
\Delta_{{\mathscr{F}}_{s}}={\mathscr{F}}_{s}-{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
. Then
\Delta_{{\mathscr{F}}_{s}}
is a closed analytic subset of
{\mathscr{F}}_{s}
, and the quotient
\Delta\coloneqq G\setminus\Delta_{{\mathscr{F}}_{s}}
is a closed analytic subset of
G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}
. Consider the period map
g\colon G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\rightarrow B^{2}
, which is an isomorphism by Theorem
9.3
, and define
U\subset B^{2}
as the complement of
g(\Delta)
in
B^{2}
.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{1,2}
be an anti-unitary involution. Then
\alpha
induces an anti-holomorphic involution
\alpha\colon B^{2}\rightarrow B^{2}
; let
B^{2}_{\alpha}\coloneqq(B^{2})^{\alpha}
denote its fixed locus. Then
B^{2}_{\alpha}
is naturally isometric to real hyperbolic space of dimension two, see Lemma
3.4
. Let
U^{\alpha}\coloneqq B^{2}_{\alpha}\cap U
. We claim that
U^{\alpha}\neq\emptyset
. Indeed, if
U^{\alpha}=\emptyset
, then
B^{2}_{\alpha}\subset g(\Delta)
which is absurd since
g(\Delta)\subsetneq B^{2}
is a properly contained closed complex analytic subset of
B^{2}
, hence
g(\Delta)
cannot contain a closed real analytic submanifold of
B^{2}
which is real analytically isomorphic to a real two-ball.

By the naturality of the Fox completion, the anti-holomorphic involution
\alpha\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\rightarrow{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
extends to an anti-holomorphic involution
\alpha\colon{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\rightarrow{\mathscr{F}}_{s}
which is compatible with the natural anti-holomorphic involution
\kappa\colon G\rightarrow G
hence descends to an anti-holomorphic involution
\alpha\colon G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\rightarrow G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}
. Moreover, the period map
g\colon G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\xrightarrow{\sim}B^{2}
is equivariant for the anti-holomorphic involutions
\alpha\colon G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}\rightarrow G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{s}
and
\alpha\colon B^{2}\rightarrow B^{2}
, and induces an isomorphism
g\colon G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}U
. In particular,

(G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{0})^{\alpha}\cong U^{\alpha}\neq\emptyset.

Consequently, to prove the theorem, it suffices to show that the natural map

\displaystyle G^{\kappa}\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{0}^{\alpha}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow(G\setminus{\mathscr{F}}_{0})^{\alpha}

(57)

is an isomorphism. For this, by Lemma
9.8
below, it suffices to show that
G
acts freely on
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
and that
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,G)=0
, where the latter denotes degree one non-abelian group cohomology, and where
\mathbb{Z}/2
acts on
G
via conjugation by
\kappa\colon G\rightarrow G
.

Let us first prove that
G
acts freely on
{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
. This holds, since if
(F,[i])\in{\mathscr{F}}_{0}
and
(g\cdot F,[i\circ g^{\ast}])=(F,[i])
, then
g
induces an automorphism
\tilde{g}\colon C_{F}\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{F}
such that
(\tilde{g})^{\ast}\colon{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(C_{F},\mathbb{Z})
is multiplication by some power of
-\zeta_{3}
, thus
\tilde{g}
, and hence also
g
, is some power of
-\zeta_{3}
(Torelli theorem for curves).

Thus, to finish the proof of Theorem
9.6
, it suffices to show that
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,G)=0
. For this, we first show that the natural map
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty})\rightarrow{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,G)
is surjective. Let
\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty}^{\prime}
be the set of linear and anti-linear transformations
\mathbb{C}^{2}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
that fix
\infty=[1\colon 0]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
, and let
G^{\prime}=\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty}^{\prime}/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
. Let
\chi_{0}\in G
such that
\chi_{0}\kappa(\chi_{0})=e
. Then
\chi\coloneqq\chi_{0}\circ\kappa\in G^{\prime}
satisfies
\chi^{2}=\chi_{0}\kappa\chi_{0}\kappa=\chi_{0}\kappa(\chi_{0})=e
. Let
\gamma\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty}^{\prime}
be an element that maps to
\hat{\chi}\in G^{\prime}
. Then
\gamma
is anti-linear since
\hat{\chi}
is anti-holomorphic. Note that
\gamma^{2}=\zeta_{6}^{i}
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}/6
(because
\gamma
maps to
\hat{\chi}
, which is an involution). Define
\alpha=\gamma^{3}
. Then
\alpha
maps to
(\hat{\chi})^{3}=\hat{\chi}
and
\alpha^{2}=(\gamma^{2})^{3}=(\zeta_{6})^{3i}=(-1)^{i}
. Thus
\alpha^{2}=\pm 1
. We claim that
\alpha^{2}=1
. Assume for a contradiction that
\alpha^{2}=-1
. Write
\alpha=\kappa\circ\beta
for some
\beta\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, where
\kappa
is complex conjugation. Note that
\beta\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty}
, hence
\beta=\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}&b_{2}\\
0&b_{4}\end{pmatrix}
for some
b_{1},b_{2},b_{4}\in\mathbb{C}
. Then
\alpha^{2}=\kappa\beta\kappa\beta=\beta\circ\bar{\beta}=-1
which implies that
\left|b_{1}\right|^{2}=\left|b_{4}\right|^{2}=-1
, which is a contradiction. Hence
\alpha^{2}=1
.
Thus, we have found an element
\alpha\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty}^{\prime}
with
\alpha^{2}=\textnormal{id}
, and such that
[\alpha]=\chi\in G
. Define
\alpha_{0}\coloneqq\alpha\circ\kappa
. Then
\alpha_{0}\circ\kappa(\alpha_{0})=\alpha\circ\kappa\circ\kappa(\alpha\circ\kappa)=\alpha\circ\kappa\circ\kappa(\alpha)=\kappa(\alpha)^{2}=\kappa(\alpha^{2})=\textnormal{id}
. Hence
\alpha_{0}
defines an element in
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty})
that maps to the class of
\chi_{0}
in
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,G)
under the natural map
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty})\rightarrow{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,G)
, thus, this map is surjective.

To finish the proof, it suffices to show
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty})=0
. Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}
an anti-linear involution that fixes
\infty
. It suffices to show that there exists
f\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})_{\infty}
such that
\alpha=f^{-1}\kappa f
. Recall that all real structures on a complex vector space are equivalent, so that there exists
h\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
with
\alpha=h^{-1}\kappa h
, where
\kappa\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. Notice that
(h^{-1}\kappa h)(\infty)=\alpha(\infty)=\infty
implies
\kappa(h(\infty))=h(\infty)
. In other words, we have
h(\infty)\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
. Let
f_{1}\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that
f_{1}(h(\infty))=\infty
, and define
f\coloneqq f_{1}\circ h
. Then
f(\infty)=\infty
. Moreover, since
f_{1}\in\textnormal{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have
f_{1}^{-1}\kappa f_{1}=\kappa
, so that
f^{-1}\kappa f=h^{-1}(f_{1}^{-1}\kappa f_{1})h=h^{-1}\kappa h=\alpha
.

This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
X
be a complex analytic space, equipped with an anti-holomorphic involution
\alpha\colon X\rightarrow X
. Let
G
be a complex Lie group equipped with an anti-holomorphic involution
\sigma\colon G\rightarrow G
, such that
G
acts holomorphically on
X
with
\alpha(g\cdot x)=\sigma(g)\cdot\alpha(x)
for
x\in X
and
g\in G
.
Assume that
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,G)=0
(where
\mathbb{Z}/2
act on
G
by conjugation by
\sigma
, and
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,G)
denotes the first non-abelian cohomology group of
\mathbb{Z}/2
with coefficients in
G
), and that
G
acts freely on
X
. Then the natural map
G^{\sigma}\setminus X^{\alpha}\rightarrow(G\setminus X)^{\alpha}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
x\in X
such that its orbit
[x]\in G\setminus X
is fixed by
\alpha
. Then
\alpha(x)=gx
for some
g\in G
. We have
x=\alpha^{2}(x)=\sigma(g)\alpha(x)=\sigma(g)gx
. Since
G
acts freely on
X
, we get
\sigma(g)\cdot g=e\in G
and hence
g\cdot\sigma(g)=e
. Since
{\mathrm{H}}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2,G)=0
, the equality
g\cdot\sigma(g)=e
implies that there exists
h\in G
such that
g=h^{-1}\sigma(h)
. Thus, we have
\alpha(x)=gx=h^{-1}\sigma(h)x
. Therefore,
h\alpha(x)=\sigma(h)x
, and hence
\alpha(\sigma(h)x)=h\alpha(x)=\sigma(h)x
. In other words,
\sigma(h)x\in X^{\alpha}
. Since
[x]=[\sigma(h)x]\in(G\setminus X)^{\alpha}
, we conclude that the map
G^{\alpha}\setminus X^{\alpha}\rightarrow(G\setminus X)^{\alpha}
is surjective.

To prove injectivity of this map, let
x,y\in X^{\alpha}
such that
gx=y
for some
g\in G
. Then
\sigma(g)x=\alpha(gx)=\alpha(y)=y=gx
. As
G
acts freely on
X
, we get
g\in G^{\sigma}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

Let
n\geq 2
.
Consider the Eisenstein hermitian lattice
{\mathscr{L}}^{2}_{\mathrm{eis}}=(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3}),\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{2,1})
of rank three, and the Eisenstein hermitian lattice
{\mathscr{L}}^{n}_{\mathrm{eis}}=(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3}),\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1})
of rank
n+1
. Consider moreover the anti-unitary involutions
\alpha_{n,i}\colon\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}
defined in (
40
). By definition of
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{eis}}
, we have
M({\mathscr{L}}^{n}_{\mathrm{eis}},\alpha_{n,0})\cong\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{eis}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}H^{n}
.
Our goal is to prove that
\Gamma^{n}_{\mathrm{eis}}
is non-arithmetic, i.e., that the complete connected hyperbolic orbifold
M({\mathscr{L}}^{n}_{\mathrm{eis}},\alpha_{0})
is non-arithmetic.
By Theorem
9.5
, each anti-unitary involution of
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{2,1}
is
\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{2,1})
-conjugate to exactly one of the
\pm\alpha_{2,i}
. Thus, we have
M({\mathscr{L}}^{2}_{\mathrm{eis}})=M({\mathscr{L}}^{2}_{\mathrm{eis}},\alpha_{2,0})
by Lemma
8.5
, and we can apply Theorem
8.1
to see that
M({\mathscr{L}}^{n}_{\mathrm{eis}},\alpha_{n,0})
is non-arithmetic if
M({\mathscr{L}}^{2}_{\mathrm{eis}})
is non-arithmetic. In other words, to prove the theorem, it suffices to prove
\Gamma^{2}_{\mathrm{eis}}
is non-arithmetic.
By Theorem
9.4
, the lattice
\Gamma^{2}_{\mathrm{eis}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(2,1)
is conjugate to the lattice
\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty}\subset\textnormal{PO}(2,1)
defined in
[
ACT06
, Section 5]
. The lattice
\Gamma^{\mathbb{R}}_{\infty}\subset\textnormal{PO}(2,1)
is non-arithmetic by
[
ACT06
, page 168]
. Therefore,
\Gamma^{2}_{\mathrm{eis}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(2,1)
is non-arithmetic, and we are done.
∎

10

Volume

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
1.9
. We thus return our attention to the Eisenstein hermitian lattices
{\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n}=(\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3}),\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1})
for
n\geq 2
.
Let
{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
be the set of anti-unitary involutions
\alpha\colon\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}
, let
P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
be the quotient of
{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
by
\mu_{\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3})}=\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
, and let
C{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}\subset P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
be a set of representatives for the action of the group
L^{n}\coloneqq\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1})/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
on
P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
by conjugation. Thus, we have
C{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}\cong L^{n}\setminus P{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
.

For
i=0,\dotsc,n
, consider the integral quadratic form
\Psi_{i}^{n}
defined as

\Psi_{i}^{n}(x_{0},\dotsc,x_{n})=-x_{0}^{2}+3x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+3x_{i}^{2}+x_{i+1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2}.

For each
i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}
, define an anti-unitary involution
\alpha_{i}
as follows:

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}(x_{0},\dotsc,x_{n})=\left(\bar{x}_{0},-\bar{x}_{1},\dotsc,-\bar{x}_{i},\bar{x}_{i+1},\dotsc,\bar{x}_{n}\right).

Theorem 10.1
.

For each
i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}
, one has a canonical isomorphism

L_{\alpha_{i}}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{PO}(\Psi_{i}^{n},\mathbb{Z}),

where
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}
and
L^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\subset{\mathrm{Isom}}(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}})
are defined in equation (
1
).

Proof.

For simplicity, write
L=L^{n}
and
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}
. Let
i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}
, consider the diagram

\textstyle{L_{\alpha_{i}}}
\textstyle{L_{\alpha_{i}}^{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}})(\mathbb{Z})^{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}})(\mathbb{Z}),}

see equations (
20
), (
21
) and (
22
) in the proof of Theorem
3.6
. We claim that, in this particular case, the inclusions on the left and right of (
10
) are equalities.

Let us first prove that
L_{\alpha_{i}}^{I}=L_{\alpha_{i}}
. For this, recall that
L_{\alpha_{i}}=N_{L}(\alpha_{i})
(see Lemma
3.5
). Thus, to prove that
L_{\alpha_{i}}^{I}=L_{\alpha_{i}}
, it suffices to show that there are no
g\in\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
such that
g\circ\alpha_{i}=-\alpha_{i}\circ g
. This is true because of Lemma
7.4
, which implies that the elements
\alpha_{i}
and
-\alpha_{i}\in{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
are not
L
-conjugate.

It remains to prove that
\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}})(\mathbb{Z})^{I}=\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}})(\mathbb{Z})
. This comes down to showing that for each
i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}
, every isometry of the
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice
