\Lambda^{\alpha_{j}}
is induced by an isometry of
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}
. To prove this, note that

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\theta\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus i}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus(n-i)}.

(60)

One can check that the lattice
M\coloneqq\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}\cap\theta\Lambda\subset\Lambda
can be described in terms of
\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}
as
M=3(\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}})^{\vee}
where
(\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}})^{\vee}\subset\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
denotes the lattice dual to
\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}
. Therefore, every isometry of
\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3})=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]}\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3})
which is induced by an isometry of
\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}
preserves the
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-span of
\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}
and
(1/\theta)\cdot M
, which is exactly
\Lambda
.

We conclude that
L_{\alpha_{i}}=\textnormal{PO}(\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}})(\mathbb{Z})
. Finally, observe that, because of (
60
), we have an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-lattices
\Lambda^{\alpha_{i}}\cong(\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus(n+1)},\Psi_{i}^{n})
. The theorem follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

Consider the anti-unitary involution
\alpha_{0}\colon\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}
defined as
(x_{0},\dotsc,x_{n})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\bar{x}_{0},\dotsc,\bar{x}_{n})
. Recall (see Section
1.2
) that
M({\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n},\alpha_{0})\subset M({\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n})
denotes the connected component containing the image of the natural map
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{0}}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n})
, and that

\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\subset\textnormal{PO}(n,1)

is a lattice such that
M({\mathscr{L}}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}^{n},\alpha_{0})\cong\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}
, see Theorem
1.2
. For each
i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}
and each
j\neq i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,j\right\}
, define

H_{ij}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{j}}.

Then
H_{ij}^{\prime}
is a geodesic subspace of codimension
\left|i-j\right|
in
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}
. For instance, if
r_{1}=(0,1,0,\dotsc,0)\in\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1}
, then
r_{1}
is an element of norm one, the involution
\alpha_{0}
preserves the hyperplane
H_{r_{1}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, and

H_{01}^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{0}}\cap\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{1}}=\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{0}}\cap H_{r_{1}}=(H_{r_{1}})^{\alpha_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{0}},

which is a geodesic subspace of codimension one in
\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{0}}
. By Lemma
7.4
, the elements
\alpha_{0},\dotsc,\alpha_{n}\in C{\mathscr{A}}_{\mathrm{eis}}^{n}
are pairwise distinct. Consequently, if we define

{\mathscr{H}}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\bigcup_{j\neq i}H_{ij}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}},

then the natural map

\coprod_{i=0}^{n}L_{\alpha_{i}}\setminus\left(\mathbb{R}H^{n}_{\alpha_{i}}-{\mathscr{H}}_{i}^{\prime}\right)\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow M({\mathscr{L}},\alpha_{0})\cong\Gamma^{n}_{{\mathrm{eis}}}\setminus\mathbb{R}H^{n}

is an open immersion of hyperbolic orbifolds, where
L=L^{n}=\textnormal{Aut}(\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{3}]^{n,1})/\langle-\zeta_{3}\rangle
.
As
L_{\alpha_{i}}\cong\textnormal{PO}(\Psi_{i}^{n},\mathbb{Z})
by Theorem
10.1
, the theorem follows in view of Theorem
1.2
and its proof, see Section
5.4
.
∎

11

Complex ball quotient and moduli of abelian varieties

The goal of this section is to study the structure of the complex ball quotient
L\setminus\mathbb{C}H^{n}
associated to an admissible hermitian lattice
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
(cf. Section
2.2
) as a moduli space of abelian varieties with
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-action of hyperbolic signature. The theory of this section will allow us in the next section to prove Theorem
2.5
, saying that the hyperplanes in the arrangement
{\mathscr{H}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
are orthogonal along their intersection.

11.1

Alternating and skew-hermitian forms

Let
K
be a CM field, with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, and let
\sigma\colon K\rightarrow K
be the involution compatible with complex conjugation on
\mathbb{C}
. Let
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}
be the different ideal. Thus, we have
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}=\left\{x\in K\mid{\mathrm{Tr}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}(x\mathcal{O}_{K})\subset\mathbb{Z}\right\}
, where

{\mathrm{Tr}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\colon K\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}

is the trace morphism, see e.g.
[
Ser79
, Chapter III, §3]
or
[
Neu99
, Chapter III, §2]
.

For a finite free
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module
\Lambda
we put
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
, and call a
\mathbb{Q}
-bilinear form

T\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow K

skew-hermitian
if
T
is
K
-linear in its first argument and satisfies
T(y,x)=-\sigma(T(x,y))
for
x,y\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
. If
T\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow K
is skew-hermitian, then
T(x,a\cdot y)=\sigma(a)\cdot T(x,y)
for each
x,y\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
a\in K
.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
.
Let
\Lambda
be a free
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module of rank
n+1
.
The assignment
T\mapstochar\rightarrow\textnormal{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\circ T
defines a bijection between:

1.

The set of skew-hermitian forms
T\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow K
.

2.

The set of alternating forms
E\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
that satisfy
E(a\cdot x,y)=E(x,\sigma(a)\cdot y)
for
x,y\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
a\in K
.

Under this correspondence,
T(\Lambda,\Lambda)\subset{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}
if and only if
E(\Lambda,\Lambda)\subset\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let
T\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow K
be a skew-hermitian form. Define
E_{T}=\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\circ T
. Since
T
is skew-hermitian, we have, for each
x,y\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
, that

\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}T(x,y)=-\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\sigma\left(T(y,x)\right).

Since
K/\mathbb{Q}
is separable, for
x\in K
, we have

\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}(x)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq g}\left(\tau_{i}(x)+\tau_{i}\sigma(x)\right)
, see e.g.
[
Ste08
, (7-1)]
. Therefore,
\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}(\sigma(x))=\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}(x)
, so that

E_{T}(x,y)=\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}T(x,y)=-\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\sigma\left(T(y,x)\right)=-\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}T(y,x)=-E_{T}(y,x)

for any
x,y\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Moreover, we have
T(a\cdot x,y)=a\cdot T(x,y)=T(x,\sigma(a)\cdot y)
hence
E_{T}(a\cdot x,y)=E_{T}(x,\sigma(a)\cdot y)
for all
x,y\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
a\in K
.

Conversely, let
E\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
be an alternating form such that
E(a\cdot x,y)=E(x,\sigma(a)\cdot y)
for
x,y\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
a\in K
. Choose a basis
\{b_{1},\dotsc,b_{n+1}\}\subset\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
for
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
over
K
. Let
\{e_{i}\}
be the canonical basis of
K^{n+1}
,
and let
Q
be the map
K^{n+1}\times K^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
defined by
Q(e_{i},e_{j})=E(b_{i},b_{j})
. For
i,j\in\left\{1,\dotsc,n+1\right\}
, define a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear map

f_{ij}\colon K\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q},\quad\quad f_{ij}(a)=Q(a\cdot e_{i},e_{j}).

Since the trace pairing

{\mathrm{Tr}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\colon K\times K\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}

is non-degenerate (see e.g.
[
Stacks
,
Tag 0BIL
]
), for each
i,j\in\left\{1,\dotsc,n+1\right\}
there is a unique element
t_{ij}\in K
such that
f_{ij}(a)=\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}(a\cdot t_{ij})
for every
a\in K
. This gives a matrix
(t_{ij})_{ij}\in{\mathrm{M}}_{n+1}(K)
such that
\sigma(t_{ij})=-t_{ji}
, and via the basis
\{b_{i}\}
of
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
, this matrix
(t_{ij})_{ij}
induces a skew-hermitian form
T_{E}\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow K
. It is straightforward to show that the functions
T\mapstochar\rightarrow E_{T}
and
E\mapstochar\rightarrow T_{E}
are inverse to each other, providing the desired bijection between the sets
1
and
2
.

Finally, let
E
be an alternating form corresponding to a skew-hermitian form
T
under the above bijection of sets. Then
E(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
x,y\in\Lambda
if and only if for each
x,y\in\Lambda
, we have
{\mathrm{Tr}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\left(a\cdot T(x,y)\right)={\mathrm{Tr}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\left(T(a\cdot x,y)\right)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
a\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. The latter holds if and only if for each
x,y\in\Lambda
, we have
T(x,y)\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}
.
∎

Examples 11.2
.

1.

Let
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta})
is imaginary quadratic over
\mathbb{Q}
, with discriminant
\Delta
and with involution
\sigma\colon K\rightarrow K
defined by
\sigma(\sqrt{\Delta})=-\sqrt{\Delta}
. Let
E\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be an alternating form that satisfies
E(a\cdot x,y)=E(x,\sigma(a)\cdot y)
for
x,y\in\Lambda
and
a\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Define a form
T\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow{\mathfrak{D}}_{M}^{-1}=(\sqrt{\Delta})^{-1}
as

T(x,y)=\frac{E(\sqrt{\Delta}\cdot x,y)+E(x,y)\sqrt{\Delta}}{2\sqrt{\Delta}}.

(61)

Then
T
is skew-hermitian, because it is
K
-linear in its first variable (since we have
T(\sqrt{\Delta}\cdot x,y)=\sqrt{\Delta}\cdot T(x,y)
) and because
T(y,x)=-\sigma(T(x,y))
. Moreover,
T
is the skew-hermitian form associated to
E
by Lemma
11.1
since we have
{\mathrm{Tr}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}(T(x,y))=E(x,y)
, as is readily shown.

2.

Let
K=\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p})
where
\zeta_{p}=e^{2\pi i/p}\in\mathbb{C}
for some prime number
p>2
. Let
E:\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be an alternating form with
E(a\cdot x,y)=E(x,\sigma(a)\cdot y)
for
x,y\in\Lambda
,
a\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Then
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}=\left(p/(\zeta_{p}-\zeta_{p}^{-1})\right)
, and the skew-hermitian
T\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\left(p^{-1}\cdot(\zeta_{p}-\zeta_{p}^{-1})\right)
associated to
E

by Lemma
11.1
is defined as

T(x,y)=\frac{1}{p}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\zeta_{p}^{j}\cdot E\left(x,\zeta_{p}^{j}\cdot y\right).

(62)

Indeed, the form
T
in (
62
) is skew-hermitian because for any
i\in\left\{0,\dotsc,p-1\right\}
, one has

T(\zeta_{p}^{i}\cdot x,y)=\zeta_{p}^{j}\cdot p^{-1}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\zeta_{p}^{j-i}\cdot E(x,\zeta_{p}^{j-i}\cdot y)=\zeta_{p}^{i}\cdot T(x,y),

and we have
T(y,x)=-p^{-1}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\zeta_{p}^{j}\cdot E(x,\zeta_{p}^{-j}\cdot y)=-\sigma(T(x,y))
. Moreover,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}(T(x,y))=\frac{1}{p}\cdot{\mathrm{Tr}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\zeta_{p}^{j}\cdot E\left(x,\zeta_{p}^{j}\cdot y\right)\right)=E(x,y).

Consider again a finite free
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module
\Lambda
, and define
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
and
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
. Let
E\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
be an alternating form such that
E(a\cdot x,y)=E(x,\sigma(a)\cdot y)
for
x,y\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
a\in K
. Let
E_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be the
\mathbb{C}
-linear extension of
E
. By viewing
\Lambda
as a finite free
\mathbb{Z}
-module, the finite dimensional complex vector space
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}=(\Lambda_{\mathbb{R}})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
has a natural anti-holomorphic involution
z\mapstochar\rightarrow\bar{z}
, and the function

\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad\quad(x,y)\mapstochar\rightarrow E_{\mathbb{C}}(x,\bar{y})

defines a skew-hermitian form on
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
. We view
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
as an
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module by putting
a\cdot(x\otimes\lambda)=(ax)\otimes\lambda
for
x\in\Lambda
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
. At the same time,
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space in the usual way. Define

\Lambda_{\mathbb{C},\phi}\coloneqq\left\{x\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\mid a\cdot x=\phi(a)x\;\;\forall a\in\mathcal{O}_{K}\right\}.

For
x,y\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{C},\phi}
, we define
E_{\mathbb{C}}^{\phi}(x,\bar{y})=E_{\mathbb{C}}(x,\bar{y})
; this defines a skew-hermitian form
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C},\phi}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{C},\phi}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. Note that we have a canonical decomposition

\displaystyle\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}=\bigoplus_{\phi\in\textnormal{Hom}(K,\mathbb{C})}\left(\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}\right)_{\phi}.

(63)

Define
p_{\phi}
as the projection
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\mathbb{C},\phi}
induced by (
63
), and let
\pi_{\phi}
denote the canonical map
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\mathbb{C}
. The composition

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\mathbb{C},\phi}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\xlongrightarrow{\pi_{\phi}}\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K},\phi}\mathbb{C}

is an isomorphism of complex vector spaces.

Let
T\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow K
be the skew-hermitian form that corresponds to
E
via Lemma
11.1
. For any embedding
\phi\colon K\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, one obtains a skew-hermitian form

T^{\phi}\colon\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K},\phi}\mathbb{C}\times\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K},\phi}\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

by putting

T^{\phi}(x\otimes\lambda,y\otimes\mu)=\lambda\overline{\mu}\cdot\phi\left(T(x,y)\right)
for
x,y\in\Lambda,\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{C}
and extending linearly.

Lemma 11.3
.

In the above notation, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(x,y)\mapstochar\rightarrow E_{\mathbb{C}}(x,\bar{y})}
\scriptstyle{(\pi_{\phi}\times\pi_{\phi})_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{(p_{\phi}\times p_{\phi})_{\phi}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{\phi}\left(\Lambda_{\mathbb{C},\phi}\times\Lambda_{\mathbb{C},\phi}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(x_{\phi},y_{\phi})_{\phi}\mapstochar\rightarrow\sum_{\phi}E_{\mathbb{C}}^{\phi}(x_{\phi},\bar{y}_{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{\phi}\left(\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K},\phi}\mathbb{C}\right)\times\left(\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K},\phi}\mathbb{C}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(x_{\phi},y_{\phi})_{\phi}\mapstochar\rightarrow\sum_{\phi}T^{\phi}(x_{\phi},y_{\phi})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C},}

where the direct sums range over the elements
\phi\in\textnormal{Hom}(K,\mathbb{C})
.
In particular,

E_{\mathbb{C}}(x,\bar{y})=\sum_{\phi\in\textnormal{Hom}(K,\mathbb{C})}T^{\phi}(\pi_{\phi}(x),\pi_{\phi}(y))

for every
x,y\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
, where
\pi_{\phi}
denotes the canonical map
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

This is straightforward and we leave the proof to the reader.
∎

11.2

Moduli of abelian varieties with CM endomorphisms

Notation 11.4
.

In the rest of Section
11
, we work with the following data.

1.

Let
K
be a CM field over
\mathbb{Q}
, and define
\sigma\colon K\rightarrow K
as the involution that corresponds to complex conjugation on
\mathbb{C}
.

2.

Let
\eta\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
be a non-zero element such that
\sigma(\eta)=-\eta
.

3.

Let
\Lambda
be a free
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module of rank
n+1
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
equipped with a non-degenerate hermitian form
h\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\eta\cdot{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}\subset K
.

4.

Let
\Phi\subset\textnormal{Hom}(K,\mathbb{C})
be the CM type such that
\Im\left(\phi(\eta)\right)>0
for all
\phi\in\Phi
.

These data define a skew-hermitian form

T\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}
by putting
T\coloneqq\eta^{-1}\cdot h.

The form
T
is in turn attached to an alternating form (see Lemma
11.1
):

E\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\; {\textnormal{ such that }}\;E(ax,y)=E(x,\sigma(a)y)\;{\textnormal{ for all }}\;a\in\mathcal{O}_{K},\;x,y\in\Lambda.

Define
V_{\phi}=\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\otimes_{K,\phi}\mathbb{C}=(\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})_{\phi}
, and let

h_{\phi}\colon V_{\phi}\times V_{\phi}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

be the hermitian form determined by the condition that
h_{\phi}(x,y)=\phi(h(x,y))
for
x,y\in\Lambda\subset V_{\phi}
. For
\phi\in\Phi
, let
(r_{\phi},s_{\phi})
be the signature of the hermitian form
h_{\phi}
.

Let
A
be a complex abelian variety with dual abelian variety
A^{\vee}
. Assume that
A
is equipped with a polarization
\lambda\colon A\rightarrow A^{\vee}
and a ring homomorphism
\iota\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{K}\rightarrow\text{End}(A)
satisfying the following conditions (compare
[
KR14
, §2.1]
):

Conditions 11.5
.

1.

For each
a\in K
, we have
\iota(a)^{\dagger}=i(\sigma(a))
, where
\dagger\colon\textnormal{End}(A)_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\textnormal{End}(A)_{\mathbb{Q}}
denotes the Rosati involution.

2.

We have

\textnormal{char}(t,\iota(a)|\textnormal{Lie}(A))=\prod_{\phi\in\Phi}(t-a^{\phi})^{r_{\phi}}\cdot(t-a^{\phi\sigma})^{s_{\phi}}\;\in\;\mathbb{C}[t].

Here,
\textnormal{char}(t,\iota(a)|\textnormal{Lie}(A))\in\mathbb{C}[t]
denotes the characteristic polynomial of
\iota(a)
. Observe that
\dim(A)=g(n+1)
, where
g=[K\colon\mathbb{Q}]
. Let

\displaystyle E_{A}\colon{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\times{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

(64)

be the alternating form corresponding to the polarization
\lambda\colon A\rightarrow A^{\vee}
. Condition
11.5
.
2
implies that
E_{A}(\iota(a)x,y)=E_{A}(x,\iota(\sigma(a))y)
for
x,y\in{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Q})
.
Let

\displaystyle T_{A}\colon{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\times{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}

(65)

denote the skew-hermitian form attached to
E_{A}
via Lemma
11.1
, and define a hermitian form

\displaystyle h_{A}\colon{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\times{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\eta\cdot{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}\subset K,\quad\quad h_{A}\coloneqq\eta\cdot T_{A}.

(66)

Definition 11.6
.

Consider the above notation.

1.

A
polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian variety
is a triple
(A,\lambda,\iota)
where
(A,\lambda)
is a polarized abelian variety and
\iota\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\rightarrow\textnormal{End}(A)
a ring homomorphism such that Condition
11.5
.
1
is satisfied. A
\Lambda
-marking
of a polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian variety
(A,\lambda,\iota)
is an isomorphism
j\colon{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda
of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-modules which is compatible with the alternating forms
E_{A}
and
E
(equivalently, with the hermitian forms
h_{A}
and
h
). We say that a polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian variety
(A,\lambda,\iota)
is
of signature
(r_{\phi},s_{\phi})_{\phi\in\Phi}
if Condition
11.5
.
2
is satisfied. Note that any
\Lambda
-marked polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian variety is of signature
(r_{\phi},s_{\phi})_{\phi\in\Phi}
.

2.

Let
\widetilde{\textnormal{Sh}}_{K}(\Lambda)
be the set of isomorphism classes of
\Lambda
-marked polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian varieties
(A,\lambda,\iota,j)
(an
isomorphism

(A_{1},\lambda_{1},\iota_{1},j_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}(A_{2},\lambda_{2},\iota_{2},j_{2})
between such objects is an isomorphism
A_{1}\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{2}
compatible with the polarizations
\lambda_{i}
, ring homomorphisms
\iota_{i}
, and isometries
j_{i}
).

3.

Let
{\mathbb{D}}(V_{\phi})
be the space of negative
s_{\phi}
-planes in the hermitian space
(V_{\phi},h_{\phi})
.

The following proposition is due to Shimura, see
[
Shi63
, Theorems 1 & 2]
or
[
Shi64
, §1]
. We give a different proof, since our method will imply Proposition
11.12
below, whereas we did not know how to deduce Proposition
11.12
from the proofs of
[
Shi63
, Theorems 1 & 2]
. Remark that Shimura assumes
\Lambda
to be an
R
-module, for an order
R\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}
which is not necessarily maximal; our proof carries over, but we do not need this more general result.

Proposition 11.7
.

There is a canonical bijection
\widetilde{\textnormal{Sh}}_{M}(\Lambda)\cong\prod_{\phi\in\Phi}{\mathbb{D}}(V_{\phi})
.

Proof.

Let
(A,\lambda,\iota,j)
be a representative of an isomorphism class that defines a point in
\widetilde{\textnormal{Sh}}_{K}(\Lambda)
. Consider the Hodge decomposition
{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{C})={\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}\oplus{\mathrm{H}}^{0,-1}
. For each
\phi\in\Phi
, we have
\overline{{\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}_{\phi\sigma}}={\mathrm{H}}^{0,-1}_{\phi}
, hence there is a decomposition

{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{C})_{\phi}={\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}_{\phi}\oplus{\mathrm{H}}^{0,-1}_{\phi}\quad\text{ with }\quad\dim{\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}_{\phi}=r_{\phi}\;\text{ and }\;\dim{\mathrm{H}}^{0,-1}_{\phi}=s_{\phi}.

(67)

For an embedding
\phi\colon K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, let
h_{A,\phi}\colon{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{C})_{\phi}\times{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{C})_{\phi}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be the hermitian form induced by the hermitian form (
66
). By Lemma
11.3
, we have

\displaystyle\phi(\eta)\cdot E_{A,\mathbb{C}}(x,\bar{y})={h}_{A,\phi}(x,y)\quad\quad\forall x,y\in{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{C})_{\phi},\;\;\phi\in\textnormal{Hom}(K,\mathbb{C}).

(68)

Since
\Im(\phi(\eta))>0
for every
\phi\in\Phi
, the decomposition of
{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{C})_{\phi}
in (
67
) is a decomposition into a positive definite
r_{\phi}
-dimensional subspace and a negative definite
s_{\phi}
-dimensional subspace. The isometry
j\colon{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Q})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
induces an isometry
j_{\phi}\colon{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{C})_{\phi}\xrightarrow{\sim}V_{\phi}
for each
\phi\in\Phi
, and so we obtain a negative
s_{\phi}
-plane

j_{\phi}({\mathrm{H}}^{0,-1}_{\phi})\subset V_{\phi}

for each
\phi\in\Phi
. Reversing the argument shows that given a negative
s_{\phi}
-plane
X_{\phi}\subset V_{\phi}
for each
\phi\in\Phi
, there is a canonical polarized abelian variety
(A,\lambda)
with
A={\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}/\Lambda
, acted upon by
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, and inducing the planes
X_{\phi}\subset V_{\phi}
.
∎

Definition 11.8
.

Let
\textnormal{Sh}_{K}(\Lambda)
be the set of isomorphism classes of polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian varieties
(A,\lambda,\iota)
of signature
(r_{\phi},s_{\phi})_{\phi\in\Phi}
that admit a
\Lambda
-marking.

Corollary 11.9
.

There is a canonical bijection

\textnormal{Sh}_{K}(\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\setminus\prod_{i=1}^{g}{\mathbb{D}}(V_{i}).

Proof.

The bijection in Proposition
11.7
is
\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)
-equivariant.
∎

Remark 11.10
.

The above corollary generalizes
[
KR14
, Proposition 3.1]
, where the result is proven in the case where
K
is an imaginary quadratic number field.

11.3

Moduli of abelian varieties and the hyperplane arrangement

Consider Notation
11.4
.
Consider CM type
\Phi\subset\textnormal{Hom}(K,\mathbb{C})
, and choose an ordering
\Phi=\left\{\phi_{1},\dotsc,\phi_{g}\right\}
for the set
\Phi
.
This yields an embedding

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{g},\quad\quad\Phi(a)=(\phi_{1}(a),\dotsc,\phi_{g}(a)),

(69)

such that
\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{C}^{g}
. In particular, we obtain a complex torus
\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})
equipped with a natural ring homomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{K}\rightarrow\textnormal{End}(\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K}))
.
The map

\displaystyle Q\colon K\times K\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q},\quad Q(x,y)=\text{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\left(\eta^{-1}\cdot x\bar{y}\right)\quad\quad(\bar{y}=\sigma(y))

(70)

is a non-degenerate alternating
\mathbb{Q}
-bilinear form with
Q(ax,y)={Q}(x,\sigma(a)y)
for
a,x,y\in K
.

Lemma 11.11
.

Consider the element
\eta\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
, see Notation
11.4
.
2
. If
\eta^{-1}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}
, then
Q(\mathcal{O}_{K},\mathcal{O}_{K})\subset\mathbb{Z}
, and the alternating form
Q\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\times\mathcal{O}_{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
defines a polarization on the complex torus
\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})
such that Condition
11.5
.
1
is satisfied.

Proof.

We have
Q(\mathcal{O}_{K},\mathcal{O}_{K})\subset\mathbb{Z}
because
\eta^{-1}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}
. Thus, by
[
Mil20
, Example 2.9 & Footnote 16]
,
Q
defines a polarization on
\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})
. The result follows.
∎

Consider the signature
(r_{\phi},s_{\phi})
of the hermitian form
h_{\phi}\colon V_{\phi}\times V_{\phi}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, for
\phi\in\Phi
.
Let
\tau\in\Phi
, and assume that

\displaystyle\begin{split}(r_{\phi},s_{\phi})\quad=\quad\begin{cases}(n,1)\quad&{\textnormal{ if }}\phi=\tau,\\
(n+1,0)\quad&{\textnormal{ if }}\phi\neq\tau.\end{cases}\end{split}

(71)

The space
V\coloneqq\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K},\tau}\mathbb{C}

is equipped with the hermitian form
h_{\tau}\colon V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and to ease notation, we put
h\coloneqq h_{\tau}
. Define
{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
as the space of lines in
V
which are negative for
h
; in other words, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}H^{n}=\left\{{\textnormal{lines }}\ell\subset V\mid h(x,x)<0{\textnormal{ for }}0\neq x\in\ell\right\}.

For
r\in\Lambda
such that
h(r,r)=1
, let
r_{\tau}\in V
be the image of
r
under the canonical embedding
\Lambda\hookrightarrow\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K},\tau}\mathbb{C}=V
, and put

H_{r}\coloneqq\left\{\ell\in{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}\mid h(r_{\tau},x)=0{\textnormal{ for }}0\neq x\in\ell\right\},\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathscr{H}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{h(r,r)=1}H_{r}\subset{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}.

For polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian varieties
(A_{1},\lambda_{1},\iota_{1})
and
(A_{2},\lambda_{2},\iota_{2})
, we say that a morphism of polarized abelian varieties
f\colon(A_{1},\lambda_{1})\rightarrow(A_{2},\lambda_{2})
is
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear
if
f
is compatible with the ring homomorphisms
\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
.

Proposition 11.12
.

Consider the above notation. Assume the following holds:

1.

Condition (
71
) holds, i.e.,
for
\phi\in\Phi
, we have
(r_{\phi},s_{\phi})=(n,1)
if
\phi=\tau
and
(n+1,0)
otherwise.

2.

We have
\eta^{-1}\in{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}
. In particular,
\left(\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K}),Q\right)
is a polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian variety, see Lemma
11.11
.

Then, under the bijection
\widetilde{\textnormal{Sh}}_{K}(\Lambda)\cong\mathbb{C}H^{n}
of Proposition
11.7
, the subset
{\mathscr{H}}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
is identified with the set of isomorphism classes of
\Lambda
-marked polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian varieties
(A,\lambda,\iota,j)
for which there exist an
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear homomorphism

f\colon\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow A

of polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian varieties.

Proof.

Let
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
be the subset parametrizing isomorphism classes of
\Lambda
-marked polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian varieties
(A,\lambda,\iota,j)
admitting an
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear homomorphism
f\colon\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})\rightarrow A
of polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian varieties, cf. Proposition
11.7
. We must show that
{\mathscr{H}}={\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}
.

We first show that
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathscr{H}}
. To this end, let
[(A,\lambda,\iota,j)]\in\widetilde{\textnormal{Sh}}_{K}(\Lambda)
and
x\in V
, and assume that
h(x,x)<0
and that the point
[x]\in{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
attached to
x
lies in
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
. Thus, there exists an
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear homomorphism
f\colon\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})\rightarrow A
of polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian varieties. We must show that
[x]\in{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
, i.e., that there exists
r\in\Lambda
such that
h(r,r)=1
and
h(r_{\tau},x)=0
, where
r_{\tau}\in V
is the image of
r
under the canonical embedding
\Lambda\hookrightarrow V
.

To prove this, we may assume that
A={\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}/\Lambda
with
\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}={\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}\oplus{\mathrm{H}}^{0,-1}
, and that
T_{A}=T
and
E_{A}=E
. The morphism
f
induces an
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear map

\mathcal{O}_{K}\xlongrightarrow{\sim}\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})\xlongrightarrow{f_{\ast}}{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})=\Lambda

which, for simplicity, we also denote by
f\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\rightarrow\Lambda
. Define

\displaystyle r\coloneqq f(1)\in\Lambda.

(72)

Since
f\colon\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})\rightarrow A
is a morphism of polarized abelian varieties, the map
f\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\rightarrow\Lambda
satisfies
Q=f^{\ast}(E)
, i.e., we have
Q(x,y)=E(f(x),f(y))
for each
x,y\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Let
T_{Q}
be the skew-hermitian form on
\mathcal{O}_{K}
associated to
Q
via Lemma
11.1
. We claim that

\displaystyle T_{Q}(x,y)=T(f(x),f(y))\quad\quad\forall x,y\in\mathcal{O}_{K}.

(73)

To see this, note that
(x,y)\mapstochar\rightarrow T(f(x),f(y))
defines a skew-hermitian form on
K=\mathcal{O}_{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
, and that
{\mathrm{Tr}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}(T(f(x),f(y)))=E(f(x),f(y))=Q(x,y)

for
x,y\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Thus, (
73
) follows from Lemma
11.1
. Note that

\displaystyle T_{Q}(x,y)=\eta^{-1}\cdot x\bar{y}\quad\quad\forall x,y\in\mathcal{O}_{K},

(74)

see equation (
70
) and Lemma
11.1
. By (
72
), (
73
) and (
74
), we get

T(r,r)=T(f(1),f(1))=T_{Q}(1,1)=\eta^{-1},
so that

\displaystyle h(r,r)=\eta\cdot T(r,r)=1.

(75)

We claim that
h(r_{\tau},x)=0
, where the element
r_{\tau}\in V
is the image of
r\in\Lambda
under the natural inclusion
\Lambda\hookrightarrow V
.
To see this, write

M\coloneqq\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K}),
with Hodge decomposition
M_{\mathbb{C}}=M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}=M^{-1,0}\oplus M^{0,-1}
. Define
h_{Q}\coloneqq\eta T_{Q}
, so that
h_{Q}(x,y)=x\bar{y}
for
x,y\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
For
\phi\in\textnormal{Hom}(K,\mathbb{C})
, let
h_{Q,\phi}
denote the hermitian form on
(K\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})_{\phi}\cong\mathbb{C}
induced by
h_{Q}
and the embedding
\phi
, and note that
h_{Q,\phi}(x,y)=x\bar{y}
.
By Lemma
11.3
, we have
\phi(\eta)\cdot Q_{\mathbb{C}}(x,\bar{y})=h_{Q,\phi}(x,y)
for all
x,y\in(K\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})_{\phi}.

Consider the isomorphism
\Phi\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\sim}M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
induced by the isomorphism
\Phi\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\xrightarrow{\sim}M
.
As the hermitian form
h_{Q,\phi}(\Phi(x),\Phi(y))=x\bar{y}
is positive definite on
(M_{\mathbb{C}})_{\phi}
for each
\phi\in\textnormal{Hom}(K,\mathbb{C})
, as
\Im(\phi(\eta))>0
for every
\phi\in\Phi
, and as the hermitian
iQ_{\mathbb{C}}(\Phi(x),\overline{\Phi(y)})
is positive definite on
M^{-1,0}
, we have:

M^{-1,0}=M^{-1,0}_{\phi}=(M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C})_{\phi}\quad\quad\forall\phi\in\Phi.

(76)

Let
r_{\tau}^{-1,0}\in{\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}_{\tau}
and
r_{\tau}^{0,-1}\in{\mathrm{H}}^{0,-1}_{\tau}
be the components of
r_{\tau}
in the Hodge decomposition, so that
r_{\tau}=r^{-1,0}_{\tau}+r^{0,-1}_{\tau}
. As the projection of
\Phi(1)\in M
onto
M^{0,-1}_{\tau}
is zero (since
M^{0,-1}_{\tau}=0
by equation (
76
)), we get that
r_{\tau}^{0,-1}
is zero as well. Moreover, in view of (
68
), the Hodge decomposition
{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{C})_{\tau}={\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}_{\tau}\oplus{\mathrm{H}}^{0,-1}_{\tau}
is orthogonal for the hermitian form
h
on
{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{C})_{\tau}=V
. Therefore, we have:

r_{\tau}=r^{-1,0}_{\tau}\in{\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}_{\tau}=({\mathrm{H}}^{0,-1}_{\tau})^{\perp}=\langle x\rangle^{\perp},\quad\quad\text{hence}\quad\quad h(r_{\tau},x)=0.

Combining this with (
75
), we see that
[x]\in H_{r}\subset{\mathscr{H}}
, hence
{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathscr{H}}
.

To show that
{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}
, let
x\in V
be non-zero such that
[x]\in H_{r}\subset{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
for some
r\in\Lambda
with
h(r,r)=1
, and consider the
\Lambda
-marked
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear polarized abelian variety
A={\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}/\Lambda
corresponding to
[x]\in{\mathbb{C}}H^{n}
. Define an
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear map
f\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\rightarrow\Lambda
by putting
f(1)=r
. Then
f
is a morphism of Hodge structures because its
\mathbb{C}
-linear extension preserves the eigenspace decompositions and
h(x,r)=0
.
We obtain an
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear homorphism
f\colon\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})\rightarrow A
. The fact that
h(r,r)=1
implies that
f
preserves the polarizations on both sides, so
[x]\in{\mathscr{H}}^{\prime}
and we win.
∎

12

Orthogonality of the hyperplane arrangement

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
2.5
. For an integer
n\geq 2
, let

{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)

be an admissible hermitian lattice of rank
n+1
. In particular,
K
denotes a CM field, and as usual, we let
F\subset K
denote its maximal totally real subfield,
\sigma\colon K\rightarrow K
the involution corresponding to complex conjugation on
\mathbb{C}
, and
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}
the different ideal. Let
\eta\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
such that
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}=(\eta)
and
\sigma(\eta)=-\eta
; such an element exists because
{\mathscr{L}}
is admissible (see Definition
2.2
).

Let
\Phi\subset\textnormal{Hom}(K,\mathbb{C})
be the unique CM type such that
\Im(\phi(\eta))>0
for all
\phi\in\Phi
. Then
\Lambda
is a free
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-module of rank
n+1
equipped with a hermitian form

h\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{K}

of signature
(n,1)
with respect to one
\tau\in\Phi
and of signature
(n+1,0)
with respect to all other
\phi\neq\tau\in\Phi
. These data define a skew-hermitian form

T\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}^{-1}
by putting
T\coloneqq\eta^{-1}\cdot h.

By Lemma
11.1
, the skew-hermitian form
T
is in turn attached to an alternating form

E\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
such that
E(ax,y)=E(x,\sigma(a)y)
for all
a\in\mathcal{O}_{K},x,y\in\Lambda
.
As in Section
2.2
, we consider the complex vector space

V=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K},\tau}\mathbb{C}=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F},\tau}\mathbb{C}

equipped with the hermitian form

h\colon V\times V\mathrel{\smash{\meno}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

induced by the hermitian form
h\colon\Lambda\times\Lambda\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{K}
and the embedding
\tau\colon K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, and the space
\mathbb{C}H^{n}
of lines in
V
which are negative for the hermitian form
h
on
V
. As for an element
r\in\Lambda
with
h(r,r)=1
,
we define
H_{r}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
as the space of negative lines
\ell\subset V
which are orthogonal to
r
with respect to the hermitian form
h
on
V
.

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

Let
r,t\in\Lambda
such that
h(r,r)=h(t,t)=1
. Suppose that

\displaystyle H_{r}\cap H_{t}\neq\emptyset\quad\text{ and }\quad H_{r}\neq H_{t}.

(77)

We must prove that
h(r,t)=0
. Let
g\coloneqq[F\colon\mathbb{Q}]
. Define
\Phi\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{g}
as in (
69
) and consider the complex torus
B\coloneqq\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})
. Since the different ideal
{\mathfrak{D}}_{K}
is the principal ideal
(\eta)\subset\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we have

\displaystyle\{x\in K\mid\textnormal{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\left(x\eta^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{K}\right)\subset\mathbb{Z}\}=\{x\in K\mid x\cdot\eta^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{K}\subset\eta^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{K}\}=\mathcal{O}_{K}.

Therefore, the polarization
\lambda_{B}
on
B
attached to the alternating form

Q\colon\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})\times\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},\quad\left(\Phi(x),\Phi(y)\right)\mapstochar\rightarrow\textnormal{Tr}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}\left(\eta^{-1}\cdot x\cdot y\right),

see Lemma
11.11
, is in fact a principal polarization on
B
.

Consider the moduli space
{\textnormal{Sh}}_{K}(\Lambda)
of Definition
11.8
, as well as the isomorphism

{\textnormal{Sh}}_{K}(\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{Aut}(\Lambda)\setminus\mathbb{C}H^{n}

of Corollary
11.9
.
Let
[x]\in H_{r}\cap H_{t}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, and let
[(A,\lambda,\iota)]
be the point in
{\mathrm{Sh}}_{K}(\Lambda)
corresponding to the image of
[x]
in
L\setminus\mathbb{C}H^{n}
, such that
(A,\lambda,\iota)
is a polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian variety with
{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})=\Lambda
(so that
A={\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}/\Lambda
with
\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}={\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}\oplus{\mathrm{H}}^{0,-1}
), and
E_{A}=E
and
T_{A}=T
, where
E_{A}
and
T_{A}
are the forms associated to the polarization
\lambda
of
A
as in (
64
) and (
65
).

By Proposition
11.12
, the norm one elements
r
and
t
of
\Lambda
induce
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear morphisms

f_{1}\colon B\rightarrow A
and
f_{2}\colon B\rightarrow A
of polarized
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear abelian varieties. Since the polarization of
B
is principal, the maps
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are embeddings.
Moreover, since
(B,\lambda_{B})
is a principally polarized abelian variety, there exist an abelian subvariety
C_{i}\subset A
for
i=1,2
such that

A\cong B\times C_{1}
and
A\cong B\times C_{2}
as polarized abelian varieties (cf.
[
BL04
, Corollary 5.3.13]
), where for
i=1,2
, the polarization on
B\times C_{i}
is the product polarization
\lambda_{B}\times\lambda_{C_{i}}
, with
\lambda_{C_{i}}=\lambda|_{C_{i}}
the pull-back of the polarization
\lambda
along the embedding
C_{i}\subset A
.

Note that
B
is a simple abelian variety because
\textnormal{End}(B)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}=\mathcal{O}_{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}=K
is a field. In particular
(B,\lambda_{B})
is non-decomposable as a principally polarized principally polarized abelian variety. By
[
Deb96
]
, the decomposition of
(A,\lambda)
into non-decomposable polarized abelian subvarieties is unique, in the strong sense that if
(A_{i},\lambda_{i})
,
i\in\{1,\dotsc,a\}
and
(B_{j},\mu_{j})
,
j\in\{1,\dotsc,b\}
are polarized abelian subvarieties such that the natural homomorphisms
\prod_{i}(A_{i},\lambda_{i})\rightarrow(A,\lambda)
and
\prod_{j}(B_{j},\lambda_{j})\rightarrow(A,\lambda)
are isomorphisms, then
a=b
and there exists a permutation
\sigma
on
\{1,\dotsc,a\}
such that
B_{j}
and
A_{\sigma(j)}
are
equal
as polarized abelian subvarieties of
(A,\lambda)
, for every
j\in\{1,\dotsc,a\}
. Thus, for the abelian subvarieties
B_{i}\coloneqq f_{i}(B)\subset A
, we have either that
B_{1}=B_{2}\subset A
, or that
B_{1}\cap B_{2}=\{0\}\subset A
. We claim that the first option is impossible. Indeed, if
B_{1}=B_{2}
as polarized abelian subvarieties of
A
, then if we let
f_{i}\colon\mathcal{O}_{K}\rightarrow\Lambda
denote the map induced by
f_{i}\colon B=\mathbb{C}^{g}/\Phi(\mathcal{O}_{K})\rightarrow{\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}/\Lambda=A
, we have
\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot r=f_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{K})=f_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})=\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot t\subset\Lambda
, hence
r=\lambda t
for some
\lambda\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\ast}
; but then
H_{r}=H_{t}\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
which contradicts (
77
). Therefore, we must have

A=B_{1}\times B_{2}\times C

as polarized abelian varieties, for some polarized abelian subvariety
C
of
A
. This implies that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}=\text{Lie}(A)=\textnormal{Lie}(B_{1})\oplus\textnormal{Lie}(B_{2})\oplus\textnormal{Lie}(C),

(78)

and this decomposition is orthogonal for the positive definite hermitian form
iE_{\mathbb{C}}(x,\bar{y})
on
{\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}
, where
E\colon{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\times{\mathrm{H}}_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is the alternating form corresponding to the polarization
\lambda
of
A
.

Let
r_{\tau}
(resp.
t_{\tau}
) be the image of
r
(resp.
t
) in
V=\left(\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}\right)_{\tau}=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K},\tau}\mathbb{C}
under the natural map
\Lambda\rightarrow V
. Observe that
r_{\tau}=r^{-1,0}_{\tau}\in{\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}_{\tau}
and
t_{\tau}=t^{-1,0}_{\tau}\in{\mathrm{H}}^{-1,0}_{\tau}
, see the proof of Proposition
11.12
. By Lemma
11.3
, see also equation (
68
), we have

\displaystyle h(r,t)=h(r_{\tau},t_{\tau})=\tau(\eta)\cdot T^{\tau}_{\mathbb{C}}(r_{\tau},t_{\tau})=\tau(\eta)\cdot E_{\mathbb{C}}(r_{\tau},\overline{t_{\tau}})=\tau(\eta)\cdot E_{\mathbb{C}}(r_{\tau}^{-1,0},\overline{t_{\tau}^{-1,0}}).

Since
r_{\tau}^{-1,0}\in\textnormal{Lie}(B_{1})
and
t_{\tau}^{-1,0}\in\textnormal{Lie}(B_{2})
, we have
iE_{\mathbb{C}}(r_{\tau}^{-1,0},\overline{t_{\tau}^{-1,0}})=0
by the orthogonality of (
78
). We conclude that
h(r,t)=0
, and the proof is finished.
∎

Example 12.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and
{\mathscr{L}}=(K,\Lambda)
an hermitian lattice of rank
n+1
, such that the associated hermitian form
h\colon V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
on the complex vector space
V=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
has signature
(n,1)
.
Assume that
K
is the CM field
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d})\subset\mathbb{C}
for some
d\in\mathbb{Z}
with
d<0
. By Example
2.12
,
{\mathscr{L}}
is admissible.

There is an alternative, more elementary proof of Theorem
2.5
in this case, see
[
ACT02a
, Lemma 7.29]
. Namely, let
r,t\in\Lambda\subset V
with
h(r,r)=h(t,t)=1
. Assume that
H_{r}\neq H_{t}
and that
H_{r}\cap H_{t}\neq\emptyset\subset\mathbb{C}H^{n}
. We claim that
h(r,t)=0
. To prove this, let
[x]\in H_{r}\cap H_{t}
, lift
[x]
to an element
x\in V
, and consider the decomposition
V=\langle x\rangle\oplus\langle x\rangle^{\perp}
. Since
r,t\in\langle x\rangle^{\perp}
and the signature of
(V,h)
is
(n,1)
, the elements
r
and
t
span a positive definite subspace
\langle r,t\rangle\subset V
. Therefore, the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}h(r,r)&h(r,t)\\
h(t,r)&h(t,t)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&h(r,t)\\
h(t,r)&1\end{pmatrix}

is positive definite, hence
\left|h(r,t)\right|^{2}<1
. Since
\left|h(r,t)\right|^{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have
h(r,t)=0
.
